# IO AND ARGUS.

...which served him as a staff, in his hand, he tended a flock of goats while in the meantime he played upon the flute. Argus, enchanted by the sound of this instrument, requested that Mercury come and sit by him, which he did; and after he had entertained and amused him for some time with his stories, he blended his voice with the sound of the flute, in order to thus bring him to sleep. Argus's eyes began to close, one after the other. Being on the point of falling asleep, he requested Mercury to tell him the origin of that flute, on which he played with such a pleasant tone. The God had scarcely finished his tale, when he saw that sleep had closed all of Argus's eyes. He touched them gently with his rod <note>Mercury’s caduceus, a herald’s staff with wings and entwined snakes</note>, to bring him into a yet deeper sleep; whereupon, drawing his sword, he cut off his head and threw it down from the rock where they sat.

Juno, to hold the memory of Argus in honor, took all his eyes and placed them in the tail of the Bird⁴, which is dedicated to her. Then she turned all her wrath toward the unfortunate <term>Io</term>. She delivered her into the power⁵ of a terrible Fury, who disturbed and pursued her without ceasing. When she had thus caused her to wander over the whole earth, the Nymph finally arrived at the banks of the Nile: she lay down by the river, and lifting her eyes to Heaven, she seemed by her sighs, her tears, and mournful lowing, to pray to <term>Jupiter</term> for an end to her misfortunes. He then, embracing <term>Juno</term>, pleaded with her most fervently that she would no longer plague the unfortunate <term>Io</term>; swearing to her by the Styx that he would never again give her reason for jealousy. Juno allowed herself to be persuaded, and <term>Io</term> regained her first form. She was afterwards worshipped in Egypt under the name of <term>Isis</term>.

### EXPLANATION OF THE FABLE.

Most Greek writers say that <term>Io</term> was the daughter of⁶ <term>Inachus</term>, the first King of Argos; that Jupiter, having abducted her, brought her to Crete, where she bore Epaphus, who afterwards ruled over Egypt and was the father of Libya: that she herself, having departed for that country, married <term>Osiris</term> there. This <term>Osiris</term> is believed to have been the same as Apis, son of Phoroneus, the second King of Argos, who, having handed over the kingdom to his brother Aegialeus, traveled to Egypt⁷, where he ruled, and after his death

### REMARKS.

<columns>2</columns>

4. THE BIRD, WHICH IS DEDICATED TO HER.] This is the Peacock.

5. BY A TERRIBLE FURY.] Others say that it was a gadfly <note>A stinging fly sent by Juno to torment the cow-form Io, driving her to madness</note>.

6. INACHUS.] Most chronologists place him as a contemporary of Moses; but Eusebius has shown that he lived many centuries earlier; and Father Petavius (a) building on Eusebius's calculation, places the beginning of his reign in the 2127th year from the creation of the World. His name was given to a River, previously called Amphilochus, because he had widened its bed to prevent the devastations it caused when rains made it overflow. Pausanias says that the dominion over this River was given to Juno, the principal deity of the Argives. It had its origin from Mount Artemisius in Arcadia; but since this source, called Lyrceus, gave little water, the River was never large, except when the rains caused it to swell. Ptolemy and Strabo speak of it in their Geography, and an old writer who has written about Rivers notes that this Inachus was formerly called Karmanor, according to some, and according to others Haliakmon; or the Argolic river Argus, as Pausanias, on

<column-break/>

the authority of Aeschylus, wishes to prove. Today it bears the name of *Planizza*, if Sophian and Nikolai may be believed. See here also the reason why it was said to be so devoid of water: Neptune and Juno being at one time in dispute over who held the sovereignty over Argos, Juno maintained that she was the closest to it, because the city had been dedicated to her. Neptune, to support his claim, argued that he had given her the waters that moistened the whole land, without which it would have remained barren. When they finally submitted their case to the judgment of Inachus, Phoroneus, Cephisus, and Asterion, and the verdict was favorable to Juno, Neptune, to avenge himself, took all the water from those four Rivers; such that without the rains, they ran the danger, especially in summer, of drying up entirely and losing their names. Afterward, to show that he could cause even more harm with his power, he placed it entirely under a violent flood; but Juno, through her prayers, achieved so much that he finally caused the waters to recede; whereupon those of Argos, at public expense, built a magnificent Temple in honor of Neptune.

# IO AND ARGUS.

...was placed among the rank of the Gods under the name of <term>Serapis</term> <note>A Graeco-Egyptian god who combined traits of Osiris and Apis</note>. See here, then, how one explains the circumstances of the Fable of <term>Io</term> and <term>Argus</term> on this basis. When <term>Jupiter Apis</term>, King of <term>Argos</term>, had fallen in love with Io, this ignited a fit of jealousy in his wife <term>Niobe</term>, also called <term>Juno</term>. She then placed Io under the guard of Argus, to whom the Poets attribute a hundred eyes because of his watchfulness. Apis had him killed by his confidant; and his Mistress, to escape Niobe's vengefulness, took to a ship whose prow had the shape of a Cow, and this gave cause to say that she had been transformed into a cow.

<term>Pausanias</term> ⁸ believes that this Princess is younger than the one of whom we now speak. He says that she was the daughter of Iasus, Son of Triopas, the seventh King of Argos; and this opinion agrees better with the chronology: For since <term>Danaus</term> and <term>Aegyptus</term>, great-grandsons of <term>Epaphus</term>, did not live ⁹ until around the year two thousand five hundred and nine after the creation of the world, Io must have lived a long time after <term>Inachus</term>, who reigned many centuries before <term>Moses</term>.

Finally, <term>Herodotus</term> ¹⁰ relates that the Phoenicians landed at Argos—a city which at that time surpassed all the cities of Greece ¹¹—and having set out their goods for sale, a multitude of women went down to the seashore, and among others, Io, daughter of Inachus: that while she was busy buying what most pleased her, the Phoenicians abducted this Princess and carried her to Egypt. In this way, he says, the Greeks relate this History; but the Phoenicians claim that Io, having fallen in love with the Captain of the ship and feeling herself to be pregnant, departed with them of her own accord to escape the wrath of her parents.

We shall not linger, as the account of this History is so diverse and confused, on demonstrating what foundation this Fable of Io has. It is, however, credible that it took its origin from a certain event which occurred in Greece. That Inachus was held to be the Father of Io is because, when he settled in Argos, he introduced the religion of <term>Isis</term> <note>The major Egyptian goddess often depicted with cow horns</note> into Greece; and the similarity between the two names has mingled her History with that of Io.

---

### REMARKS.

...as Saint <term>Augustine</term> says of him: (a),
> "In this time <term>Apis</term>, King of the Argives, crossed the sea to travel to Egypt, and having died there, he became the famous Serapis, the greatest of all Egyptian Gods." <note>original Latin: "In hoc tempore Apis rex Argivorum navibus transvectus in Aegyptum, cum ibi mortuus fuisset, factus est Serapis, omnium maximus Aegyptiorum deus."</note>

<term>Varro</term> tells us why, after his death, he was not called *Apis*, but *Serapis*. This happened, he says, because the word *Soros* in Greek signifies a coffin, and since they had honored the grave of Apis before they had established a Temple for him, he was called at that time *Sorofapis*, or *Sorapis*, and afterward *Serapis*, with the change of but one letter, as often happens.

Some modern Scholars (b) attempt in vain to refute what we have brought forward from Augustine and Varro:

<column-break/>

believing that Apis, King of Argos, did not settle in Egypt, and that there was never another Apis in that land than the Bull which bore that name. However, such matters would require a more extensive investigation than we have proposed for ourselves in these Remarks.

8. PAUSANIAS.] *In Corinthiacis* <note>In the "Description of Greece," Book 2: Corinth and Argolis</note>.

9. DID NOT LIVE UNTIL AROUND etc.] See Petavius's *Rationarium Temporum* <note>The Reckoning of Time</note> Part II. Book II. chapter V.

10. HERODOTUS.] Book I.

11. SURPASSED etc.] This is beyond all probability. Argos has not surpassed all other cities of Greece since the times of Inachus, its founder.

# PAN AND SYRINX.

No less honor was shown to him in Egypt, where he was worshipped under the form of a goat. Herodotus says that the inhabitants of the province of Mendes placed Pan in the rank of the eight primary deities: that they depicted him with a goat’s head and goat’s feet, although they otherwise judged him to be of the same nature as the other Gods; and that in the City of Mendes—whose name in the language of that land also signifies a goat—they kept a sacred goat, which was a symbol of Pan, and for whose death the entire land mourned, just as was done in Memphis after the death of Apis <note>The sacred bull of Memphis, regarded as an incarnation of the god Ptah or Osiris</note>.

Diodorus the Sicilian says that the Egyptian Priests dedicated themselves primarily to Pan, and that they placed the image of this God, in the form of a goat, in their temples; by this, they intended nothing else than to thank the Gods for the fertility of Nature, and for that of their land in particular. The Lupercalian Feast <gloss>6</gloss> was also established in his honor.

Porphyry, as cited by Eusebius, says that Pan was one of the Spirits who served Bacchus; that he sometimes appeared to the country folk and struck them with such a terrible fear that many died from it: from which the expression "Panic terror" <note>original: *terror Panicus*</note> comes. Plutarch believes that it <gloss>7</gloss> has another origin: saying that the Pans and the Satyrs, having heard of the death of Osiris (who was killed by his brother Typhon) and having spread the rumor thereof, made such a terrible screaming and howling that all the inhabitants were terrified to the highest degree.

---

### REMARKS.

<columns>2</columns>

6. THE LUPERCALIAN FEASTS.] Pausanias says that under the reign of Pandion, the second King of Athens, the games and contests which were called *Luperkalia Lycæa* <note>Lycian Lupercalia</note> were established in Arcadia by King Lycaon, who ruled over that land, near a Temple of Pan, although they were dedicated to Jupiter the Lycean. When Evander had come from Arcadia to Italy, he introduced the custom there of celebrating the Lupercalian Feasts in honor of Pan; and Dionysius of Halicarnassus, to whom we owe this account, provides a description of it as a Feast that was still in fashion in his time. The testimony of both these writers—one of whom says that Lycaon dedicated the Lupercalian Feasts to Jupiter the Lycean, and the other who claims they were established in honor of Pan—gives reason to believe that these two deities were con-

<column-break/>

fused with one another; this is confirmed by another passage from Dionysius of Halicarnassus, where he says that Pan is the greatest and oldest God of Arcadia; and by Ovid, who in his *Fasti* <note>A poetic treatise on the Roman religious calendar</note> testifies that the Priest of Pan, as well as that of Jupiter, was called *Flamen Dialis* <note>A high priest of the Roman religion, usually specifically dedicated to Jupiter</note>.

7. HAS ANOTHER ORIGIN.] Although both accounts are equally fabulous, they may nevertheless have given occasion for this expression: it is enough that it was believed that Pan struck people with that kind of fear for which the reason was unknown, which is why it has been called *Terror Panicus*, a "terror of Pan." Bochart, and those who follow him, derive the origin of the word from the Hebrew word *Pan* or *Phan*, which signifies someone who is terrified, or seized by fright.

# S E M E L E

## JUPITER'S LIGHTNING CONSUMED.

> Her mortal body could not endure the heavenly tumult, and she burned with the bridal gifts. <note>original: "Corpus mortale tumultus Non tulit æthereos, donisque jugalibus arfit." Ovid. Met. 3.</note>

<image-desc>A decorative ornamental woodcut initial 'S' featuring floral motifs and foliage within a squared frame.</image-desc>

Semele <note>1</note> was loved by Jupiter. Juno, having noticed that she was pregnant, took the form of Beroë, the nurse of her rival. She went to the Princess's chambers and, after entertaining her with a long conversation, imperceptibly brought up the subject of Jupiter. "Alas," she said, "how I fear you are being deceived! Is it truly Jupiter who loves you? How often have mortals been seen taking the name of a God to deceive young flowers such as yourself? If it is true that the Master of the Gods loves you, then let him give you certain proof of it. Demand of him that he come to see you clothed in the same majesty with which he comes to Juno as a husband; that, to set your mind at rest, he assume the signs which distinguish him from other Gods." Cadmus's daughter allowed herself to be blinded by these plausible words. She then asked Jupiter for a favor, without explaining herself further. "Demand," he said to her, "whatever you please; it shall in no way be refused you. I swear it to you by the Styx, that God who is the overseer of all Gods and makes all Gods fear." Semele, unaware of how deadly this favor would be to her, rejoiced in the assurance that her request would be granted. She then addressed him in this manner: "When you come to visit me, show yourself in that same form and greatness with which you are clothed when embracing Juno." Jupiter wanted to stop her from speaking, but the time had passed and the word was out. It was not in his power to revoke Semele's request or the promise he had made to her. Overwhelmed with sadness, he ascended to Heaven. He gathered the clouds, the rain, the storm-light, the thunder, and the lightning; and, trying as much as he could to diminish the power of that terrible lightning, he took one of those to which the Cyclopes, when they forged them, had given less fire and power. He then descended, clothed in all the signs of his greatness, into the Princess's palace. It was impossible for a mortal to endure the intensity of the heavenly fire

---

### R E M A R K S .

1. SEMELE.] She was the daughter of Cadmus, King of Thebes, and Hermione. She had three sisters, namely *Autonoe* <note>The original Dutch text spells this name as 'Antonoe'</note>, *Ino*, and *Agave*, and a brother named *Polydorus*.

<meta>catchword: 2. THE</meta>

<summary>This page recounts the tragic myth of Semele, who, deceived by a disguised Juno, demands to see Jupiter in his true divine form and is consequently consumed by his celestial fire. It includes genealogical details about Semele's family, identifying her as the daughter of Cadmus.</summary>

could bear. Semele was consumed by the flames. The child, <note>2</note> which she was to bring into the world, would have perished in the same manner as she, had not Jupiter enclosed it in his thigh, where he kept it as long as its mother would have had to carry it in her body. His aunt Ino <note>3</note> served as his nurse; and the Nymphs of Nysa, to whom his education was later entrusted, <note>4</note> hid him in their caves.

->EXPLANATION OF THE FABLE.<-

This Fable cannot well be explained without making very bold conjectures. Semele perhaps had a secret love affair with someone, which later came to light; and to preserve her honor, it was spread about that Jupiter had been her lover. Some writers say that she was struck by lightning. But, according to the account of others, <note>5</note> Cadmus, being angered with her and having enclosed her with her son in a chest and thus set them out to sea, she arrived not far from Brasiae, on the coast of Lycaonia <note>The text specifies Lycaonia, though Brasiae is historically in Laconia; the author likely conflated the two</note>, where Semele was found dead and given a most magnificent funeral.
Diodorus the Sicilian <note>6</note> relates that Bacchus, on his expedition to India, was so tormented by the heat that he was forced to leave the plains and retreat to one of the mountains, which bore the name of *Meros* <note>7</note>, which signifies as much as the thigh <note>original: "dye"</note> of a man; and this gave the Greeks occasion to invent the fiction <note>original: "verdichten"</note> that he had been enclosed in Jupiter’s thigh.

->R E M A R K S.<-

2. THE CHILD.] This is Bacchus. Philostratus (a) relates that there were three princes of this name: namely, a Theban, an Indian, and an Assyrian. The Indian Bacchus was, according to the account of Diodorus the Sicilian, a son of Jupiter Ammon and Amalthea. He was given the name of *the Bearded One*, because he allowed his beard to grow in the Indian fashion. The Assyrian was considered the son of Ceres, because he was the very first to teach the Assyrians the art of agriculture. The Theban was the son of Jupiter and Semele. Cicero (b) names as many as five. The first, the son of Jupiter and Proserpina. The second, the son of the Nile; it is he who built the City of Nysa. The third, who had Caprius for his father, was King of Asia; he had the surname of *Sarbazius* <note>Likely a variation of Sabazius, a Phrygian deity</note>. The Feasts which were established in his honor were celebrated by night and bore the name of *Sarbazia*. The fourth was the son of Jupiter and the Moon. To him the Orphic games were dedicated. The fifth, finally, was the son of Thyone and Nisus.
Since Bacchus is not the subject of this Scene, we shall 
<column-break/>
not involve ourselves in a more extensive account of his history.
3. INO etc.] Apollodorus (c) relates that Juno, wishing to pour out her wrath upon Ino and her husband Athamas, brought it about that he, while hunting, killed his son Learchus, thinking he had a stag before him, and that Ino threw herself into the sea with her son Melicertus. That Jupiter then, to hide the young Bacchus from Juno's rage, changed him for a time into a goat; but that Mercury later brought him to the Nymphs who resided near Nysa, and that he was further raised by them.
4. NYSA.] There was in Arabia a city and a mountain of that name.
5. ACCORDING TO THE ACCOUNT OF OTHERS etc.] Pausanias in *Laconia* <note>original: "Laconicis", referring to Pausanias's 'Description of Greece'</note>. He adds that after Bacchus was saved from the sea, Ino, who was then still wandering about, was his nurse; and that in his time the cave was still shown where she had brought him up, which was called the cave of Bacchus.
6. DIODORUS.] Book II.
7. MEROS.] Thus speaks Quintus Curtius of it in Book VIII.

<meta>This page continues the mythological accounts and interpretations of Ovid's Metamorphoses, specifically focusing on the tragic transformation of Leucothoe and the nature of the god Apollo.</meta>

->L E U C O T H O E.<-

Clytie, who had been beloved by the Sun, had conceived a jealousy over the happiness of her rival. She revealed the love of his daughter to Orchamus. The King, transported by rage, did not delay in punishing her crime. The unfortunate Leucothoe lifted her hands to Heaven, and cried out that he <note>the Sun god, Helios/Apollo</note> had ravished her. This inhuman Father had her buried alive in the earth, and ordered a great heap of sand to be thrown upon her body. The Sun, through the power of his rays, dispersed it, to give his lover a free breath; but the time had passed, and she was already suffocated by the weight of the earth. He tried, through his warmth, to make the Princess's body living again. But seeing that Fate opposed his efforts, he gave himself over to grief, and having sprinkled Leucothoe's body and the earth which covered her with Nectar, he spoke: "I shall nevertheless at least ensure that you raise your head into the air." The body, made soft by this heavenly fluid, sprouted roots all around, and, piercing through the earth, it became an <term>incense tree<note>Original: "wierrookboom," referring to the frankincense tree (Boswellia sacra).</note></term>.

### E X P L A N A T I O N  O F  T H E  F A B L E.

The mythographers tell us nothing satisfactory regarding the fable of Leucothoe and that of Clytie, which is a continuation of it. They suggest, without however giving proof of it, that Orchamus was the first who had the incense tree planted in his Kingdom, and that it was called Leucothoe; and for this reason it was said that he was its Father, and that he had buried his Daughter alive. They add that it has been feigned how Apollo was in love with her because incense is of great use in the art of medicine, of which this God is held to be the inventor. If this interpretation is well-founded, one must say that the allusion was fetched from afar.

### R E M A R K S.

3. APOLLO.] I use the names of the Sun and *Apollo*, who are dealt with in this Fable, interchangeably, although several writers distinguish them. This difference of opinions comes from this: 1. Because the Sun has been worshipped under the names of many other deities; to such an extent that <term>Macrobius<note>A Roman writer (c. 400 AD) who argued in his "Saturnalia" that all pagan gods were ultimately symbols for the Sun.</note></term> (a) attempts to prove that most of the Gods of Heathendom are nothing other than the Sun. 2. Because there have been many Apollos. Cicero names four of them. The first, and the oldest, was the Son of Vulcan. The second the Son of Corybas. He was born on the island of Crete,

<margin>(a) *Saturnalia, book I, chapters 17—22.*</margin>

<column-break/>

whereof he disputed the sovereignty with Jupiter. The third was held for the Son of Jupiter and Latona. The fourth was originally from Arcadia, and is called *Nomius* by the Arcadians, because he had been a Lawgiver: for <term>*Nomos*<note>Original Greek: "νόμος"</note></term> signifies a *Law* in Greek. But, Cicero adds (b), although there have been many Apollos, one nevertheless speaks of no other than the Son of Jupiter and Latona, and all that the others performed has been brought into his history.

<margin>(b) *Cicero, On the Nature of the Gods, Book III, chapter 22.*</margin>

<summary>This page recounts the myth of Leucothoe, who was buried alive by her father and transformed into an incense tree by the Sun, followed by a rationalized historical explanation and a scholarly discussion on the various identities of Apollo.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the myth of Clytie and Leucothoe, focusing on the scientific and moral interpretations of their transformations.</meta>

# C L Y T I E.

<columns>
...of Clytie, and the disastrous end of Leucothoe, could find a complete explanation.

To provide a reason for her love toward Apollo <note>The sun god in Greek mythology, whom Clytie loved and watched constantly after he abandoned her.</note>, it is said that the sunflower constantly turns its blossom toward the sun. This sort of thing should not be examined too strictly; it is enough to say that the weight of this flower pressing it downward, it must naturally turn toward that side where the sun dries out and causes the fibers, from which its stem is composed, to shrink.

Finally, there are writers who, limiting themselves to the moral meaning, regard the fable of Leucothoe and Clytie as nothing other than a picture of the disorder and troubles produced by <term>jealousy<note>original: "minnenyd", literally "love-envy" or romantic jealousy.</note></term>: a passion no less deadly for those who surrender to it than for those who are its object.

### OBSERVATIONS.

...a sort of imitation which they attribute to the sunflower, making it imitate the frankincense tree <note>In the myth, Apollo's other lover, Leucothoe, was buried alive by her father and subsequently transformed by Apollo into a frankincense tree.</note> and attempt to produce incense. They say that if one splits the stem of these flowers, a gluey fluid indeed emerges which resembles melted gum, the scent of which approaches the fragrance of frankincense.

<column-break/>

But there is nothing falser or more trivial than this explanation: for there are few plants that do not exude a certain gluey sap when the stem is wounded; and the scent of the sunflower has not the slightest resemblance to that of frankincense.

# THE MOON
### AND
# ENDYMION.

->*Latmian Endymion brings no blush to you, O Moon.* <note>original: "Latmius Endymion non est tibi, Luna, rubori."</note><-
->Ovid, *The Art of Love*, Book 3.<-

Endymion, the shepherd <gloss>1</gloss> so renowned for his beauty, was the son of Ethlius, the first King of Elis, who was born of Jupiter and Protogenia. After Jupiter had admitted him into Heaven, Endymion attempted to dishonor Juno by force; but as punishment for his crime, he was doomed to an eternal sleep <gloss>2</gloss>. The Moon, making her journey around the earth, saw him on Mount <gloss>3</gloss> Latmus, where Jupiter had caused him to be carried. She could not look upon him without loving him; it is said that, although her love was nourished by no hope, she nevertheless descended to this mountain every night so that she might at least soothe her pain and torment through the pleasure she found in the embraces of the one who was the cause of it.

->EXPLANATION OF THE FABLE.<-

Endymion, the successor <gloss>4</gloss> of his father Ethlius, was driven from his kingdom because he had been defeated in the Olympic Games. He then withdrew to Caria on Mount Latmus, where he practiced astronomy <note>literally: "star-art"</note>, in which he progressed so successfully that he is considered <gloss>5</gloss> the first to have given a description of the course of the Moon; and from this, the fable of his amorous affairs with Diana <gloss>6</gloss> arose. His secluded...

->R E M A R K S.<-

<columns>2</columns>

1. THE SHEPHERD.] Others say that he was a hunter. Furthermore, those to whom it seems strange that a King's son, and Jupiter’s grandson, would be a shepherd, must have no knowledge of ancient customs.
2. AN ETERNAL SLEEP.] Or, according to the opinion of others, for no longer than thirty years.
3. MOUNT LATMUS.] This was a mountain in Caria, a region of Asia Minor. Strabo (a) says that even in his time, Endymion’s burial place was seen on this mountain.
4. SUCCESSOR OF HIS FATHER etc.] See Pausanias in *Eliaca*, and Strabo, Book XIV.
5. THE FIRST etc.] See Pliny, Book II, Chapter 9.
6. DIANA.] She was the daughter of Jupiter and Latona, and sister to Apollo. Although there were several Dianas, as we shall soon show, it is this one of whom the writings of the poets generally speak. She is given three names and duties, which differ greatly from one another. In Heaven, she is the Moon; on Earth, Diana, the Goddess of the Hunt; and in Hell, Proserpina. She

<column-break/>

is also depicted with three heads; the right head is that of a horse, the left that of a dog, and the middle one that of a wild boar; others say it is a human head. It is for this reason that she has been given the name of *Triformis*, or the Triple-formed Hecate.

> And triple-headed Hecate, the three faces of the virgin Diana. (b) <note>original: "Tergeminamque Hecaten, tria Virginis ora Dianae."</note>

Her names and duties have been successfully brought together in this couplet:

> Proserpina, Luna, and Diana terrify, illuminate, and hunt;
> The depths, the heights, and wild beasts; with scepter, radiance, and arrow.
> <note>original: "Terret, lustrat, agit, Proserpina, Luna, Diana, / Ima, suprema, feras, sceptro, fulgore, sagitta."</note>

She was also given oversight over pregnant women and those in labor, under the names of Lucina and Opis, because she had served as a midwife to her mother, Latona, when she gave birth to Apollo:

# L Y C A O N.

[...] having had [parts of the guest] boiled and others roasted, he had them served to Jupiter. When the God saw these ghastly dishes, the fire of his wrath, by his command, set the Tyrant’s court ablaze. Lycaon fled in terror, and finding himself in the middle of a field, he began to howl instead of cry out, and tried in vain to speak. Carried away by his resentment, he turned the murderous rage he had once used against men toward animals. His clothes transformed into a hairy hide, his arms into paws. In a word, he became a Wolf. One can still see in him, after this change, the traces of his former shape, such as the grey hair, a savage nature, and fiery eyes; and thus he remains forever a personification of cruelty.

### E X P L A N A T I O N  O F  T H E  F A B L E.

Lycaon <gloss>2</gloss> was a cruel Prince toward strangers, whom he often sacrificed to the Gods. Pausanias <gloss>3</gloss> teaches us that the entertainment spoken of here is nothing other than a sacrifice in which he offered one child upon the altar of Jupiter. It was he, as we have already <gloss>4</gloss> noted, who established the Lupercalian <gloss>5</gloss> Festivals <note>The Lupercalia was an ancient Roman festival, though the author here links its origins to Lycaon’s Greek rites in Arcadia.</note>, in which it was customary <gloss>6</gloss> to slaughter human sacrifices. The cruelty of this Prince, and his name, which in Greek means a Wolf <note>The Greek word "lykos" (λύκος) means wolf.</note>, gave occasion for the invention of this Transformation. Moreover, he was much beloved by his subjects, whose customs he civilized. He also taught them the art of building houses and forming societies. He taught them to feed on acorns instead of herbs, which they had eaten like beasts.

### R E M A R K S.

[...] to take [the opportunity]. To make his subjects submit to this, he pretended that Jupiter often came to lodge in his court in the guise of a stranger, to take close note of their behavior. At a certain time, while preparing a sacrifice to receive that God well, his children wanted to test whether he was indeed a God; for this reason, they mixed the flesh of a child, whom they had slaughtered, among the meat of the sacrifice; convincing themselves that no one but a God could sense the deception. But a terrible storm arose, and lightning consumed these impious wretches to ash. Lycaon, to appease Jupiter, established the Lupercalian Festivals.

2. LYCAON.] This is the second of that name; he was the

<column-break/>

son of Pelasgus. His daughter Callisto was loved by Jupiter and transformed into a bear by Juno. She was the mother of Arcas.

3. PAUSANIAS.] *In Arcadica.* <note>original: "In Arcadicis" — referring to the book on Arcadia in Pausanias's *Description of Greece*.</note>

4. NOTED.] In the VIth Remark on the Fable of Pan and Syrinx.

5. WHO ESTABLISHED THE LUPERCALIAN FESTIVALS.] See the Arundel marbles <note>The Arundel Marbles are a collection of ancient Greek sculptures and inscriptions brought to England in the 17th century.</note>, Epoch 18, and Pausanias *in Arcadica*.

6. HUMAN SACRIFICES, etc.] This barbaric custom was later abolished when they became satisfied with sacrifices of animals.

<meta>This page continues the mythological explanations of various transformations, specifically focusing on the story of Tithonus, who was granted immortality but not eternal youth by Aurora.</meta>

->TITHONUS AND AURORA.<-

### EXPLANATION OF THE FABLE.

This fable is also one of those about which one can only make conjectures. The most suitable explanation is to say that this Prince left Phrygia <note>An ancient kingdom in the west-central part of Anatolia, in what is now modern Turkey.</note> to settle in the East; for this is established on the testimony of <gloss>4</gloss> ancient writers. However, there is no agreement regarding the country where he stayed. Some maintain that it was in <gloss>5</gloss> Susa, a part of Persia, others in Egypt; but according to the most common opinion, he settled in Ethiopia. As for his Metamorphosis <note>A transformation or change of form, in this case from a man into an insect.</note>, it is believed that this Prince, having reached a very great age, discovered the art of preserving his strength through medicines; which led people to say that he, like the cicada <note>The original Dutch uses "krekel" (cricket), but in classical mythology, Tithonus is transformed into a cicada.</note>, was rejuvenated. Perhaps he was compared to this small creature only because he had the common fault of old people—namely, that he talked a great deal. Or finally, this fiction is perhaps built on nothing other than his love affairs with Aurora; for it is claimed that the cicada lives on the dew which falls before sunrise, and it is known that the Poets call the dew the tears of Aurora <note>Aurora is the Roman goddess of the dawn.</note>.

---

### REMARKS.

<columns>2</columns>

4. THE TESTIMONY OF ANCIENT WRITERS.] See, among others, Diodorus Siculus <note>A Greek historian from the 1st century BC.</note> <note>original: "Lib. IV"</note> Book IV.

5. IN SUSA.] It is believed that he was the founder of this city. By Aurora he had two sons: Memnon, of whom—

<column-break/>

—whom will be treated in the following scene, and Emathion, who gave his name to Emathia, a region in Macedonia.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut tailpiece featuring a woman's torso emerging from a floral cornucopia, flanked by symmetrical acanthus-leaf scrollwork and floral garlands.</image-desc>

<meta>catchword: XIX. THE</meta>

<summary>This page provides a rationalist explanation for the myth of Tithonus and Aurora, suggesting his "transformation" into a cicada was a metaphor for old age or his use of restorative medicines. # M E M N O N.

XX.

truly one soon appeared, as it began to flap its wings. An innumerable number of similar birds rose from the same ashes. This flight flew three times around the funeral pyre; three times they raised a funeral cry. The fourth time they divided themselves into two bands, and <margin>2</margin> gave battle to one another with their beaks and curved claws, with such fury that they fell down into the ashes from which they had emerged; thereby demonstrating that they owed their birth to a brave Hero. From him they also received the name of Memnonides <note>These "Memnonian birds" were said in Greek mythology to be the transformed companions or ashes of Memnon, returning annually to his tomb to fight and mourn.</note>. They returned every year to the same place, where, resuming their struggle, they <margin>3</margin> sacrificed themselves to the shades <note>In this context, "shades" refers to the spirits or ghosts of the deceased in the underworld.</note> of this Hero.

This event, however wondrous it may be, served less to make the name of Tithonus's son immortal than the Statue which was erected upon his grave. It was made of a black stone, brought from the Ethiopian mountains. When the first rays of the sun fell upon it, one would have said that it seemed to come alive, and it gave a sweet and pleasant sound; but when this Luminary <note>The Sun.</note> went down, it seemed to sigh, as if it were saddened by its absence.

### EXPLANATION OF THE FABLE.

After Memnon had been killed by Achilles, magnificent funeral services were held in his honor. While they were busy burning his body, a flight of migratory birds appeared above the pyre; and this was enough to spread the word that they were born from his ashes. This is the most probable thing that can be said about the legendary part of this History. However, it is different regarding the Statue of Memnon: for since many <margin>4</margin> trustworthy Authors have spoken of it, one cannot doubt that such a Statue, as we have described, existed. Father Kircher <margin>5</margin> attributes the sound which it gave to a certain internal mechanism, or spring, whose cords were stretched by the moisture of the night and, shrinking again due to the heat of the sun, snapped and gave a sound, like the string of a Violin when it bursts loose.

### R E M A R K S.

<columns>2</columns>

2. **Gave battle to one another.**] According to the testimony of *Kremutius*, cited by (a) *Pliny*, these birds went every five years to Ethiopia, near the Palace of Memnon, to fight one another.

3. **To the shades of this Hero.**] It is added that in the same place where he had perished, a fountain sprang up which annually, on the anniversary of his death, gave out blood.

4. **Trustworthy Authors.**] Concerning the history of Memnon, one may generally consult *Strabo*, who asserts that he saw the miracle of this Statue himself, Book XIII. XV. XVII. *Pliny* Book X. chapter 26. Book XXXVI. chapter 7. *Tacitus* Book II. *Annals*. *Pausanias* in *Phocica* and in *Attica*. *Suidas*. The two *Philostrates*. *Lucian* in *The Liar*. *Tzetzes* *Chiliad* VI. History 64. *Quintus Calaber*, Book II. *Hesiod* *Theogony*. *Diodorus Siculus*, Book IV. *Apollodorus*, Book III. etc. Furthermore,

<column-break/>

it is said that Cambyses had this Statue broken in half to discover its secret; but it always continued to give the same sound. For this reason *Juvenal* (b) said:

> Where the magic strings resound from the broken Memnon. <note>original Latin: "Dimidio magicæ resonant ubi Memnone chordæ." This refers to the torso of the Colossus of Memnon which continued to "sing" even after being damaged.</note>

Pliny (c) assures us that this Statue was in the Temple of Serapis, at Thebes in Egypt. Some place it on a mountain near Susa, in Persia. Others, finally, maintain that it was in Ethiopia; which can be reconciled with Pliny's account: for in ancient times, Ethiopia was often confused with Upper Egypt.

5. **Father Kircher.**] In *Oedipus*, Volume II. The manner in which *Pausanias* (d) speaks of the sound which this Statue gave confirms the explanation of this Father <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680) was a famous Jesuit scholar who attempted to explain ancient wonders through contemporary physics and mechanics.</note>.

# PYGMALION.

> And leaning over the couch, he gave her kisses: she seemed to grow warm. He moves his mouth to hers again, and also tries her breast with his hands: the ivory, having been tested, grows soft, and its stiffness laid aside, it gives way to his fingers and yields — it was a body, the veins throb under the pressure of his thumb.
<note>original: "Incumbensque toro dedit oscula: visa tepere est. Admovet os iterum, manibus quoque pectora tentat: Tentatum mollescit ebur, positoque rigore Subsedit digitis, ceditque — Corpus erat, saliunt tentatæ pollice venæ."</note>
-> OVID. Met. 10. <-

Pygmalion¹, having witnessed the indecency of the Propetides², developed such an aversion to the female sex—whom Nature had made subject to such shameful weaknesses—that he resolved to lead a bachelor's life. Meanwhile, he crafted an ivory statue which surpassed the most beautiful women in beauty, and with which he fell in love. This statue represented a young daughter. One would have said that it was alive, and that nothing but modesty prevented it from moving. So wonderful was the art within it. Pygmalion, transported by love for this image and deceived by his own work, touched it to see if it was flesh or ivory; and having laid his hands upon it, he still could not believe that it had no life. He began to kiss it, and imagined that it kissed him back; that during his embraces and caresses, the touched limbs yielded to his fingers: and he feared that by pressing too hard, he might bruise it. Then he stroked the image, then he brought it gifts which are so pleasing to young maidens: shells, little birds, flowers, and amber. He also adorned it with costly clothing, veiled it, placed precious gemstones on its fingers, hung a chain around its neck, and pearls from its ears. But however great a pleasure he took in seeing his statue decked out in these clothes and ornaments—all of which suited it so charmingly—he found it no less beautiful when it was naked. He called it his bed-companion, and laying it in bed, he took care that it lay softly, just as if it might have feeling for such things. In the meantime, when the Festival of Venus—a very great holy day on the island of Cyprus—had arrived, and white cows with gilded horns were being slaughtered everywhere as offerings, and incense was lit on every altar; Pygmalion, having offered his sacrifices to the Goddess, approached her all a-tremble with this prayer: "If it is true," he spoke, "that you, O Gods, can give all things, then I pray you, grant me a wife who is exactly like my ivory statue." ---

### R E M A R K S.

<columns>2</columns>
1. PYGMALION.] One must not confuse this sculptor with Pygmalion, King of Tyre and brother of Dido.
2. PROPETIDES.] These were women of the island of Cyprus who were turned into stone. Venus, to take revenge for their boldness in maintaining that she was not a goddess, kindled an unchaste flame of love in their hearts. Ovid says that they were the first women who

<column-break/>

prostituted their bodies <note>original: "haere ligchaemen ten beste gaven"</note>; and that as they had already lost all sense of honor, their transformation consisted only of a small change. These words show us what meaning we must give to this Fable. Because the Propetides had no sense of honor or shame, the poets said they were turned into rocks. With this, they also wished to teach us that man is ultimately hardened by the habit of sinning.

# PYGMALION.

> Leaning over the couch, he gave her kisses: she seemed to grow warm. Pygmalion¹, having witnessed the unchastity of the Propetides², developed such a distaste for the female sex—whom Nature had made subject to such shameful weaknesses—that he decided to lead a life of bachelorhood. Meanwhile, he created an ivory statue which surpassed the most beautiful women in its loveliness, and he fell in love with it. This statue represented a young maiden. One would have said that she was alive, and that nothing but modesty prevented her from moving. Such wonderful art was contained within it. Pygmalion, transported by love for this image and deceived by his own work, touched it to see if it was flesh or ivory; even after laying his hands upon it, he still could not believe that it had no life. He began to kiss it, and imagined that she kissed him back; he imagined that while embracing and caressing it, the limbs he touched yielded to his fingers: and he feared he might bruise it by pressing too hard. Now he stroked the image, then he brought it gifts that are so pleasing to young maidens: shells, little birds, flowers, and amber. He also dressed it in costly clothing, adorned it, put precious stones on its fingers, hung a chain around its neck, and pearls in its ears. But however much pleasure he took in seeing his image decorated with these clothes and ornaments—all of which made it look so charming—he found it no less beautiful when naked. He called it his bed-companion, and when laying it in bed, he took care that it lay softly, as if it could feel the comfort. Meanwhile, when the Feast of Venus—a very great holy day on the island of Cyprus—had arrived, and white cows with gilded horns were slaughtered as sacrifices everywhere, and incense was lit on all altars; Pygmalion, having offered his sacrifices to the Goddess, approached her, trembling, with this prayer: "If it is true," he spoke, "that you, O Gods, can grant everything, then I pray that you grant me a wife who is just like my ivory statue." # O E N E U S ,
### OR THE
# C A L Y D O N I A N   B O A R .

> They say the altars of the daughter of Leto <note>original: "Latoides"; refers to Diana</note> were left alone without incense, passed over and neglected.
> — Through the fields of Oeneus, the scorned goddess sent an avenging boar. —
> ->OVID. Metamorphoses 8.<-

Oeneus¹, King of Calydon², having received a rich harvest from the Gods, wished to show them his gratitude. He then offered the first fruits of the grain to Ceres, wine to Bacchus, and oil to Minerva. Only Diana’s altar was left without incense. The Goddess, enraged by this slight, sent a terrifying wild boar into the fields of Calydon. Its eyes were red and shot out sparks. Its head and back stood stiff with bristles, as if they were javelins. The boiling foam, which sprayed from its snout, covered its shoulders; its tusks were as large as those of an elephant, and the breath that came from its maw scorched the grass and flowers. Now this Boar trampled the newly sprouted grain, then destroyed the farmer's harvest in an instant. It spared neither vineyards nor olive trees. The shepherds, the dogs, and even the fiercest bulls were unable to protect the herds from its fury. Everyone fled, and people did not even consider themselves safe within the cities. While this Monster wrought destruction everywhere and inspired terror, Meleager decided to rid the land of it. He was accompanied by a band of youths, driven no less than he by ambition. Atalante³, the jewel of the city of Tegea and of the Arcadian forest, wished to share in the praise of such a dangerous undertaking. Her extraordinary beauty, and the martial appearance added by her hunting attire and equipment, made her everything that one could

### R E M A R K S .

---

<columns>2</columns>
1. OENEUS.] He was the son of Parthaon and Euryte. By Althaea, his first wife, he had, among other children, Meleager and Deianira; who was married to Hercules, and, in the opinion of others, was the daughter of Bacchus. After Althaea's death, he married Periboea, daughter of Hipponous, by whom he fathered Tydeus, the father of Diomedes, and Menalippus or Pisander, whom Tydeus accidentally killed. Those who hold Bacchus to be Deianira’s father relate that this God, staying at Oeneus's house at a certain time, fell in love with Althaea; and that her husband, noticing this, showed such compliance that he went to the countryside; out of gratitude for which Bacchus taught him the art of planting <term>wyngaerden aentequeeken</term> <note>Establishing and cultivating vineyards</note>.
2. CALYDON.] A city of Aetolia, which gave its name to the forest.

<column-break/>

3. ATALANTE.] She was the daughter of Iasius, King of Arcadia. One must not confuse her with another Atalante, who made a promise to marry whoever could outrun her, and was defeated by Hippomenes because she stopped to pick up the golden apples he dropped behind him. This latter one was the daughter of Schoeneus, King of Scyros. In some editions of Ovid, where he speaks of this hunt, one reads:

> Atalante came, the most beautiful maiden, daughter of Schoeneus. <note>original: "Venit Atalante Schænei pulcherrima virgo."</note>

However, these verses are not found in the best manuscripts, and are unanimously rejected by the most learned commentators.

# O E N E U S.

find graceful in men as well as in women, combined in her. Meleager ⁴, seeing her, fell in love with her. “O happy man!” he cried out, “whom you shall choose for your bridegroom.” He would have said more, had he had more time and not been ashamed to speak of love when it was time to go to battle. The forest, where these young warriors gathered, had never been cut and was densely overgrown; but the entrance was level ground and sloped imperceptibly until one came into a valley. The men, having arrived there, busied themselves—some with stretching nets, others with uncoupling the hounds from their packs; many followed the beast by its tracks; all wished to encounter it, regarding the danger to which they exposed themselves as nothing in the world. The rainwater, always sinking toward the center of the valley, made a basin surrounded by willow trees, light brushwood, rushes, and reeds. Here was the lair of the Boar. At the noise made by the hunting party, it sprang up, bursting out like lightning when it tears the clouds asunder, and knocking over all the trees that stood in its way. The hunters, seeing the animal, raised a great shout and lowered their boar-spears; but nothing could restrain it, and by striking with its tusks, it drove all the hounds that approached it into flight. Many of these young heroes, having thrown their darts <note>original: "schichten"; refers to light javelins or arrows</note> at this raging beast in vain, were already dangerously wounded when Atalanta, releasing her bow, struck it below its ear with an arrow. Meleager felt more joy over such a lucky shot than Atalanta herself; and showing his companions the blood flowing from the wound, he cried out that the honor of the entire hunt belonged to the Princess. Those who were with her encouraged one another with a great cry, ashamed that a woman surpassed them in bravery. But they shot their arrows so confusedly that one flew into another. Meleager's hand had to deal the death blow to the Boar. The first dart he threw was in vain; but the second wounded it in the back, and while it—doubling its rage and spitting blood mixed with foam—turned many times to tear the dart from the wound, Meleager thrust his javelin through the middle of its body.

### E X P L A N A T I O N   O F   T H E   F A B L E.

The hunt of the Calydonian Boar ⁵ is recounted by so many writers ⁶ that it cannot well be placed among the Fables <note>The author is suggesting the event has a historical basis rather than being purely mythological</note>. The revenge of Diana is merely an ornament which the poets have added to the history, and probably rests on the fact that Oeneus, not being a lover of the hunt but occupying himself only with agriculture, performed no sacrifices except to those Gods to whom he, in his opinion, owed the fertility of his land.

<meta>This page continues the tragic story of the Greek hero Meleager, whose life was bound by the Fates to a piece of wood. Following his killing of his uncles, his mother Althaea must choose between her son and her brothers.</meta>

# M E L E A G E R.

filled with rage, appearing cruel and fierce, like one who threatens to carry out a shameful deed. Tossed by such conflicting passions, she knows not what to do; but finally, sisterly affection gains the upper hand over maternal love. She then seizes the fateful log and casts it into the midst of the flames.

As the wood burns, Meleager, who was elsewhere, feels himself consumed by a fire whose cause is unknown to him; but his courage allows him to overcome his pains. Nevertheless, he grieved that he had to die such an ignoble death, envying the fate of <note>Ancaeus was a hero of the Argonauts who died during the hunt for the Calydonian Boar.</note> Ancaeus, who fell while fighting the Boar. While he calls out for his father, brothers, and sisters—by whom he was dearly loved—as well as his dear companion and perhaps also his mother, the fire redoubles its strength, and the pain increases. At last, he sinks into a deathly wasting away, and as the wood is consumed, he draws his final breath. Althaea, having become a sacrifice to the despair brought on by such a terrible crime, took a sword with which she pierced herself, sacrificing her own life to the shades of her son.

## EXPLANATION OF THE FABLE.

When Meleager had killed Toxeus and Plexippus, Althaea's brothers, this princess, in her first transport of rage, took her son's life by poison; or, according to others, she used the magical arts to do away with him. This is what the Poets have in mind when they speak of the log to which the <term>Fates</term> <note>original: "Schikgodinnen"</note> had bound Meleager's destiny, and which Althaea threw into the fire. Most of the ancients agree with Ovid in describing the history of this Prince. However, <note>Homer is the legendary author of the Iliad and the Odyssey.</note> Homer makes no mention of his death. He does say that Althaea dedicated her son to the gods of the underworld, but he adds that her anger finally cooled.

## REMARKS.

<columns>2</columns>
2. <term>HIS SISTERS.</term>] Ovid says that <note>The Roman goddess of the hunt, known as Artemis to the Greeks.</note> Diana changed them into birds after they had long lamented Meleager's death.
3. <term>HOMER.</term>] <note>Iliad, Book IX.</note> Since his account differs from that of Ovid (the summary of which has just been seen), it will provide much light to this history if we insert it here. I have for the most part followed <note>Jan Hendriksz Glazemaker (1619–1682) was a famous Dutch translator of classical texts.</note> Glazemaeker’s translation.
> "The <note>The Dutch text uses 'Kreters' (Cretans), but in the context of this myth, it refers to the Curetes, a neighboring people of the Aetolians.</note> Curetes fought against the warlike Aetolians near the city of Calydon, and they destroyed one another. For the Aetolians sought to protect the pleasant Calydon, and the Curetes sought to destroy it with steel. Diana... being angered because Oeneus had not offered the first fruits to her from the fertile ground, stirred up a great evil against them. He had offered sacrifices of a hundred beasts to the other Gods who consume food, but he had passed her by—though she is a daughter of the great Jupiter—either through forgetfulness or through insolence. This angered Goddess... stirred up a wild Boar with white tusks, which lay down in the grass and, devastating Oeneus's land, caused much damage. For this wild Boar felled many high trees, with their roots and fruits, down to the ground. Meleager, son of Oeneus, having gathered many hunters and dogs from various cities, killed this wild Boar, which could not be subdued by a few men, as it was very large and caused many men to perish miserably. The Goddess therefore stirred up, for the sake of the Boar's head and bristly skin, a great commotion and struggle between the Curetes and the high-minded Aetolians. As long as the warlike Meleager fought, the Curetes were defeated and, though their number was great, could not remain outside the walls. But when Meleager was overcome by rage, which also masters the minds of others, even if they are upright in their thinking

<column-break/>

> ... he went to lie with his beautiful Cleopatra, while the rage that tormented his spirit overwhelmed his heart; he was gravely angered by the curses of his Mother, who, weeping sadly for the slaying of her brothers, petitioned the Gods. While she struck the earth with her hands and fervently prayed to Pluto and the troublesome Proserpina—whom she embraced—(while tears rolled down her cheeks and bosom) that they should kill her Son. <note>The Erinyes or Furies are deities of vengeance.</note> Erinys, wandering in the darkness and having an unforgiving mind, heard her from the hellish Pool. Meanwhile, the besiegers made a great noise while they rammed and stormed the towers. The gray-haired men of the Aetolians meanwhile entreated Meleager, and sent the foremost Priests of the Gods to him, to request, under promise of great gifts, that he should come out and show them help. The old Knight Oeneus, climbing upon the threshold of the magnificent hall and knocking on the joined planks, poured out humble prayers for his Son. His venerable Mother herself [laying aside her anger] and sisters also prayed to him with great fervor. But he refused all the more. He was also greatly entreated by his companions, who were his friends and held in high esteem by him. While... the Curetes broke in and set the great city on fire, Meleager’s beautiful consort came weeping before her Husband and, pouring out her prayers with great lamentation, showed him the miseries that come upon men whose city is taken: namely, that some kill the men, others set the city on fire, and others carry away the children and beautiful women. Meleager, hearing of these cruel deeds, was moved in his spirit, and putting on his magnificent armor, hastened to the front. He then, abandoning his rage, delivered the Aetolians from the misery that was destined for them.

# HERCULES

## AND

## ACHELOUS.

---

> There remained the third form of a fierce bull; changed into a bull's limbs, I renewed the fight. He wrapped his arms around the muscles of my left side, and dragging me as I charged, he followed, fixing my pressed-down horns into the hard ground, and threw me down in the deep sand. Nor was this enough: while his fierce right hand held the stiff horn, he broke it, and tore it from my mangled forehead.
->OVID, Metamorphoses Book 9.<-

DEIANIRA, though sought after by the greatest princes of Greece, had not yet made her choice when Hercules and Achelous arrived at the same time at the Calydonian court to request her hand in marriage from her father, <note>Oeneus was the King of Calydon and father of Deianira and Meleager.</note> Oeneus. As soon as they had declared their intentions, all the other suitors withdrew, no longer wishing to contest for the princess. These two illustrious rivals spared no effort to bring forth everything that might serve their advantage and persuade the king to give them his daughter. This dispute between the rivals did not end peacefully: Achelous reproached Hercules, claiming he was born of adultery <note>Achelous is insulting Hercules' divine parentage, as he was the son of Jupiter and the mortal Alcmena, rather than a legitimate royal heir.</note>; at which this hero, not deigning to respond with insults, fell upon him with great fury. Achelous, stretching out his arms, awaited him in the posture of a wrestler ready for battle. They fought foot to foot, their arms entwined, and their heads pressed against each other. Three times Hercules tried in vain to wring himself from his enemy’s arms; on the fourth attempt, he succeeded and fell upon him with such force that he sent him tumbling. Taking advantage of this position, he threw himself on top of him, and holding him by the throat, made him bite the dust. Achelous, who could not match Hercules in strength, took recourse in his usual <term>trickery</term> <note>original: "kunstenaryen" - referring here to magical shape-shifting.</note>. He then transformed himself into a serpent and, escaping the hands of his rival, tried to terrify him with horrific hissing. Alcmena's son <note>A common epithet for Hercules, referring to his mother Alcmena.</note>, looking at him with contempt, addressed him in this manner: “You must know that overcoming snakes was already my work in my childhood.” Hardly had he finished this speech when he squeezed the

The terrifying Dragon <gloss>1</gloss>, which is the subject of this scene, lived in the marsh of Lerna <gloss>1</gloss>. She never emerged from her hiding place without causing terrible destruction. The fearful farmer dared not till his fields, and the boldest traveler trembled when passing the place where this monster stayed. It was called the Hydra <gloss>2</gloss>. It had the head of a serpent, the claws of a dragon, its back covered with scales, and seven heads which breathed out fire and sulfur. Hercules undertook to rid the earth of it. Armed only with his club and accompanied by Iolaüs <gloss>3</gloss>, he went to seek out the serpent in the middle of its marsh; which, seeing him approach, fell upon him with great fury and covered him with a cloud of smoke and flame. Hercules awaited it with his raised club and cut off one of its heads with the first blow. But immediately two heads appeared in its place; and the blood, which ran from the wound onto the ground, brought forth scorpions and serpents. He looked upon this wondrous event without fear; redoubling his blows, he did nothing but increase the monster's rage. As he cut off heads, others took their place, and that which should have caused its death served only to make its strength grow. The invincible son of Jupiter <note>Hercules</note> would have failed in this undertaking without the help of his companion. The only means to prevent the dragon's heads from multiplying was

1. **THE MARSH OF LERNA.**] This marsh was in the Peloponnese, in the region of Argos. But Hyginus <note>Gaius Julius Hyginus, a Latin mythographer</note> (a) says that it was a stream.
2. **HYDRA.**] She was the daughter of Typhon <note>original: "Tyſon"</note> and Echidna (b), and the sister of the Gorgon, Cerberus, and the dragon which guarded the Golden Fleece, of the Chimera, the Sphinx, and the Hesperian dragon. He is the same one who was made young again by Hebe (d) <note>the goddess of youth</note>.

# THE HYDRA.

to stem the blood, which ran from the wound. Iolaüs succeeded in this, by holding a burning piece of wood against it; and the Monster, receiving blow upon blow, finally fell down dead before the feet of its Conqueror.

## EXPLANATION OF THE FABLE.

Very different explanations have been given for this Fable.
1. The Marsh of Lerna was, in the opinion of some, packed with snakes, which multiplied despite the care taken to destroy them. Hercules, with the help of his friends, set fire to the reeds with which this pool was surrounded, and by that means exterminated the Serpents.
2. Others say that he merely dried up the Marsh, which previously used to submerge the land with entire streams of water.
3. Some believe it was a hideout for highwaymen <note>The original Dutch 'struikrooveren' refers to bandits who ambushed travelers from the bushes.</note>. Hercules killed many of them; but as they increased in number as quickly as they were destroyed, he forced them to retreat into their hiding place, where he wiped them out by setting fire to it.
4. Finally, some Writers are of the opinion that it was a fortress, over which someone ruled as Commander, who bore the name of Lernus, and had a garrison of fifty or a hundred heads <note>In this historical interpretation, the "heads" are understood as individual soldiers or captains.</note> under his command. For the Poets differ greatly regarding the number of the heads of this Pool-serpent. It has been given seven, then nine, then fifty, and even a hundred. While Hercules and Iolaüs held this place under siege, a neighboring Prince set out to relieve it; and this is the Crab *, which joined with the Hydra to protect it, and which Hercules trampled with his feet. The besieged defended themselves with so much bravery that the place could not be overcome without setting it on fire.

to triumph over the Hydra, sent a Sea-crab, which
<column-break/>

# HERCULES
## DEATH
### A N D
## D E I F I C A T I O N.

> Mother, whatever in me was mortal of yours, the heaped fire has taken away; the part from my father is given to heaven, while yours is given to the flames. <note>original Latin: "Mater, quicquid in nobis tui Mortale fuerat, ignis ingestus tulit; Paterna coelo pars data est, flammis tua."</note>
> ->SENECA *Hercules Oetaeus* Act 5.<-

<image-desc>Ornamental drop cap 'H' decorated with floral and leaf motifs.</image-desc>
**H**ERCULES, returning to his fatherland with Deianira ¹ whom he had taken in marriage, came to the banks of the River Evenus, which had swollen exceptionally high due to winter rains. Because of the swift current, he feared little for himself, but his dear wife was his only concern. Nessus ¹, who happened to be nearby and knew the shallow spots, offered to carry Hercules' wife across, while the hero himself would attempt to swim over. The Hero accepted his offer and placed Deianira in his hands; she, trembling and pale with fear, was terrified of both the <term>Centaur</term> <note>A mythological creature with the upper body of a man and the lower body of a horse.</note> and the river. He first threw across his club and curved bow, and, burdened only with his quiver and the lion's skin hanging about his shoulders, he threw himself into the water without seeking the least dangerous path available. Having reached the other bank, he heard Deianira calling him for help. At this, he turned back, while Nessus stood ready to flee with the prize <note>original: "pand," referring to Deianira herself as a person entrusted to his care.</note> entrusted to him. In vain he tried to stop him with threats; the Centaur took flight, yet his speed could not protect him from Hercules' vengeance: the Hero took an arrow, aimed it at him, and shot him through instantly. Nessus, having pulled the arrow from the wound, saw his blood flowing, mixed with the venom of the <term>Hydra</term> ² <note>A multi-headed water serpent Hercules killed as one of his Twelve Labors; its blood was a deadly poison.</note>. Even as he was about to give up the ghost, he thought of revenge. He took his blood-stained garment and gave it to Deianira as an infallible means to make herself beloved.

When, after a long time had passed, Hercules returned as a victor from Oechalia <note>A city in ancient Greece; Hercules conquered it after being slighted by its king.</note> and prepared himself to make the promised sacrifice to Jupiter, Deianira heard that her husband was in love with Iole. She surrendered herself to sorrow and, unable to see her rival triumphing, she was on

# THE DEATH OF HERCULES
<meta>catchword: And</meta>

contemplated means to bring her husband back to his duty. While she thus forged various undertakings in her mind, she thought of the robe of Nessus: she <gloss>3</gloss> sent it immediately to Hercules, who put it on before he began to offer his sacrifice. But hardly had he cast the first incense into the flames and poured the wine upon the altar, when the venom of the Hydra <note>original: "Poelslange," literally "pool-snake," referring to the Lernaean Hydra.</note>, becoming heated, spread itself through all his limbs. For a long time, he overcame the pain he felt through his courage; but finally, succumbing to the violence of the poison, he left the altar and made the groves of Mount Oeta echo with his cries. He attempted to tear the deadly robe from his body, but it clung so fast to his skin that he could not rip it away without the flesh hanging to it. His blood, ignited by the power of the venom, made the same sound in its circulation through the veins as when one plunges a glowing iron into water. The fire, which consumed his entrails, forced a seething sweat from his body, made his nerves crack, and the marrow in his bones melt. He took to running toward the mountains, like a bull that runs away with the javelin with which he has been struck. He redoubled his strength to tear the poisoned tunic from his body, but in vain effort, he is seen bellowing with rage and raising a terrifying howl, uprooting trees and ripping rocks from their foundations. Now and then his rage begins to subside: he lifts his hands to Heaven, calling upon his father for help. At last, unable to withstand the fire by which he is consumed any longer, he throws down several trees on Mount Oeta, erects a funeral pyre, covers it with the skin of a Nemean lion; and having commanded Philoctetes to set it on fire, he goes to lie upon it as if on a bed, resting his head upon his club and showing such a cheerful countenance as if he were at a feast. This Hero, after slaying so many monsters and tyrants, now still had to conquer fire and death. That which he had taken from his Mother had to perish; but that which he had from his Father was immortal. When the earthly parts of his body were consumed by the flames, Jupiter carried him away in a chariot with four horses, placing him in Heaven in the rank of the Gods.

### EXPLANATION OF THE FABLE.

Nessus, whom the Poets have transformed into a Centaur, was <gloss>4</gloss> a Knight <note>original: "Ridder," here meaning a horseman or man of rank on horseback.</note> who offered to take Deianira on the back of his horse and thus carry her across the river. Hercules, having noticed that he wanted to abduct his wife, shot him through with an arrow. The Centaur, who perhaps had heard it said that the arrows of Hercules were poisoned, judged that the poison would mix with the blood flowing from his wounds; wherefore, wishing to avenge himself, he gave his blood-stained robe to Deianira, assuring her that it had the power to kindle love; and imagining that as soon as she conceived a jealousy against her Consort, she would not fail to make use of it to bring him back to his duty.

AND DEIFICATION.

And truly, when Hercules had abducted Iole, after conquering Oechalia and killing Eurytus, the father of this Princess (who had refused her to him), he wished, having returned from his undertaking, to make an offering to Jupiter. Deianira, having learned of his infidelity, sent him—among the clothes he had requested from her for that ceremony—the robe of Nessus <gloss>5</gloss>. Hercules put this on; but a deadly sickness overcame him, accompanied by unbearable pains which drove him to madness; until, no longer able to withstand the intensity of the ailment, he had himself laid upon a funeral pyre and burned alive.

# ->HERCULES<-
## ->BIRTH AND DEEDS.<-

**B**efore we speak of <gloss>6</gloss> Hercules’ birth, we deem it useful to recount his deeds.

* Perseus had by Andromeda: Alceus, Sthenelus, Helas, Mestor, Electryon, and a daughter named Gorgophone, who was married to Perieres.

Alceus, married to Hipponome, daughter of Menoeceus, had two children by her, namely Amphitryon and his sister Anaxo.

From Mestor and Lysidice, daughter of Pelops, Hippothoe was born, who was abducted by Neptune—that is, by a pirate—and carried to the Echinadian islands, where she had a son called Taphius, who established a colony at Taphos, whose inhabitants he later caused to be called Teleboans. From this Taphius was born Pterelas, who had several male children and a daughter called Cometho.

Electryon married his niece Anaxo, daughter of Alceus, and from this marriage Alcmene came into the world.

From Sthenelus and Micippe, daughter of Pelops, were born Alcinoe, Medusa, and Eurystheus, who was later King of Mycenae.

After Taphius's death, Pterelas sent his children to Mycenae to request from their great-uncle Electryon, who was King there, their portion in the succession of their grandfather Mestor. When this Prince would not listen to them, they laid waste to the fields and drove away his herds. The sons

---

### ->REMARKS.<-

<columns>2</columns>
<gloss>5.</gloss> **THE ROBE OF NESSUS.]** Others believe (a) that it was a love potion from which he died; or a garment smeared with a certain sort of Babylonian glue <note>original: "Babylonisch lym," likely referring to bitumen or naphtha, which was known for its flammable properties.</note>, which, becoming warm, stuck fast to the skin.

<gloss>6.</gloss> **HERCULES.]** Here, mention is made only of the Theban Hercules. The ancients make mention of many other Heroes who bore the same name. Diodorus of Sicily (b) names three: One Egyptian, who traveled in Africa and erected those famous pillars named after him near Cadiz. The second was born on the island of Crete, among the Dactylic Idaeans. The third was the son of Jupiter and Alcmene. Cicero (c) is of the opinion that there were six. The first was the son of Jupiter and Lysita. The second was the Egyptian Hercules, who was born from the Nile. The third was one of the Dactylic Idaeans. The fourth the son of Jupiter and Asteria, Latona's sister. The fifth is the Indian, nicknamed Belus;

<column-break/>

and the sixth, the son of Jupiter and Alcmene.

The two oldest were the Tyrian and the Egyptian. One can add several others to those already mentioned; and there is hardly a province that does not speak of its own Hercules. Varro names as many as forty-three. This has given reason to believe that the name of Hercules is nothing other than a nickname, which people were accustomed to give to great men who had made themselves famous through their brave deeds. A contemporary writer (d) even believes that this name is derived from the Phoenician word *Harokel*, which means as much as a merchant; and that all those Herculeses came from nothing other than the exaggerated tales regarding the adventures of certain Phoenician merchants, who went to carry out their trade in far lands, established colonies there, and made themselves famous by [clearing] those lands of devouring beasts.

(a) *Historical Explanation of Fables* <note>original Latin/French: "Explicat. Histor. de Fables"</note>, by Mr. the Abbot *Banier*, Vol. III. (b) Book III & IV. (c) Book III of *On the Nature of the Gods* <note>original Latin: "de Nat. Deor."</note>. (d) Mr. Le Clerc. *Universal Library* <note>original Latin: "Bibl. Univerſ."</note> Vol. I.
* *Historical Explanation of Fables*, Vol. III.

# ->HERCULES’ BIRTH<-

...he always wore the skin of this animal as a sign of his victory. In this manner, the Poets recount the story of Hercules’ hunt for a very large Lion, which he killed. <note>The Nemean Lion, the first of the twelve labors.</note>

II. His second heroic deed was the killing of the Serpent of Lerna <note>The Hydra</note>; for which we have already provided the explanation.

III. In another hunt, he killed the ferocious Boar in the woods of Erymanthus. To embellish this history, it has been fancifully told that he brought it alive, bound, to Eurystheus, who, upon seeing it, nearly died of fright.

IV. After he had pursued a Hind, sacred to Diana, for an entire year in the mountains of Maenalus—which Eurystheus had commanded him to bring back alive—he finally caught the animal in its flight. To demonstrate its speed, it has been fancifully told that it had bronze feet.

V. He also hunted the Birds of Prey by Lake Stymphalis in Arcadia, which were so numerous that they darkened the sun and fell upon men to devour them. This is to say that Hercules and his companions rooted out a band of highwaymen who stayed by this lake and murdered passersby.

VI. When Eurystheus gave Hercules the command to seize the belt of Hippolyta, Queen of the Amazons—that is, to wage war against these Heroines and plunder their treasures—he set out by ship upon the Euxine Sea <note>original: "Pontus Euxinus," now known as the Black Sea</note>; and, having arrived at the banks of the Thermodon, he gave battle to the Amazons, defeated them, robbed their Queen of her belt, took her prisoner himself, and gave her to Theseus in marriage.

VII. The stables of King Augeas had not been cleaned in thirty years, and there was so much manure and filth in them that there was no more room for the beasts; whereupon Hercules, with the help of his people, diverted the stream Alpheus and caused it to flow through the middle of the stables; and in this way they were emptied and cleansed <gloss>11</gloss>.

VIII. The island of Crete being devastated by a raging Bull that breathed fire and flames—sent to them by Neptune to avenge a certain wrong done to him by the inhabitants—Hercules subdued it and brought it alive to Eurystheus. This means nothing other than that Eurystheus commanded him to go to the island of Crete to seek a Bull for the purpose of breeding calves.

IX. Diomedes, a tyrant of Thrace, cast all strangers who passed through his land to his beasts. Hercules, having conquered him, made him suffer the same fate. This is to say that, going there on Eurystheus's command to take some of Diomedes' horses (which were very fine), this prince was killed in the struggle when he tried to offer resistance; and because he had ruined himself <gloss>12</gloss> to feed his horses, and had even sold his slaves for that purpose, it was said that his horses were fed with human flesh.

<meta>X. The</meta>

---

### R E M A R K S.

11. **CLEANSED.**] Augeas rewarded this service very poorly, refusing to give Hercules the agreed-upon payment. For this reason, this Hero sought an opportunity to avenge himself, and having encountered Eurytus—who is sent by his Father every year to the Isthmian games—he put him to death. When he later arrived in Elis, he caused Augeas to be killed and appointed his son Phileus as King

<column-break/>

in his place; who, when he had been chosen as arbiter, had advised his Father to give Hercules his reward.

12. **HAD RUINED HIMSELF.**] Some have said this regarding the daughters of Diomedes, who grew fat at the expense of strangers, lured by their unchaste nature into the court of their Father, who gave them away to the highest bidder. Erasmus <note>Desiderius Erasmus (1466–1536), the famous Dutch humanist scholar.</note> says (a),

# ->T H E  D E E D S.<-

X. The island of Erythia <sup>13</sup> is ruled by a King named Geryon, who was said to have three bodies. The oxen kept by this Prince devoured strangers and were guarded by a Dog and a Dragon, of which the first had two heads and the other seven. Hercules overcame the Oxen, and, having killed the Dog and the Dragon, drove the herds away with him. The three heads of Geryon signify that he held dominion over three islands, namely Majorca, Minorca, and Ebuse or Ibiza. Some say that they were three brothers who lived in such close friendship that they seemed to have but one soul. Others <sup>14</sup> claim that this Fable owes its origin to the Phoenicians; and here is how they explain it. When Hercules had come to the island of Erythia, the inhabitants divided themselves into three bands to fight him; but they were defeated, which the Phoenicians expressed in their language with these words: *He defeated the three heads of their inhabitants* <note>original Phoenician: "Hacche thelath refche gereon"</note>; which literally means, *He defeated the three heads of their inhabitants*. But if the last word is taken as a proper name, it can be translated: *He defeated, or killed, the three heads of Geryon*. The name *Gargitius*, which the myth-writers give to Geryon's Dog, comes from two Phoenician words, *Gere-chitta* <note>original: "Gere-chitta," interpreted here as the "terror of strangers"</note>, that is, the terror of strangers.

XI. When Busiris <sup>15</sup> had sent out some pirates to kidnap the Hesperides <sup>16</sup>, daughters of Atlas, King of Mauritania, Hercules drove them away, rescued the Maidens, and even killed Busiris himself. Atlas <sup>17</sup>, to reward this service, gave him the finest sheep of his land as a gift. And because it was said that these sheep, due to the beauty of their wool <sup>18</sup>, had golden fleeces, and because the Greek word which means a *Sheep* also expresses an *Apple* <note>The Greek word "melon" (μῆλον) can mean both an apple or a sheep/goat</note>, the rumor spread that this Hero had brought back golden Apples from the garden of the Hesperides <sup>19</sup>. Some writers insist, however, that these Apples were nothing other than *Oranges* <sup>20</sup>, or Lemons, with which the gardens of Mauritania were filled; and the Dragon, which was killed by Hercules so that he could steal this precious fruit, would have been nothing more than a large Dog that guarded some of those gardens. Finally, others believe that through this Fable, one intended to signify the riches which Atlas or another Prince had gathered: for the word *Malon* or *Melon* means *Riches* in the Arabic language, and in Greek, an *Apple* or a *Sheep*, as we have already said.
