### BIRTH OF HERCULES

...to abduct the daughter of Aidoneus <note>A name often used for Pluto, the King of the Underworld, but here interpreted as a mortal King of the Molossians</note>, King of the Molossians, gave rise to this Fable. This Prince, informed of this, had Pirithoüs mauled by a dog which was given the name Cerberus, and held Theseus prisoner; but he released him some time later, at Hercules' request, from his prison. Since Aidoneus condemned his prisoners to the mines, and one had to cross a river called the Acheron when passing through his land, he has been conflated with Pluto, and the journey of Hercules has been viewed as a descent into Hell. This Hero, having killed a snake at the same time which had retreated into the cave of Taenarum—which was regarded as the Realm of the dead—and having brought a large dog from Epirus with him, it was said that he had chained Cerberus.

Such are the twelve labors of Hercules, of which the Poets have sung so much ²¹. Since the Ancients do not agree on everything regarding this subject, we shall recount some other deeds which have also been placed among his heroic exploits.

During his stay in Italy, he killed a famous robber named Cacus ²², who resided in a cave on the Aventine Hill and had stolen some oxen from him. Dionysius of Halicarnassus ²³ teaches us what is true in this history. Hercules, having come from Spain into Italy with his army, was forced to remain there for a long time to wait for his fleet and to bring certain peoples to obedience who had not been willing to submit voluntarily. Cacus, the leader of a band of robbers, relying on the natural strength of his retreat, had the courage to resist him. This Hero, having had his troops occupy a neighboring plain, attacked him by night while his people slept, plundered his camp, and returned laden with booty. Hercules besieged him in his stronghold; and Cacus, while defending himself bravely, falling in battle, the stronghold was won and razed.

When Hercules had undertaken to bring a colony to Africa to establish trade there, he was driven back by a merchant who had strengthened himself there to such an extent that it was impossible to overpower him. He lured him skillfully across the sea, and having cut off the access from the land side—from where he had to receive his supplies and new troops—he caused him to perish from want. From this comes the Fable of Antaeus, the son of the earth, that famous Giant seventy cubits high <note>About 105 feet; a cubit is the length of a forearm</note>, and who throttled all strangers when he wrestled with them. It has been feigned that Hercules, having thrown him to the ground three times and having noticed that he gained new strength every time he fell on the ground, held him aloft in the air until he had strangled him.

It is told that to relieve Atlas, he took the Heavens upon his shoulders...

...says that the Libyans called lemons *Apples of Hesperia*, and that Hercules brought a species of fruit called *golden apples* from that region into Greece.

21. OF WHICH THE POETS etc.] One would be mistaken if one wished to believe that Hercules always carried out his undertakings alone. The wisest among the Ancients assure us that he was provided with a good company; and Dionysius of Halicarnassus (a) says among others, that this Hero,

<column-break/>

the foremost General of his time, traveled through the whole world at the head of a numerous army, subduing Tyrants, punishing unjust Usurpers and possessors of lands, and civilizing the wildness of the most barbaric peoples through the wisdom of his laws.

22. CACUS.] Some of the Ancients say that he had three heads.

23. DIONYSIUS OF HALICARNASSUS.] Book I.

### ...AND DEEDS.

thirsty. We shall have the opportunity to explain this Fable when we speak of **ATLAS**.

When, in his journey to Spain, he had penetrated as far as Cadiz, which in ancient times was considered the end of the World, he erected two Pillars there as markers that he had finished his travels in that place. These Pillars, so famous in Fable-histories, are the mountains *Calpe* and *Abyla* <note>original: "24"</note>, one of which is in Africa and the other in Europe, at the narrow of the Strait of Gibraltar; and they were given the name of the Pillars of Hercules for no other reason than because this Hero, having reached those boundaries which nature itself had set, could not extend his conquests any further.

The liberation of **PROMETHEUS**, whom Jupiter had ordered to be bound to a Rock on Mount Caucasus, is also attributed to Hercules. We have explained this Fable in the Discourse on **PROMETHEUS**.

When Alcestis gave herself up to death to save the life of her lover, Admetus, Hercules descended into Hell, conquered Death, holding her bound with a diamond chain <note>Likely referring to "adamantine," the mythical unbreakable metal</note>, until she promised to give the Princess her life back. Behold here what gave occasion to this Fable: When Pelias was killed by his daughters, his son Acastus pursued his sisters even into the court of his cousin Admetus, where Alcestis had fled. As this Prince, who had fallen in love with her, would not return her, Acastus laid waste to his States; and when Admetus was taken prisoner in the battle, Alcestis offered herself as his ransom. Admetus obtained his freedom on this condition; but fearing that Acastus might avenge Pelias’s death upon his mistress, he requested Hercules’ help. This Hero pursued Acastus, rescued Alcestis, and delivered her back into the hands of Admetus, who took her in marriage.

If we wished now to recount the remaining deeds attributed to Hercules, we would go far beyond the limits we have set for ourselves. However, since the History of this Hero is one of the most important subjects of Mythology, we do not doubt that it will be appreciated if we add here a Chronological Historical account <note>original: "25"</note> of his principal life events, in which they are brought into alignment with the years before the birth of Jesus Christ and the Trojan War, and to the years of our Hero’s age.

Hercules was in his eighteenth year when he began to give proofs of his valor. At this time, he killed the Lion of Mount Cithaeron, defeated the Minyans, and killed their King, Erginus. Creon, King of Thebes, gave him his daughter Megara in marriage as a reward for this last deed. By this Princess, he fathered three children; and in this same year

### HERCULES' BIRTH

<note>Though the header reads "Birth," the text focus on the chronology of his adult life and the atonement for his crimes.</note>

...he lost Amphitryon, the husband of his mother Alcmene. After a lapse of several years, falling into a fit of madness—which occurred to him several times during his life—he killed the children he had fathered by his wife Megara. Forced by this incident to leave the court of Creon <note>The King of Thebes</note>, he went to consult the Oracle of Delphi about his fate. Apollo commanded him to travel to Mycenae or Athens, where Eurystheus reigned, assuring him further that by following the orders of this Prince, he would attain immortality. Hercules obeyed the command of the Gods; and, at the command of Eurystheus, he subsequently carried out those twelve deeds, so famous in Greek Heroic Histories. He was occupied with the first ten for eight years and one month; thus, if one sets a time of eight years for these twelve deeds, Hercules—who offered himself to the service of Eurystheus <note>Original: "Erytheus," likely a scribal error for Eurystheus</note> in his twenty-third year, four years after his marriage to Megara—was around thirty-three years old when he returned to Boeotia. Once there, he cast off Megara, because seeing her constantly brought to mind the miserable death of his sons, who had perished by his hands. After he had discarded her, he sought an opportunity to unite with another woman and sent a request for marriage to Iole, the daughter of Eurytus, King of Thessaly. But since the fate of Megara's children inspired horror at the thought of marrying Hercules, she was refused to him. This injustice having plunged him into a new fit of rage, he killed Iphitus, the brother of his beloved; but coming to himself, he had such a vivid feeling of this new misfortune that he sought to escape his remorse through religious duties. He went to consult with the Oracle at Delphi; which gave him the answer that there was no other means to atone for this murder than to let himself be sold as a slave in a foreign land. Before Hercules fulfilled Apollo's command, he judged himself bound to be reconciled through such ceremonies as were customary on such occasions; but he found no one who wished to do him that service except Theseus, who, having recently come from Troezen, reconciled him at the Isthmian Games.

To fulfill the Oracle of Apollo, Hercules let himself be sold as a slave by one of his friends and was brought to Libya <note>Mythologically, this usually occurs in Lydia, but the text specifies "Lybie"</note> to the court of Omphale, who, upon the death of her husband Tmolus, had become Queen of Libya. The first year of Hercules' servitude began with his thirty-fourth. The second, which began with his thirty-fifth, was the year in which a son was born to him from a slave girl of Omphale, called Malis by Hellanicus. Herodotus calls this son Alcaeus; Diodorus, Cleolaus; and Hellanicus, Akelis.

Hercules had made himself famous through his undertakings against the Cercopes <term>Cercopes: A pair of mischievous forest creatures or bandits, often depicted as monkey-like, whom Hercules captured</term>, a kind of bandits who ravaged Libya; his reputation and name had also made Omphale curious, and she soon learned of his birth and bravery. This hero did not limit his undertakings to slaying monsters: he was still young, and not over thirty-six years old; and therefore he understood very well the art of pleasing the Queen. This Princess, who was a widow and cared for nothing, surrendered herself fearlessly to her passion; and became pregnant with a son, whom Apollodorus calls Agelaus, and Diodorus, Lamon. His slavery lasted three years and ended in his thirty-seventh year. When, in this same year, he had equipped a small fleet of six ships, he went with it to wage war against Laomedon, King of Troy, who had offended the Greeks through his treachery. Hercules, though having few people with him, succeeded...

### AND DEEDS.

...in this undertaking, he fared very well. He took the City of Troy by surprise, killed Laomedon and his children, except for Priam, whom he placed on the throne, and carried away Hesione, the sister of this young Prince; she later married Telamon and was the mother of Teucer.

Hercules, having returned to the Peloponnesus <note>The large peninsula forming the southern part of mainland Greece</note> after the conquest of Troy and Laomedon, prepared himself to punish Augias, King of Elis, for the treachery committed against him while he was busy carrying out the commands of Eurystheus. A great number of Arcadians and volunteers from all the cities of Greece entered into Hercules' service. Augias, for his part, considered means of resistance and raised an army, the supreme command of which he gave to his nephews, the sons of Molion. But Hercules, having fallen ill in the midst of this first campaign—possibly a consequence of a dangerous wound received during his return from Troy at the siege of Kos—entered into negotiations with the sons of Molion. However, when they broke the truce and attacked his troops in violation of the treaty, he also considered himself no longer bound to fulfill the obligations that had been first violated by his enemies. He then set an ambush against the Molionids, surprised them while they were going to offer sacrifices at the Isthmian games in the name of the people of Peloponnesus, and defeated them near *Cleonae*.

After the death of the Molionids, Hercules marched to Elis; there he surprised Augias, who was not expecting such a sudden attack, and killed him along with his children, except for Phyleus, the youngest, who had taken no part in his father's treachery; and to him he left the Kingdom of Elis. Hercules departed from here for Messenia, intending to avenge himself on Neleus, who had refused to atone for the murder of Iphitus <note>Neleus had refused to purify Hercules of the blood-guilt for killing Iphitus</note>. He passed through Olympia and attended the funeral games established a few years earlier in honor of Pelops, his great-grandfather on his mother's side. Here he competed himself, regulated the ceremonies of those games, and made the Greeks of the Peloponnesus understand that they were all bound to gather there and make of those games a General Council <note>Original: "algemeene Raedsvergadering"</note> to bring their common interests before it.

Polybius <note>A famous Greek historian of the Hellenistic period</note> says that Hercules was the founder of the Olympic games and assembly, and that he regulated all the ceremonies there: he adds that he gave a sort of accounting there to justify his wars against the Greek peoples, claiming that he had undertaken nothing without higher orders (that is, those of Eurystheus) or without being forced by a lawful defense.

As soon as these games, which were celebrated around the summer solstice, were ended, Hercules marched to Pylos; he took the city by storm and slew the sons of Neleus, who was King there, in the battle: they were nine in number. Nestor, the youngest of all, alone escaped the defeat: for he was at Gerenium at that time.

From Pylos he went to Lacedaemon <note>The region of Sparta</note>, from which he drove out Hippocoön and restored Tyndareus—the father of Helen, and of Castor and Pollux—to the throne.

Subsequently, he settled with the military bands that had accompanied him on his expeditions in Pheneus in Arcadia, near Sicyon; where he spent four full years: but Eurystheus, fearful of the proximity of Hercules—a man of such an enterprising nature—forced him in the fifth year, which was his forty-fourth, to leave the Peloponnesus entirely. He never returned there afterward; but he departed with his troops for Aetolia and entered the service of the King of Calydon. Shortly thereafter he married Dejanira, the daughter of this Prince, and sister of

->### HERCULES' DEEDS<-

Meleager, by whom he had three sons. While he was in Aetolia <note>A mountainous region in west-central Greece</note>, he waged war against the Thesprotians, and, having captured the city of Ephyra, he carried away with him the daughter of the King of the Thesprotians, Astyochia. By her he fathered a son, named Tlepolemus by Homer, who served among the Greeks in the siege of Troy and was slain by Sarpedon.

Hercules married Deianira in the first year of his stay at the Aetolian Court. It was also in that same year that he won the city of Ephyra from the King of the Thesprotians. This city, later called *Cichyrus*, was built on the banks of the Cocytus and the Acherusian Lake, formed by the waters of the river Acheron <note>In Greek mythology, these rivers are associated with the Underworld, though here they are treated as physical geography</note>. Here reigned Aidoneus, according to the testimony of Pausanias—the same man whose wife Persephone Theseus attempted to abduct. Plutarch calls him the King of the Molossians, inhabitants of a land neighboring Thesprotia, where the Oracle of Dodona was located. There was in Thesprotia itself that very famous Oracle of the Dead, which gave the Poets the occasion to confuse this Aidoneus with Hades or Pluto. It was, in all likelihood, during this war that Hercules released Theseus from the prison of Aidoneus.

The forty-fifth year of Hercules' life, or the fifty-sixth after the taking of Troy <note>The chronology here follows specific historical reconstructions common in the early modern period</note>, was the birth year of Hyllus; and in that same year, Hercules was forced by himself to leave Aetolia because of an involuntary manslaughter. He departed, accompanied only by his wife Deianira and his son Hyllus. During this journey, he had that encounter with the Centaur Nessus and received from Deianira 그 deadly gift, the consequences of which are known to everyone.

Hercules went to Trachis, to King Ceyx. His bands of soldiers joined him there, and, having received new auxiliary troops from Arcadia, he undertook the war against the Dryopians for the sake of Aegimius, King of the Dorians. He also defeated the Lapiths, the King's enemies. Hercules spent most of the forty-fifth and forty-seventh years of his life on these undertakings.

This Hero had little love for Deianira; and weary of carrying a woman with him in his exile whom he had only married to find a safe sanctuary through this marriage, he let his thoughts turn to making a marriage alliance with someone else. To this end, he sent a request for Astydamia, daughter of Ormenius, King of the Thessalian Pelasgians; but when she was refused him, he waged war against him, took his capital city, killed him, and carried away his daughter as a prisoner. After this undertaking, seeing himself at the head of a mighty and warlike army—which he could not maintain except by war and plunder, as he had no state over which to rule—he decided to go and wage war against the sons of Eurytus in Oechalia. He used as his pretext that they had previously refused him their sister Iole. To his Arcadian bands, he added the Dorians, the Locrians, and the Trachinians; and with this power, he quickly brought the war to an end. The City of Oechalia was conquered; Eurytus’ children were killed; and Iole fell into Hercules’ hands. The sight of this Princess very easily kindled a fondness in his heart, which time had not entirely extinguished; and Deianira, fearing that she would be cast off by him, judged it was now time to use that love potion <note>original: "liefdesap"</note> given to her by the Centaur Nessus. Thinking then that this juice would make her husband’s heart remain faithful to her, she smeared it upon the robe <note>This refers to the famous "Shirt of Nessus," which was poisoned, not a love charm</note> which he was to put on for the performance of his sacrifices. But this juice, being in part the blood of...

# AND DEATH.

<sig>S 2</sig>

...mixed with the Centaur <note>The text continues from the previous page, referring to the blood of the Centaur Nessus, which had been tainted by Hercules' own arrows</note>, was a powerful poison; because the arrows of Hercules, dipped in the venom of the Hydra <note>The original Dutch *Poelſlange* translates to "marsh-serpent," referring to the multi-headed Hydra of Lerna</note>, infected the blood of those who were struck by them, and changed it also into venom. Scarcely had Hercules put on the fatal tunic <note>Commonly known in mythology as the "Shirt of Nessus." His wife, Deianira, gave it to him thinking it was a love charm, unaware it was poisoned</note> before he felt himself seized by the most agonizing pains. The efforts he made to tear off the garment, which was already clinging to his skin, served no other purpose than to give the venom—which was now fully mingled with his blood—even greater strength. When he could no longer endure staying in that condition, he had himself placed upon a funeral pyre and burned alive, in the forty-ninth year of his age, and the 53rd year before the capture of Troy <note>The author uses a specific historical timeline common in the 17th and 18th centuries to date mythological events</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the mythological figures Castor and Pollux, following the account of their mother Leda. It concludes the narrative of their transformation into the constellation Gemini and provides rationalist interpretations of the myth.</meta>

# THE TWINS.

abductors, and having seized them, Castor perished—after he had killed Lynceus—by the hands of Idas, whom Pollux then deprived of life. Jupiter, to comfort his son over the loss of such a dearly beloved brother, promised to allow him to share in the immortality which Castor <note>The author here appears to reverse the traditional myth; usually, Pollux is the immortal son of Jupiter, while Castor is the mortal son of Tyndareus.</note> possessed by virtue of his birth. He placed them both in the Heavens, where they constitute the Sign of the Twins, and where one sees them die and be born in turns.

## EXPLANATION OF THE FABLE.

The interpretations that writers have attempted to give for the Fable of Leda differ greatly from one another. Some authors claim that it is based on nothing other than Helen’s beauty, and especially on the extraordinary whiteness of her neck and bosom; which they believed could be expressed in no other way than by saying that she had a Swan for her Father. Others guess that Leda may have had a certain encounter on the banks of the Eurotas; and that, since many swans frequented that place, it was believed that the honor of the Queen could be preserved by spreading the rumor that Jupiter, in order to deceive her, had taken the form of a Swan. Others finally suggest that what has been said of the unusual birth of the Tyndarides <note>The "Tyndarides" refers to the children of Leda: Castor, Pollux, Helen, and Clytemnestra.</note> must be attributed to the ambiguity of the Greek word *Oon* <note>original Greek: ᾠόν</note>, which signifies both an egg <gloss>7</gloss> and the highest stories of a house; and that the Fable of the Egg is based on nothing more than that Leda received her Lover in one of the upper rooms of her Palace, or that she had her children raised there.

After Castor and Pollux had earned, through their deeds, the right to be placed in the rank of demigods, they were placed among the Constellations, in the Sign of the Twins. And since one of the principal stars that make up that Sign sets when the other rises, it was feigned that they die and are born in turns.

# P R O T E U S.

stood, he had entered, and after he had told her the cause of his distress, which was easily calmed, his sisters, the Nymphs, each did their best to receive him: one gave him water to wash himself; others served the table; others were seen bringing the vessels necessary for the meal. Also, the fire on the altar, to burn incense, was already lit. It was then that Cyrene, turning to her son, spoke to him thus: "Take, my son, take this cup of Meonian wine <note>4</note>; and let us pour it out in honor of the Sea-god." Having said this, she directed her prayer to Neptune and the Nymphs, his sisters, and poured the liquid three times upon the burning hearth, and three times the flame rose up to the vault of the hall. Taking courage from this omen, she let herself be heard in this manner: "While I speak with you, my son, Proteus <note>5</note>, one of our Sea-gods, is crossing the Carpathian Sea <note>6</note> in a chariot drawn by sea-horses. He is on his way to return to his fatherland of Macedonia, and to visit Pallene <note>7</note>, his birthplace. This God is honored by the Nymphs, and even by Nereus, because he has the gift of knowing the present, the past, and the future. It has pleased Neptune to favor him with this privilege, in return for his care in driving his uncouth sea-cattle <note>original: "onbeschoft watervee," referring to seals</note> to pasture beneath the sea. You must, O son, first manage to bind this Soothsayer, if you wish him to reveal to you the cause of your disasters, and to point out the remedy for them. Do not think that you will obtain his answer without doing him violence, or that you will soften him with prayers. When you have caught him fast, do not spare him at all, but pull the snares with which you have bound him with all your might: thereby you will make him lose all his trickery. I myself will lead you into the secret chamber where this old Gray-beard, weary from staying under the water, goes to rest upon the shore. There you shall catch him in his deepest sleep. You must be warned that when you have him in your snares, he will attempt to deceive you. Immediately he will take the form of various wild beasts. Now you will see him change into a wild boar; a moment later into a tiger; then into a lion. When he has changed into fire, he will crackle loudly like a flame to escape you, and having melted into water, will try to slip through your fingers. But hold him, the more he changes into all sorts of shapes, all the more tightly in your snares, until you see him take on that same form in which you surprised him while he slept." The Goddess, having given her son this wholesome advice, anointed him with a scent of Ambrosia, which gave his whole body a lively strength.

At the side of the mountain is a cavern, made by the sea, and gently sloping towards the rock. Here Proteus goes to rest, after he had closed the mouth of the cave with a mighty stone. He is followed by his fish, which, leaping all around him, sprinkle him with water which they spout from their mouths. His sea-oxen <note>referring to seals</note> go here and there on the shore to lie down and sleep.

He himself goes to lie down to taste the sweetness of sleep; but Aristaeus hardly gives him time to settle comfortably and, as soon as he sees a favorable opportunity, falls upon him and binds him with the snares he had ready. Proteus, then not forgetting his usual tricks, transforms himself into all sorts of shapes: into a wild beast, into a fire, and into water. > “O young and reckless mortal,” he spoke thus to Aristaeus, “who advised you to attack me in my cave? What do you intend with this plot?... You know it well, divine Proteus,” said the Shepherd, “you know it well: for who can deceive you? Rather, you yourself stop trying to trick me. I come here at the command of the Gods, to consult you in my misfortune.” 

Aristaeus said no more. Proteus then, compelled by the violence done to him, turned his glowing and sea-green eyes upon him, and after a violent movement, began to give him this prophecy:

<margin>8. **WHEN EURYDICE, TO ESCAPE YOUR PURSUIT.** See the Commentary on Orpheus.</margin>
> “Your crime, O Aristaeus, exceeds your punishment. The wretched Orpheus brings these disasters upon you. When Eurydice, to escape your pursuit 8, fled swiftly along the bank of a river before you, a snake, hidden in the grass, bit her and caused her death. This is your crime; and the loss you complain of is the effect of the wrath of a Fury <note>The original Dutch uses "Wraekgodinne" (Goddess of Vengeance).</note>.” 

Thus spoke Proteus, leaping down into the sea from above after finishing these words.

<margin>9. **CYRENE APPROACHED ARISTAEUS.** The following does not properly belong to the Myth of Proteus. However, although we have greatly shortened Virgil's narrative, it will be easily seen that we could not have made this passage any shorter.</margin>
Hereupon Cyrene approached Aristaeus 9, who was terrified beyond measure by the Soothsayer’s answer.

> “My son,” she spoke, “it is now time to let go of your sad heartache; for we now know the cause of your misfortunes. The Nymphs, who inhabit the same woods with Eurydice and are her playmates, have brought death to your bees. Think of offering them your gifts and reconciling with them; and strive through your reverence to obtain mercy, which they will very easily grant you.”

She then taught him which sacrifices he must use to reconcile with them. Aristaeus did not delay in obeying his mother’s commands. He then went to the Temple of the Nymphs, where he erected altars and brought four bulls and as many cows. Nine days later, he performed a funeral sacrifice for Orpheus; and to appease Eurydice, he honored her shades <note>Spirit or ghost</note> by slaughtering a cow and a black sheep for her. Thereupon he goes to the sacred grove where he had left the bodies of the sacrificial animals, and sees a new and unexpected miracle. In the entrails of the bulls lying on the ground, the bees are heard buzzing. These animals were born inside these sacrificial beasts from the fermenting fluids, and the swarms are seen bursting forth from the ribs of the bulls. Suddenly a thick throng comes out like a cloud, which attaches itself to a tree and looks like a cluster of grapes on a vine.

<meta>This page transitions from the poetic myth of Proteus and Aristaeus to a rationalized, historical interpretation of the legend (a method known as Euhemerism). The previous page concluded the narrative of the sea-god's prophecy, while this page explains the "real" man behind the myth.</meta>

# PROTEUS.

...ruled during a time of war. Because he was a wise and prudent Prince, it was believed that he possessed the gift of foresight; and because his designs were inscrutable, one had to, so to speak, bind and surprise him in order to discover them. His political secrets, impossible to fathom, caused him to assume all manner of appearances to carry out his intentions. In addition to these fine qualities, he was proud by nature, seldom appeared in public, and would not suffer anyone to meet him on the road. Only a small number of the highest-ranking courtiers had the freedom to accompany him: it is these whom Homer and Virgil call *Phoci* <note>original: "Phoci"; Latin for seals or sea-calves.</note>, or sea-calves, or large fish; and the flocks over which they give him oversight are nothing other than soldiers, whom he frequently inspected. Such a man was this Prince, from whom they have made a Sea-god and Soothsayer, and to whom they attribute such strange <term>Metamorphoses</term> <note>The Dutch "Gedaentewiſſelingen" refers to the changing of forms or shapes.</note>. The name *Cete* <note>original: "Cete"; from the Greek *ketos*, meaning a whale or sea monster.</note>, which Diodorus gives him and which signifies a whale or large fish, may also have given occasion to this Fable: as well as that which this Writer adds, that this Prince often covered his helmet with the skin of one animal or another, such as a Lion, Tiger, Panther, or Serpent, in order to arouse all the greater respect for himself among his people.

The explanation provided here is the most probable and built upon the best authority. Some Writers have said that Proteus was a Sophist, or a deceptive Orator. Lucian 11 holds him to be a nimble dancer or mimic, so supple of limb that he assumed all manner of gestures. Heraclides of Pontus believes that this Fable signifies the various forms which the Universal Mind has given to matter. Others are of the opinion that Truth is understood by it, which remains hidden for those who neglect the investigation of it.

# GLAUCUS
## TRANSFORMED INTO A
# SEA GOD.

> <note>original: "Pabula decerpsi, decerptaque dente momordi. / Vix bene combiberant ignotos guttura succos, / Cum subito trepidare intus præcordia sensi, / Alteriusque rapi naturæ pectus amore. / Nec potui restare diu; repetendaque nunquam / Terra vale dixi, corpusque sub æquore mersi. / Dii maris exceptum socio dignantur honore."</note> *I plucked the herbs, and chewed what I had plucked with my teeth. Scarcely had the unknown juices reached my throat when I felt my heart tremble within, and my breast was seized by the desire for another nature. Nor could I remain long; saying "Farewell, land that I shall never return to," I plunged my body beneath the waves. The gods of the sea deigned to receive me with a shared honor.*
>
> ->OVID. Metamorphoses 13.<-

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'G' featuring scrolling floral and acanthus leaf motifs.</image-desc>

Glaucus was a famous fisherman from the city of <note>Anthedon was a port city in Boeotia, Greece, known for its association with this myth.</note> Anthedon. One would constantly see him on the seashore with his nets, or sitting on a rock fishing with a hook. By the shore, where he usually spent his time, was a green meadow, washed on one side by the sea waves, and on the other always covered with a pleasant greenery; of which no sheep nor goats had ever tasted: the bees themselves had never fed upon its flowers. They had also never been plucked to make crowns, and they were never mown. Glaucus was the first who went to sit in this lovely meadow to dry his nets. On a certain day, while busy counting his caught fish, he witnessed an unbelievable miracle. As soon as the fish touched the ground where he threw them down, they began to move and jump, just as they used to do in the sea. While he marveled at this occurrence, they all escaped from him into the sea. Standing amazed, he did not know whether to attribute this wonder to one god or another, or to the powers of this meadow. "Is it possible," he said to himself, "that this grass has such a miraculous power?" He went immediately to test it; he plucked it, brought it to his mouth, and chewed it. Scarcely had he gotten the juice into his throat, when he felt his heart and entrails tremble, and was seized by such a violent urge to change his nature, that it was impossible for him to resist. He then left the earth, never to return to it again,

He says that Glaucus, having caught a hare on a certain mountain in Aetolia, brought it to the foot of a fountain; and that, having covered it with a certain herb which he found there, the animal, which was already dead, again
</margin>

Glaucus was a famous Fisherman from the city of Anthedon. He was constantly seen on the sea-shore with his nets, or sitting on a rock fishing with a hook. Near the strand where he usually spent his time, there was a green meadow, washed on one side by the sea waves and on the other always covered with pleasant greenery, from which no sheep nor goats had ever tasted; even the bees had never fed upon the flowers there. No one had ever plucked them to make garlands <note>The original "kroontjes" refers to small wreaths or crowns made of flowers.</note>, and they were never mown. Glaucus was the first to sit in this lovely meadow to dry his nets. On a certain day, while busy counting his caught fish, he witnessed an unbelievable miracle. As soon as the fish<sup>1</sup> touched the ground upon which he threw them, they began to move and jump, just as they used to do in the sea. While he marveled at this occurrence, they all escaped from him back into the sea. Standing amazed, he did not know whether to attribute this wonder to one God or another, or to the powers of this meadow. "Is it possible," he said to himself, "that this grass has such a wonderful power?" He immediately went to put it to the test; he plucked it, brought it to his mouth, and chewed it. Scarcely had he gotten the juice into his throat when he felt his heart and entrails tremble, and he was seized by such a violent impulse to change his nature that he could not possibly resist. He then left the earth, never to return to it again,

<meta>This page concludes the story of Glaucus's transformation into a sea deity, previously describing his discovery of a magical herb. It then provides a rationalized historical explanation and a scholarly catalog of various figures named Glaucus in Greek mythology.</meta>

and leaped into the sea. The Gods who inhabited it received him among them, and requested Oceanus and Tethys <note>The primordial Titan deities of the sea.</note> to strip away all that was mortal from him. These two deities purified him by having him recite incomprehensible words nine times, and by commanding him to throw himself into a hundred rivers. Immediately, one hundred springs were seen to open, forming as many rivers that flowed over his head. When he regained his senses, he found that he was entirely different than before, both in respect to his body and his soul. He then saw for the first time that green beard, and that long hair which trails behind him on the water; those broad shoulders, bluish arms, and that fish-tail which, beginning from the waist, serves him in place of legs. He was placed in the rank of the Sea-gods, and men erected temples and altars to him.

### EXPLANATION OF THE FABLE.

Palaephatus ² <note>A Greek author (c. 4th century BCE) known for providing rational, non-supernatural explanations for myths.</note> tells us that Glaucus ³ was a very skilled fisherman and a good swimmer from the city of Anthedon in Boeotia. He sometimes stayed under water so long that people held him for lost; and, subsequently appearing again, he claimed that he had spoken with the Sea-gods, about which he told wonderful things. But finally he drowned, according to the account of the same writer; and since he had gained much esteem through his deceptions, the people convinced themselves that the Sea-gods had accepted him into their company, and they paid him divine honors as such.

### NOTES.

1. <note>The start of this note is missing, but it refers to a magical herb that restored a fish or snake to life.</note> ...became alive again. After which Glaucus, wishing to taste of this herb, had barely swallowed it before he threw himself into the sea.
2. PALEPHATUS.] <note>original: "Lib. II. c. 28."</note> Book II, chapter 28.
3. GLAUCUS.] Athenaeus (a) <note>A Greek rhetorician and grammarian, author of the 'Deipnosophistae'.</note>, who gathered together what the Ancient Writers related of Glaucus, says that he fell in love with Ariadne, whom Theseus had left on the island of Naxos. Having tried to abduct her, Bacchus punished him by binding him to a grapevine with vine-shoots for some time. In the sea-battle between Jason and the Tyrrhenians, he was the only one who was not wounded; but, changed into a Sea-god, he appeared in the middle of the sea, and there was no one but Jason who noticed it. Some claim that he kept his residence with the Nereids <note>Sea nymphs, daughters of Nereus.</note> on one and the same island; that he, like them, foretold the future; and that he was loved by one of these Nymphs named Europia. He also loved Idna, daughter of Scyllus of Scione. It is further related that, having abducted Syme, he departed for Asia and went to live on a desolate island which was named Syme after the name of his wife.

<column-break/>

The Ancients do not agree on his birth. Some give him a certain Copeus as his Father; others say he was the son of Polybus (son of Mercury) and of Euboea (daughter of Larymnus); or of Neptune and the Nymph Naïs. Finally, there are writers who name his father Anthedon, after the name of his birth-city, and his mother Alcyone. There have been several different men named Glaucus, who must not be confused with the Glaucus known as the Pontian. One, who was the son of Sisyphus, was devoured by his horses—that is, he ruined himself through the enormous expenses he spent on his horses. The second was the son of Minos; and the third the son of Hippolochus; it is this latter one who exchanged his golden armor for that of Diomedes, which was made of bronze <note>A famous episode in Homer's 'Iliad' illustrating a very unequal trade.</note>. Polybius speaks of many other persons who bore the same name: such as Glaucus, son of Aepytus, King of the Messenians; Glaucus, son of Antenor; Glaucus of Carystus; Glaucus of Chios, the inventor of the art of welding iron together; Glaucus of Sparta; and Glaucus, a sculptor of Argos.

->(a) <note>original: "Lib. VII. c. 12."</note> Book VII, chapter 12.<-

# ALPHEUS AND ARETHUSA. 87

fields, forests, mountains, rocks, and the wildest places. Finally, seeing the sun behind her back, she saw the shadow of Alpheus, following close on her heels, stretching out before her feet. She first convinced herself that it was an effect of the fear with which she felt herself seized; but finally the sound he made while running made her realize she was not deceived. In this extreme distress, she called upon Diana for her help. “Goddess,” she spoke, “come to my aid, and do not refuse your protection to a Nymph who has dedicated herself to your service, and has often had the honor of carrying your quiver and bow.” The Goddess, moved by this prayer, covered Arethusa with a thick cloud. Alpheus, amazed that he saw her disappear, searched for her around the cloud, circling it twice without seeing her. “Arethusa,” he cried, “Arethusa, where are you?” The terrified Nymph did not yet believe she was safe; like a sheep that hears the wolf howling around the sheepfold; or like a hare which, crouched in a thorn bush, sees the dogs searching for him and dares not move. When Alpheus saw no footsteps from which he could judge that Arethusa continued her flight, he went no further; but he kept his eyes fixed upon the cloud. The Nymph then felt a cold sweat spread through all her limbs; the water dripping from it, running onto the earth, surrounded her on all sides; and in an instant she was transformed into a spring. But the God recognized his lover under this metamorphosis; and laying aside the human form he had assumed, he took up that of a river again, to mingle his waters with those of Arethusa. Diana then caused the ground to burst open to give the Nymph passage. But the enamored Alpheus did not leave her; and having followed her through the deepest subterranean hollows, Ortygia <note>An island in Syracuse, Sicily, where the Spring of Arethusa is located.</note> finally saw their waters mingle with one another.

->EXPLANATION OF THE FABLE.<-

Bochart ¹ <note>Samuel Bochart (1599–1667) was a famous French scholar of biblical geography and exotic languages.</note> guesses that the Phoenicians, who went to settle in Sicily, seeing the spring of Arethusa surrounded by willow trees, gave her the name of *En-alphe*, or *Halphaja*, that is, the spring of Willow Trees. Possibly, he adds, they may also have called it *En-alphe*, or *Halphaja*, the spring of ships, as it was on the island of Ortygia, on the seashore and close by the mouth of the harbor of Syracuse, where they used to go to fetch water. Others give the spring the name of *Arith* ², which signifies a brook. The similarity of the names led the Greeks, who came to Sicily long after the Phoenicians, to believe that this spring took its origin from the river Alpheus, which flows in Elis; and it was for this reason that they invented the Fable of the amorous dealings between this River God and the Nymph Arethusa. From this ³ also comes that very great journey through subterranean paths which this river is said to make to mingle with the waters of his Beloved. From this also comes that Fable, which has been spread, that the water of the fountain of Arethusa tasted of dung whenever the Olympic games were celebrated in Elis; and that at a certain time a cup was found therein which had fallen into the Alpheus.

->REMARKS.<-

1. BOCHART.] *Canaan*, Book I. ch. 28.
2. ARITH.] Bochart derives the name *Cypara*, which the Greeks also gave to this spring, from the Phoenician word *Kabira*, which word means *great*; and he proposes that from *Arith Kabira*, which means a *great brook*, they formed the names
<column-break/>
of *Arethusa* and *Cypara*. It indeed appears from many passages of the Ancients (a), that it was a very prominent spring.
3. FROM THIS ALSO COMES THAT VERY GREAT JOURNEY etc.] It is not only the Poets who have created fictions,
<note>(a) Cicero, *Against Verres* 4; Diodorus Siculus, 1, 5.</note>

# SALMACIS
### AND
# HERMAPHRODITUS.

---

> -> *The blended bodies of the two are joined, and one face is put upon them.* <-
> *They are not two, but a twofold form; such that it can be called neither woman*
> *nor boy: it seems to be neither, and yet both.*
> -> OVID, Metamorphoses Book 4. <note>Original Latin: "Mixta duorum Corpora junguntur, faciesque inducitur illis Una. Nec duo sunt, sed forma duplex; nec foemina dici, Nec puer ut possit: neutrumque & utrumque videtur."</note> <-

THE Naiads <note>Water nymphs who inhabited brooks, springs, and fountains</note> once raised a child in the caves of Mount Ida, whom Mercury <note>The messenger of the gods</note> had fathered by Venus <note>The goddess of love</note>. He bore the name Hermaphroditus, after the names of his father and mother, because he perfectly resembled them both in his features. Having reached the age of fifteen, he left the mountains where he had been raised to go traveling. Having arrived in Caria <note>An ancient region in western Asia Minor, modern-day Turkey</note>, he stopped by a spring bordered with beautiful green sod, and with water so clear that the bottom shone through it. The nymph Salmacis, who had her dwelling there, was the only nymph unknown to Diana <note>The goddess of the hunt and chastity, to whom most nymphs were devoted</note>. Having an aversion to the exercises of the hunt, she spent her life in a soft idleness, taking care only to adorn herself. Lying on the grass, she was busy picking flowers when she noticed a young traveler. Hardly had she seen him than she became deathly in love with him. Nevertheless, before she went to him, she wanted to see if anything was lacking in her attire. Then, surrendering herself to her passion, she made it known to him with words that expressed all the fieriness of her desires. Hermaphroditus, who did not yet know what love was, turned red with shame; and that color, lending him a new charm, moved the nymph to approach him to embrace him. The youth, astonished by her intense fits of love, wanted to take flight, but the nymph, fearing she would lose him, feigned a desire to leave the place and went to hide behind a thicket. Hermaphroditus, believing that he was alone, went—after he had walked around the spring for some time—to undress himself in order to bathe in it. Salmacis, having thrown off her garment and cast herself into the water with him, cried out: "I have won; you cannot escape me now!" He, fending her off, tried to escape her embraces; but she held him fast, despite all his struggling, tightly clasped in her arms.

### R E M A R K S.

I. AFTER THE NAME OF HIS FATHER AND MOTHER.] The Greeks called Mercury, *Hermes*, and Venus, *Aphrodite*.

# THE SIRENS.

> The Sirens, that cheerful punishment of sailors,
> Those charming deaths and cruel joy,
> Whom no man who heard them ever escaped,
> The clever Ulysses is said to have left behind.
<note>original Latin: "Sirenas hilarem navigantium pœnam, Blandasque mortes, gaudiumque crudele, Quas nemo quondam deferebat auditas, Fallax Ulysses dicitur reliquisse."</note>

->MARTIAL, book 3, Epigram 63.<-

---

The Sirens, daughters of the river Achelous and the Muse <gloss>1</gloss> Calliope, were nymphs, half maiden and half bird <note>In early mythology, Sirens were depicted as bird-women; the fish-tailed "mermaid" image became more common later.</note>. They lured people who came near the island where they resided with their sweet singing and caused them to die in an inhuman manner. Anyone who had the imprudence to approach them and listen to them was held by the charm of their song in a vast meadow, where nothing was seen but the fragments of dead men's bones and corpses dried by the sun. When the ship of Ulysses <note>The Roman name for the Greek hero Odysseus.</note> was driven by the winds to this disastrous island, this Hero—having been instructed by Circe on the means to escape the danger—plugged his own ears and those of his companions with wax after kneading it with his fingers, and had himself bound hand and foot to the mast of the ship. The Sirens, becoming aware of their approach, began to sing, lifting their lovely voices. "Come closer," they sang, "come closer, O brave Ulysses, ornament and glory of the Greeks! Guide your ship to this shore to hear our voice. No one has ever traveled past these places without first marveling at the pleasant harmony of our songs. Once you have enjoyed this pleasure and learned many things from us, you may continue your journey. For we know the hardships which the Greeks and Trojans endured before the walls of Troy by the will of the gods; and nothing that happens in this world is unknown to us." Ulysses, moved by the pleasantness of their voices, wished to go closer to hear them; he gave a signal to his companions to release him. But Perimedes and Eurylochus, standing up, bound him even tighter; and, all together redoubling their strength, they made the sea foam strongly and thus escaped that terrifying danger. The Sirens, because they had not been able to detain him, were so overcome with spite that they threw themselves into the sea, where the lower part of their bodies was changed into a fish, while the head and upper body retained their former shape.

->*R E M A R K S.*<-

1. OF THE MUSE CALLIOPE.] Others say of *Terpsichore*, or *Melpomene*.

<page-num>92</page-num>
<header>THE SIRENS.</header>

## EXPLANATION OF THE FABLE.

The Sirens were <gloss>2</gloss> three loose women, <gloss>3</gloss> who inhabited three small islands named the *Sirenusae* after them, located near Capri, opposite Sorrento. They lured strangers with their beauty and sweet voices, who then brought themselves to ruin through luxury and great expense. The name *Siren* comes from the <gloss>4</gloss> Punic word *Sir*, which means *Song*. It has been said that they were the daughters of the River Achelous because the island of Taphos, from which they had departed to settle in Capri, was at the mouth of that river. Others, however, maintain that the Fable of the Sirens originated because a certain pleasant sound was heard near Capri or Sorrento, caused by the sea waves breaking between the rocks, which drew sailors toward them and caused them to suffer shipwreck.

Ovid <gloss>5</gloss> tells us the history of the metamorphosis of Achelous's daughters <gloss>6</gloss> into birds. "When Proserpina," <note>The daughter of Ceres, abducted by Pluto to the underworld.</note> he says, "was abducted by Pluto, the Sirens—after they had searched for her in vain across the whole earth—prayed that they might be given wings so they could also search for her over the sea. Their prayers were granted: they received bird wings and feet; but they retained their human faces and voices." <gloss>7</gloss> That is to say, they equipped ships with sails to go in search of Proserpina.

---

### R E M A R K S.

<columns>2</columns>

<gloss>2</gloss>. WERE THREE.] There is no agreement regarding their names. Those who posit three call them *Parthenope*, *Leucosia*, and *Ligea*. Others recognize only two, without mentioning names. Some count five, naming the last two *Aglaophone* and *Mopse*.

<gloss>3</gloss>. THREE LOOSE WOMEN, WHO INHABITED THREE SMALL ISLANDS.] See here what Servius says of this (a).
"According to the Fables, the Sirens were three Nymphs; half maiden and half bird; born of the River Achelous and the Muse Calliope. One sang; another played the flute; and the third played the lyre. At that time they lived near Pelorus, and later on the island of Capri. However, they were in truth three harlots who lured travelers to them and brought them to ruin; and from this comes the claim that they caused them to suffer shipwreck."

<gloss>4</gloss>. FROM THE PUNIC WORD SIR.] See Bochart *Chanaan*, Book I. chapters 18 and 27. <note>Samuel Bochart was a 17th-century scholar who attempted to trace Greek myths back to Phoenician/Punic origins.</note>

<gloss>5</gloss>. OVID.] In the 5th Book of his *Metamorphoses*.

<gloss>6</gloss>. INTO BIRDS.] From this it follows that the Poets describe them sometimes as birds, and other times as fish. They were, after

<column-break/>

their first transformation, half bird; and after the second, half fish. <note>This explains the transition from the classical Greek bird-like Siren to the medieval mermaid figure.</note>

<gloss>7</gloss>. THEIR HUMAN FACE AND VOICES.] Hyginus (a) relates this Fable in another way. According to him, the daughters of Achelous were changed into birds by Ceres because they had not come to the aid of her daughter Proserpina when Pluto abducted her. Their fate was that they would live only as long as some traveler or another was able to escape the charm of their voices. When Ulysses, therefore, traveled past them without stopping, they threw themselves into the sea and perished there. However, this Fable does not agree with the adventure had by Orpheus; who was so fortunate that he escaped the snares of the Sirens and achieved victory over them through the pleasant sound of his voice and lyre. Pausanias (b) says that the Sirens, encouraged by Juno, wished to have the honor of singing better than the Muses and dared to challenge them to a singing contest; but the Muses, having defeated them, plucked the feathers from their wings and made crowns out of them.

<column-break/>

-> (a) On the 5th Book of the *Aeneid*. (b) Fable 141. (c) Book IX. <-

# C E Y X
### A N D
# A L C Y O N E
## TRANSFORMED INTO KINGFISHERS.

->Then the path of the sea is safe: Aeolus guards the winds and restrains them from going out; and he provides a calm sea for his grandchildren. <note>original Latin: "Tum via tuta maris: ventos custodit, & arcet Aeolus egressu; praestatque nepotibus æquor."</note><-
->OVID, Metamorphoses Book 11.<-

---

Ceyx<sup>1</sup>, King of Trachis<sup>2</sup>, was married to the beautiful Alcyone, daughter of Aeolus<sup>3</sup>. Never did lovers adore one another more tenderly; they were always seen together, and nothing could part them but death. Meanwhile Ceyx, troubled by mournful omens since the death of his brother Daedalion and his niece Chione, made a resolution to rid himself of his anxious cares and to go consult the Oracle of Apollo at Claros. Alcyone, fearful for the life of her dear husband, spared no effort to advise him against that journey, or at the very least, to obtain permission from him that she might accompany him. Her tears, her sighs, her distress—all were in vain: he departed, after he had promised her that he would be home again in two months. But hardly had he lost sight of the shore when a terrible storm caused him to find death in the lap of the waves. Alcyone, meanwhile, performed her daily offerings to the Gods for his happy return. Juno, whose protection she especially sought, could no longer bear that prayers were addressed to her for someone who was no longer among the living; she gave an order to the God of Sleep to make the fate of her husband known to Alcyone through a dream. The Princess woke suddenly and ran in her despair to the seashore, to see at least once more the place from which Ceyx had departed. While she brought to memory all that had happened on the day they parted from each other, and, shedding a flood of tears, plucked out her hair and scratched her face, she saw something far off at sea, which... <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "ver-schrik-", likely "frightened" or "terrified" her.</note>

### O B S E R V A T I O N S.

<columns>2</columns>

1. <term>Ceyx</term>.] He has been passed off as the son of the Morning Star because his father was called *Phosphoros*; the name which the Greeks give to this star. In Latin, it is called *Lucifer*. <note>Lucifer literally means "Light-bringer" in Latin, referring to Venus as the morning star.</note>
2. <term>Trachis</term>.] A city of Thessaly.
3. <term>Aeolus</term>.] This is not Aeolus the God of the Winds, as Ovid says; but Aeolus the son of Hellen, who was born of Deucalion.
4. <term>Daedalion . . . Chione</term>.] <margin>(a) Ovid. Metam. XI.</margin> Daedalion was a very cruel and bloodthirsty Prince (a). He had a very beautiful daughter,

<column-break/>

of whom Mercury and Apollo both had use; the one by putting her to sleep with his snaked-staff <note>The Caduceus</note>, and the other deceiving her under the guise of an old woman. After nine months, she gave birth to Autolycus and Philammon. When Chione (for such was the name of the Princess) had the boldness to place her beauty above that of Diana, the Goddess pierced her tongue with an arrow, from which she died. Daedalion, out of despair over his daughter’s death, threw himself from a rock; but Apollo held him suspended in the air and transformed him into a Hawk.

<meta>This page continues from the previous accounts of mythological transformations, moving here to the famous story of Icarus and Daedalus. It provides both the poetic myth and a "Euhemerist" explanation—a historical interpretation where the supernatural elements (wings) are explained as misunderstood historical facts (the invention of sails).</meta>

# I C A R U S.

The young Icarus, now becoming bolder and wishing to see the heavens from nearby, left his guide and took his flight too high. The wax with which the feathers of his wings were fastened having become soft, he perceived that he could no longer hold himself up: in vain he moved his arms; in vain he called his father for help: he fell into the sea, which since that time has borne his name.

### EXPLANATION OF THE FABLE.

The history of Daedalus is so intertwined with that of Minos, Pasiphae, Ariadne, and Theseus, that we cannot relate it at length <gloss>1</gloss> without falling into a tediousness which we have intended to avoid. It shall be enough to say that Daedalus was an Athenian, of illustrious birth, and the first of his time in the arts of building and sculpture. A rivalry, unbecoming of such a great man as he was, had caused him to kill one of his nephews named Talos <gloss>2</gloss>, whom he had raised and whom he feared would surpass him in art; standing ready to be condemned to death by the Areopagus <note>The high supreme court of ancient Athens.</note>, he saved himself by taking flight to Crete, where he was received by Minos. It was here that he made that Labyrinth, so famous in Antiquity. Minos, having perceived that he had favored the love affairs of Pasiphae, had him put in prison. But he found opportunity to secretly construct two light vessels, one for himself and one for his son Icarus; and to protect himself from the pursuit of Minos's ships, which had to be rowed, he devised—seeing the wind favorable—to place a sail upon his own, which had not yet been done before him <note>This historical explanation suggests that the "wings" of the myth were actually the first sails, which allowed Daedalus to outrun rowed galleys.</note>. By this means he landed successfully: but Icarus, having been unable to steer his ship, suffered shipwreck and drowned; the current carried his body to an island near Samos, which at that time had no name. Hercules, being there by chance, recognized Icarus's body and committed it to the grave. Pausanias <gloss>3</gloss>, from whom we have drawn this explanation, adds that one still saw in his time, on a promontory in the Aegean Sea, a small piece of land where Icarus was buried. Since 그that time, the island and the sea which surrounds it have borne the name of the Icarian.

### REMARKS.

<columns>2</columns>

1. THAT WE CANNOT RELATE IT AT LENGTH ETC.] Regarding the history of Daedalus, one can read Apollodorus <gloss>(a)</gloss>, Ovid <gloss>(b)</gloss>, and especially Diodorus the Sicilian <gloss>(c)</gloss>.

<column-break/>

2. TALOS.] Ovid calls him *Perdix* <note>Latin for "Partridge".</note>, and says that Minerva changed him into a Partridge.
3. PAUSANIAS.] Book 9 <note>original: "Lib. IX"</note>.

<column-break/>

<header>XXXVII.</header>

# LEANDER
### AND
# HERO.

> *When bold Leander sought his sweet love,* <note>original: "Cum peteret dulces audax Leandrus amores, Et fessus tumidis jam premeretur aquis, Sic miser instantes affatus dicitur undas: Parcite, dum propero; mergite, dum redeo."</note>
> *And, exhausted, was already being overwhelmed by the surging waters,*
> *The poor soul is said to have addressed the encroaching waves thus:*
> *Spare me while I hasten; drown me when I return.*
->Martial, Epigrams on the Spectacles 25.<-

Sestos<gloss>1</gloss> and Abydos, built right across from each other on the banks of the Hellespont <note>The narrow strait in northwestern Turkey now known as the Dardanelles.</note>, were the dwelling places of the two young lovers, equally famous for their tender love and their tragic end. Although they were watched by their parents and separated by the narrowness of the strait, they nevertheless found a way to see each other. Love made them overcome the greatest obstacles. Whenever the sea was calm, Leander swam across at night to visit his beloved Hero, who did not fail to keep a torch burning on a high tower to serve as a beacon for her lover. For a time, the winds seemed to favor their secret meetings; the sea did not oppose their desires any more than was necessary to make them more fervent. But at last, that gentle calm ceased: a terrible storm caused the waves to swell with such violence that even the boldest sailors did not have the heart to pit themselves against their fury. Leander, meanwhile, remained constantly on the shore, keeping his gaze fixed on the opposite side where the object of his desire lived. After he, burning with impatience, had waited six days for the storm to subside, he made the decision to defy the wrath of the sea and the winds. Standing ready to throw himself into the sea, he addressed his prayer to Neptune <note>The Roman god of the sea.</note> and Boreas <note>The god of the North Wind.</note>:
"O Deities," he spoke, "who yourselves once felt all the powers of love, hear the prayers of the most tender lover: send the winds away to practice their fury in another place than this strait which separates me from my beloved. I ask no more of you than that I may see her just once more. Command the waves to spare me during my crossing; let them swallow me upon my return." The Gods...

<columns>2</columns>
1. SESTOS AND ABYDOS.] These were two cities on the banks of the Hellespont, the first in Europe and the other in Asia. Today, one sees two castles there, called the Dardanelles,
<column-break/>
of which one was named the New Castle of Natolia <note>Modern day Anatolia, Turkey</note>, and the other the New Castle of Europe or Rumelia <note>The historical name for the Balkan portion of the Ottoman Empire</note>. Leander was from Abydos, and Hero was from Sestos.

XXXVIII.

# THE NYMPH
# ECHO
## CHANGED INTO A VOICE.

> ->*Only her voice and her bones remain.*<-
> ->*They say her bones took the form of stone.*<-
> ->*Since then she hides in the woods, seen on no mountain;*<-
> ->*She is heard by all: it is sound, which lives in her.*<-
> -><note>original: "Vox tantum atque ossa supersunt. ossa ferunt lapidis traxisse figuram. Inde latet sylvis, nulloque in monte videtur; Omnibus auditur: sonus est, qui vivit in illa."</note> OVID. Metamorphoses, Book 3.<-

<image-desc>A large decorative initial 'D' containing a landscape scene with a figure, possibly Narcissus or Echo, near a body of water within a wooded setting.</image-desc>

THIS Nymph <note>Echo, a mountain nymph in Greek mythology</note>, who never speaks first, and cannot remain silent when others speak, fell in love with Narcissus. At that time, she was not a mere voice, as she is today; however, she did have the defect that she could only mimic the last words of what she heard spoken. Juno <note>The queen of the gods in Roman mythology</note> had brought her to this state. Whenever this Goddess sought to catch Jupiter <note>The king of the gods, husband of Juno</note> with one nymph or another, the clever Echo would delay her with a long speech, so that the nymphs would have time to escape. Juno, having noticed this trickery, said: "I shall ensure that from now on you will not have much use of that tongue, which has so often deceived me." The execution followed the threat; and since that time, she repeats only the last words that she hears spoken. 

When she then, at a certain time, encountered Narcissus <note>A youth known for his extraordinary beauty and eventual self-obsession</note> on the Hunt, she followed him without him being able to notice. Just as sulfur catches fire when held to a flame, so her heart caught fire in proportion to how she followed her Beloved. How many times did she wish to reveal her love to him, and to come quietly to his side! But that wretched punishment, which she had brought upon herself, prevented her from making a beginning with speaking. Forced to wait until he spoke himself, she held herself ready to at least answer him. Narcissus, having lost his way and wishing to call his companions, raised his voice in this manner: "Is there no one here with me?" *Me*, Echo answered him. "Come then to me," he continued. *To me*, Echo repeated. He looked all around him, and astonished to see no one, he cried out, "Why do you flee from me?" *Flee from me?* said the Nymph. He stood still at these words, and being continually deceived by the same voice, he began again: "Let us come together here." Echo, who could never have answered a more pleasant request, hurried to call out, *Come together!* She then emerged from the woods into the light to embrace him. But Narcissus, whose heart no nymph had ever been able to touch, wrenched himself from her arms, and taking flight, he said, "Do not hope that I shall surrender myself to you." She mimicked these words with a mournful voice, *Surrender myself to you*; and ashamed to see herself despised, she went into the
->deep-<- <note>The text continues on the following page.</note>

# NARCISSUS

## TRANSFORMED INTO A FLOWER.

> *Enthralled by the image of the beauty he beheld, he loves a bodiless hope: he takes for a body what is only a shadow, and stands spellbound by his own self.* <note>Original Latin: "Visæ correptus imagine formæ, Spem sine corpore amat: corpus putat esse quod umbra est, Ac stupet ipse sibi."</note>
> ->OVID *Metamorphoses* Book 3.<-

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'T' depicting a pastoral scene with a figure sitting beneath a tree in a landscape.</image-desc>

WHen Liriope¹ consulted the seer Tiresias <note>A famous blind prophet of Thebes in Greek mythology.</note> regarding the fate of her son Narcissus, she received the answer that he would reach a great age, provided that he did not see himself. For a long time, this prophecy seemed nonsensical; but the strange manner in which Narcissus lost his life, and his peculiar passion, proved it only too true. The unfortunate Echo was not the only one rejected by this young hunter; all the Nymphs had their share of his scorn as well. One of them, provoked by the foolish pride he took in his own beauty, cried out at a certain time, lifting her hands to heaven: "May he also love in the same way, and never enjoy that which he loves!" The Goddess of Vengeance <note>Nemesis, the deity who punished those who succumbed to hubris (arrogant pride).</note> heard and granted such a righteous prayer. Narcissus, coming from the hunt, approached a spring to quench his thirst. Captivated by his own image, which he saw in the water, he fell in love with it: he marveled at all his own features and felt himself consumed by a fire ignited by his own self. How often did he kiss that water in vain? How many times did he plunge his arms into it to embrace that delusion which constantly retreated before him? Fool that he is! He does not recognize himself in that faithful mirror. But even more foolish is he that, after having noticed his error, he still continues to love it. Nothing was able to draw him away from the spring; in contemplating himself, he forgot both sleep and food. Now he lay stretched out on the grass, unable to satisfy himself with the sight of that vain form which had led him astray. Then again, having raised himself up a little, he spoke to the surrounding woods, taking them as witness that he was tormented by a foolish love. Thus lamenting, he tore his clothes and beat his breast. But when he saw the marks of the blows he had given himself, he could no longer endure the intensity of his grief. Just as wax, when it is

# ARION
## SAVED BY A
# DOLPHIN.

> *Then (greater than belief) they say a dolphin placed its curved back under the strange burden. Sitting there, he holds his lyre and sings the price of his passage, soothing the ocean waters with his song. The Gods see these pious deeds: Jupiter received the dolphin among the stars and commanded it to have nine.* <note>Original Latin: "Inde (fide majus) tergo Delphina recurvo / Se memorant oneri ſuppoſuiſſe novo. / Ille ſedenś citharamque tenet, pretiumque vehendi / Cantat, & æquoreas carmine mulcet aquas. / Dî pia facta vident: aſtris Delphina recepit / Jupiter, & ſtellas juſſit habere novem."</note>
-> OVID, *Fasti*, Book 2. <-

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'A' featuring a landscape with a building and a tree.</image-desc>

Arion, a renowned poet and the premier Master of Song <note>The original Dutch 'Zangmeester' refers to a master musician or conductor of his era.</note> of his time, was a native of Methymne on the island of Lesbos. After having spent several years at the court of Periander, King of Corinth, he went to Italy and Sicily to practice his art; having enriched himself on this journey, he returned by ship from Tarentum to Corinth. Being on the open sea, the crew decided to throw him into the water in order to make themselves masters of his riches. He, perceiving their intention, offered them his treasures himself and begged only for the preservation of his life; but these barbarians, fearing they would be discovered, ordered him to throw himself into the sea or to kill himself if he wished to be buried on land. Brought to this extreme necessity, he requested their permission to sing some funeral dirges before he died. The crew, curious to hear the best Master of Song in the world, granted him his request. Arion, having put on his finest clothes, then took his lyre and sang with such a moving tone that the dolphins, lured by his pleasant rhythm, came swimming around the ship. When he had finished his song, he threw himself into the sea, where a dolphin took him upon its back and carried him as far as Cape Taenarum². From there, having come by land to Corinth, he recounted to the King the strange event by which he had saved his life. Periander, who could place no belief in his story, ordered that an eye be kept on him; and, as soon as the ship had come in,

1. <small>AFTER HAVING SPENT SEVERAL YEARS . . .]</small> This history is drawn almost word for word from Herodotus (a); and after him Pliny (b), Aulus Gellius (c), Lucian (d), and others have recorded it.
2. <small>AS FAR AS CAPE TAENARUM.]</small> Hyginus (e) adds to this that the dolphin died on the quicksand <note>In the sense of a sandbank or beach.</note> because Arion forgot to push it back into the sea; and that Periander had it buried and erected a monument for it. Some believe that this is the dolphin which has been placed among the constellations <note>The constellation Delphinus.</note>.

### REMARKS.

<columns>2</columns>
1. WERE DAUGHTERS OF PHORCYS AND CETO.] Here is what Hesiod says of it <note>original: "(a) Hesiodus"</note>: „daughters, Pephredo and Enyo,
» who came into the
» world already gray-haired, near the dwelling-place of Night; the same
» place where the Hesperides let the sweet sound of their voices
» be heard. The names of these Gorgons are Stheno, Eu-
» ryale, and Medusa, so famous for her misfortunes; the
» last was mortal, whereas her two Sisters were subject neither
» to old age nor to death".
Thus the Abbot Massieu translates this passage in his learned Treatise on the Gorgons <note>original: "(b) Mem. de l'Academ. des Inscript."</note>; which has provided us with a portion of the explanation of this Fable, and to which we wish to direct those who want to obtain a particular account of what the Poets and Historians have written on this subject.

2. PHORCYS.] Some have made a Sea-god of him, because he held dominion over islands and had made himself famous at sea. Furthermore, Hesiod gives the Father of the Gorgons the name of Gorgon.

3. CETO.] Her name, which comes very close to the word with which the Greeks <note>original: "(c) Kētos, meaning a whale or sea-monster"</note> name a whale, has caused her to pass for a sea-monster.

4. HAVING DESECRATED MINERVA’S TEMPLE WITH HER.] Thus Ovid recounts the cause of this punishment <note>original: "(d) Metamorphoses, Book 4."</note>. But Servius <note>original: "(e) In Aeneid, Book 6."</note> claims that it was because she had dared to place her hair above that of Minerva, and that the Goddess therefore changed it into snakes.

<column-break/>

5. THE HAIR OF THE GORGON INTO SNAKES.] If we may believe Aeschylus <note>original: "(f) In Prometheus Bound."</note>, all three Gorgons had their heads surrounded with snakes, and two wings on their shoulders, and killed men with their bare sight. His commentator adds that their teeth were as long as the tusks of a wild boar, and that they also had copper hands.

6. HIS WINGS.] According to the saying of some mythologists, it was the winged footwear of Pluto, and not of Mercury; and Perseus had borrowed them from the Nymphs who had them in their keeping. They also gave him the helmet of Pluto, and his bag, from which this Hero made a belt in which he enclosed the head of Medusa.

7. AND HIS SWORD.] This was a curved sword, like a sickle.

8. HAD LENT HER SHIELD.] Some say that she also lent him her mirror, and that it was this mirror, and not the shield, which Perseus used to receive the image of the Gorgon.

9. TWO OLDER SISTERS.] Aeschylus <note>original: "(g) In Prometheus Bound."</note> names three of them, and he adds that the three of them had only one eye and one tooth, which they used in turn.

# ARION.

[...fall], the sailors came before him, asking if they brought any news of Arion. The answer was that they had left him at Tarentum <note>A major Greek colony in Southern Italy, known today as Taranto.</note>, laden with honors and possessions. Arion then appeared before them, dressed in the same garments he had worn when they had cast him into the sea; they were betrayed by the dismay visible on their faces, so that, unable to conceal their crime, they were sentenced to death. To show his gratitude to the Gods who had preserved him, he erected a bronze statue in their honor at Tarentum, depicting a figure seated upon a dolphin.

### EXPLANATION OF THE FABLE.

The incident of the dolphin, the only fabulous <term>fabulous: here meaning "mythological" or "legendary," rather than "excellent"</term> circumstance of this history, can be explained by a very natural conjecture. It is probable that Arion, having been thrown into the sea by others, or having cast himself in close to the coast, had the good fortune to save himself by swimming; and that he, to win the esteem of others, spread the word that a dolphin had carried him on its back and brought him to land.
