M E D U S A. 107

[Perseus], who guarded the entrance, skillfully took possession of that eye at a time when one of them was lending it to her sister. Upon this, he successfully penetrated even into the Palace of the Gorgons without hindrance, traveling through winding paths filled with rocks and steep precipices. The figures of men and wild beasts, turned to stone by the sight of Medusa, made these places appear even more terrifying. Fortunately, at that time, the Gorgon and her snakes had fallen into a deep sleep. Perseus, guided by Minerva <note>The Roman goddess of wisdom and war, known to the Greeks as Athena.</note>, approached this dangerous maiden without looking at her directly. Having placed his shield in such a way that he could receive Medusa's image in the reflection, he took advantage of her sleep to strike off her head. From the blood that flowed from it, the horse Pegasus, Chrysaor<sup>10</sup> (the father of Geryon), and the many snakes with which Africa is so filled, were born. After his victory, the Hero placed this dreadful head in the shield of his sister Minerva, who used it to strike terror into her enemies.

# EXPLANATION OF THE FABLE.

We shall not attempt to untangle the Fable of the Gorgons. The obscurity and the constant contradictions which we encounter in the writers who deal with the subject<sup>11</sup> have led the most capable men to judge that the <meta>catchword: the</meta>

---
## N O T E S.
---

10. CHRYSAOR.] Hesiod (a) teaches us that he received his name from a golden sword <note>original: "gouden degen"</note> which he held in his hand at the moment of his birth; that he afterwards fell in love with Callirrhoe, the daughter of Ocean, and fathered Geryon upon her—that famous giant with three heads. Hyginus (b) says the same.

11. THE WRITERS WHO DEAL WITH THE SUBJECT.] To give an example of this, we shall recount what Diodorus Siculus and Pausanias say of it, who differ less from one each other than other historical writers. The first (c) begins with this observation: that in ancient times, Libya produced entire nations of the female sex who, through their warlike nature and bravery, were the wonder of the world. He proves this general statement with the example of the Gorgons, who, according to ancient traditions, waged a war against Perseus in which they acquitted themselves excellently through both their power and their courage. From this, he concludes that the power and bravery of these women must indeed have been very remarkable, because a Hero like Perseus—the bravest man among the Greeks of his time—regarded his expedition against them as the most difficult and greatest of his undertakings.

After these preliminary remarks, he proceeds to a particular account of everything concerning their history. The Gorgons and the Amazons, he says, were two bellicose nations of women, both living in Libya near Lake Tritonis. One can well imagine that many disputes occurred between them; they were women, and neighbors. It then happened that Myrina, Queen of the Amazons, raised a mighty army and took the field against the Gorgons, who approached her from their side with equal intrepidity. The two female nations came to blows and settled their differences through a battle. A gruesome slaughter took place there. But the Amazons achieved the victory; they slew a great multitude of their enemies and took more than three thousand prisoners. The remaining Gorgons escaped into the woods. Myrina ordered fire to be set to the woods, intending to exterminate the entire nation. However, as the wind did not favor her intention, she was forced to retreat to the borders of her own states. Meanwhile, the Amazons, puffed up by their victory, gave themselves over to celebrations; and because they kept a very careless watch during the night, the three thousand prisoners took advantage of the army's negligence. They seized the swords of these imprudent women—who imagined they had achieved a complete victory—and slew a very great number of them. But the Amazons, having assembled themselves again and surrounded the army of the Gorgons on all sides, fought like desperate people, and allowed themselves to be hacked to pieces. Myrina had three woodpiles prepared to burn her companions who had fallen on this occasion, and erected three monuments for them, of which some pieces were still seen in the time of Diodorus Siculus, and which were called the *Grave of the Amazons*. This writer adds that the Gorgons recovered from this great loss in the course of time, until King Perseus exterminated them at the time when they had Medusa as Queen. However, it was not this Hero who gave their power the final blow. That honor was reserved for Hercules, who, during his expedition in Libya, completely exterminated the Gorgons and Amazons, being certain that the great plan he had forged to promote the welfare of mankind would have only a very imperfect result if he allowed any nations to exist in the world that stood under the rule of women.

<column-break/>

Pausanias (d) tells us that some said Medusa was the daughter of Phorcys; that after her father's death, she held dominion over those peoples who lived around the Tritonis marsh; that they practiced hunting and she herself often went to war with the Libyans who were under her reign. He says that when Perseus had approached her at the head of that fine group of youths he had recruited in the Peloponnese, Medusa appeared before him in battle formation; that this Hero laid an ambush for her the following night, in which she fell; that the next day, having found her body on the spot, he was astonished by the beauty of this woman, cut off her head, and brought it to Greece to serve there as a spectacle. But, our historical writers continue, Procles (or Proclus) of Carthage, the son of Eucrates, speaks of it in such a manner as seems more probable to me. He says that in the wildernesses of Libya, one commonly saw beasts of an unusual shape and stature; that men and women there are wild, and like beasts of a strange sort; finally, that in his time, a Libyan was brought to Rome who differed so much from other men that everyone was amazed by him. For this reason, he believes that Medusa was one of those wild creatures who, while grazing her herds, wandered off to the Tritonis marsh, where, proud of the strength of her body, she wished to ill-treat the surrounding nations, who were finally delivered from this monster by Perseus. And that which gave occasion to believe that Perseus was helped by Minerva, he says further, is because this entire region is dedicated to Minerva, and the nations that inhabit it are under her protection.

->(a) In the place cited. (b) Fable 151. (c) Diodorus Siculus, Book 1. (d) Book II.<-

M E D U S A. 110

...the effect attributed to the shield of Minerva—namely, that it turned all who looked upon it into stone—refers to the terror that the head of Medusa, which was carved upon it, inspired in men.

Behold here a small sample of the conjectures which have been devised to explain the Fable of the Gorgons. It will now be time to say a word regarding Perseus, who plays such a significant role in this history. We shall, however, accompany him no further than the defeat of the Gorgons; the account of his other undertakings would lead us too far from our purpose. One may find a sufficient report of these by reading the mythographers, and especially Ovid, in the IVth and Vth books of his *Metamorphoses* <note>original: "Gedaentewiſſelingen." Ovid’s famous Roman poem detailing mythological transformations.</note>.

Akrisius, King of Argos, had an exquisitely beautiful daughter named Danaë. But when he went to consult the Oracle as to whether any sons would be born to him, he received the answer that he would have none; but rather, that he would lose his life at the hands of one of the sons who were to be born to Danaë ^14^. Terrified by this prophecy, he locked his daughter in an iron tower, or an underground chamber that was like an iron cage, taking every possible precaution to prevent her from having any contact with men. Jupiter, meanwhile, having fallen in love with the Princess, found a means to enter the tower through the roof by transforming himself into a Golden Rain, and thus made Danaë the mother of Perseus. Akrisius placed the mother and the son in a chest and threw it into the sea. After Danaë and the young Perseus had wandered upon the waves for a long time, they were found near the island of Seriphus (one of the Cyclades) by some fishermen, who presented them to Polydectes, the King of that island. This Prince took the education of Perseus upon himself; and when he saw him fit to bear arms, he advised him to undertake a war against the Gorgons, who had the reputation of being very wealthy. This undertaking succeeded according to his wishes, as we have seen in the Fable already related.

Through the "Jupiter" of whom we speak here, we must understand Pretus, the brother of Akrisius, who, according to the accounts of several writers, bore the surname of Jupiter ^15^. Having fallen in love with his niece, he bought the loyalty of her guards by means of gifts and was let into the prison through the roof; and from this, we have arrived at the concept of the Golden Rain. Akrisius, having heard of his daughter’s situation and fearing the fulfillment of the Oracle, had her sent out to sea with her son in a meager boat; but she had the good fortune to be saved, as we have seen.

---

## R E M A R K S.

<columns>2</columns>

14. WOULD LOSE HIS LIFE.] This prophecy was eventually verified. Perseus (a), returning as victor from all his travels, could not resist Akrisius's desire to see his grandson. He then went to meet him as far as Larissa. The Hero, then in the bloom of his years and quite pleased that he had invented the game of throwing the quoit <note>original: "worpsteen," a stone used in ancient athletics similar to a discus or shot put.</note>, wished to show a proof of his skill in this art in view of the entire city. However, misfortune would have it that, while throwing the stone with 

<column-break/>

all his might, he struck Akrisius, whom Fate had driven there, and who, struck by this blow, immediately gave up the ghost.

15. THE SURNAME OF JUPITER.] Gerard Vossius (b) <note>Gerardus Vossius (1577–1649), a renowned Dutch scholar and theologian.</note> himself believes that he is the one who must be understood as the Greek Jupiter. Others guess with sufficient probability that, in order to cover Danaë's shame, this incident was placed to Jupiter's account.

# M E D U S A.

...the effect attributed to *Minerva’s* shield—namely, that it turned all who looked upon it into stone—refers to the terror that the head of *Medusa*, which was carved upon it, instilled in people.

Here is an example of the conjectures that have been invented to explain the Fable of the *Gorgons*. It is now time to speak a word about *Perseus*, who plays such a large role in this history. We shall not accompany him further than the defeat of the *Gorgons*; the account of his other undertakings would lead us too far from our purpose. One can, however, find a sufficient report of this by reading the *fable-writers*, and especially *Ovid*, in the 4th and 5th books of his *Metamorphoses* <note>original: *Gedaentewisselingen*</note>.

*Acrisius*, King of *Argos*, had a daughter of perfect beauty called *Danaë*. But when he went to consult the Oracle as to whether any sons would be born to him, he received the answer: no; but that he would lose his life by one of the sons born to *Danaë* <note>original: *om 't leven koomen*</note><sup>14</sup>. Terrified by this prophecy, he locked his daughter in an iron tower, or in an underground chamber that was like an iron cage, taking every possible precaution to prevent her from all contact with men. *Jupiter*, meanwhile, having fallen in love with the Princess, found a way to enter the tower through the roof by changing himself into a golden Rain, and made *Danaë* the mother of *Perseus*. *Acrisius* placed the mother and the son in a chest <note>original: *koffer*</note> and threw it into the sea. When *Danaë* and the young *Perseus* had wandered upon the waves for a long time, they were found near the island of *Seriphos*, one of the *Cyclades*, by some fishermen, who presented them to *Polydectes*, the King of that island. This Prince took upon himself the education of *Perseus*; and, when he saw him fit to bear arms, he advised him to undertake the war against the *Gorgons*, who had the reputation of being very wealthy. This undertaking succeeded according to his wishes, as we have seen in the fable already related.

We must understand the *Jupiter* spoken of here as *Proetus* <note>transcribed as *Pretus*</note>, *Acrisius’s* brother, who, according to the accounts of several writers, bore the nickname of *Jupiter*<sup>15</sup>. Having fallen in love with his niece, he used gifts to bribe the loyalty of her guards and was let into the prison through the roof; and from this, the idea of the golden Rain arose. *Acrisius*, having heard of his daughter's situation and fearing the fulfillment of the Oracle, had her sent to sea with her son in a simple boat; but she had the luck to be saved, as we have seen.

The Hero, then in the bloom of his years and quite pleased that he had invented the game of throwing with a casting-stone <note>likely a discus or quoit</note>, wished to display a proof of his skill in this art before the eyes of the entire city; but misfortune would have it that, while throwing the stone with <column-break/> all his might, he hit *Acrisius*, whom his Fate had driven there, and, struck by this blow, he immediately gave up the ghost.

# MEDUSA.

## XLII.

...the effect attributed to Minerva's shield—namely, that it turned all who looked upon it into stone—is understood as the terror that the head of Medusa, which was carved upon it, aroused in men.

Behold here a sample of the conjectures that have been devised to explain the Fable of the Gorgons. It is now time to say a word about Perseus, who plays such a great role in this history. We shall, however, not follow him further than the defeat of the Gorgons; the account of his other undertakings would lead us too far from our purpose. One can, therefore, find a sufficient account in reading the Mythographers, and especially Ovid, in the 4th and 5th Books of his *Metamorphoses* <note>Ovid's "Metamorphoses" is a foundational Latin narrative poem describing the history of the world through the theme of transformation.</note>.

Acrisius, King of Argos, had a perfectly beautiful daughter named Danaë. But when he went to consult the Oracle as to whether any sons would be born to him, he received the answer: no; but that he would lose his life by one of the sons who were to be born of Danaë ^14. Terrified by this prophecy, he locked his daughter in an iron tower, or in an underground chamber that was like an iron cage, taking every possible precaution to prevent her from having any contact with men. Jupiter, meanwhile, having fallen in love with the Princess, found a way to enter the tower through the roof by transforming himself into a golden Rain, and made Danaë the Mother of Perseus. Acrisius placed the Mother and the Son in a chest and threw it into the sea. When Danaë and the young Perseus had wandered upon the waves for a long time, they were found near the island of Seriphos, one of the <term>Cyclades</term> <note>A group of Greek islands in the Aegean Sea, arranged in a circle around the sacred island of Delos.</note>, by some fishermen, who presented them to Polydectes, King of that island. This Prince took the education of Perseus upon himself; and when he saw him capable of bearing arms, he advised him to undertake a war against the Gorgons, who had the reputation of being very wealthy. This undertaking succeeded according to his wishes, as we have seen in the Fable already related.

We must, through the Jupiter spoken of here, understand Proetus <note>original: "Pretus"</note>, Acrisius' brother, who, according to the accounts of several writers, bore the nickname of Jupiter ^15. Having fallen in love with his niece, he bought the loyalty of her guards by means of gifts and was let into the prison through the roof; and from this, people arrived at the idea of the golden Rain. Acrisius, having heard of his daughter's circumstance and fearing the fulfillment of the Oracle, had her sent into the sea with her son in a meager boat; but she had the good fortune to be saved, as we have seen.

### REMARKS.

14. **WOULD LOSE HIS LIFE.**] This prophecy was later proven true. Perseus (a), returning as victor from all his travels, could not resist Acrisius' desire to see his grandson. He then went to meet him as far as Larissa. The Hero, then in the bloom of his years and in high spirits because he had invented the game of throwing with the <term>discus</term> <note>original: "worpsteen"; literally a "throwing stone," referring to the ancient athletic event of the discus throw.</note>, wanted to show a proof of his skill in this art in the sight of the whole city; but misfortune would have it that, throwing the stone with 

<column-break/>

all his might, he hit Acrisius, whom his Destiny had driven there, and, struck by this blow, he immediately gave up the ghost.

15. **THE NICKNAME OF JUPITER.**] Gerard Vossius (b) himself believes that he is the one who must be understood as the Greek Jupiter. Others guess with sufficient probability that, to cover Danaë's shame, this incident was placed to Jupiter's account.

<header>XLII. ANDROMEDA.</header>

# ANDROMEDA.

> *Andromeda had been surrendered to the sea monsters:*
> *This same woman was the noble wife of Perseus.*
<note>original Latin: "Andromede monstris fuerat devota marinis: Hæc eadem Persei nobilis uxor erat."</note>
->PROPERTIUS Book 2. Elegy 21.<-

---

When Cassiopeia <gloss>1</gloss>, on account of her beauty, had dared to exalt herself above the Nereids <gloss>2</gloss>, these nymphs were so offended that they caused a terrible monster to emerge from the sea, which filled the entire coast with terror through its destruction. In this distress, the inhabitants took refuge in the Oracle of Jupiter Ammon <note>A famous oracle in the Libyan desert where the Greek and Egyptian gods Zeus and Amun were worshipped as one</note>, who gave them the command to sacrifice a young maiden every year to the fury of that monster, in order to appease the wrath of the gods. They obeyed that command. Many victims, chosen by lot, had already been slaughtered for the welfare of the people; when Andromeda, the daughter of Cepheus and Cassiopeia, was herself forced to atone for her mother’s crime. Her high birth seemed indeed to exempt her from the decree of Fate; but the gods insist upon being obeyed. She was then brought to the shore and bound to a rock. While the terrifying form of the monster approached to devour her, Perseus <note>The hero and son of Zeus, famous for slaying the Gorgon Medusa</note> arrived; it seemed he almost forgot to beat his wings in the air as he gazed upon her. "Beautiful Princess," he said, approaching her, "those chains were not made for you: you should wear no others than those which bind the hearts of lovers together. I pray you, tell me your name, from whence you come, and why I see you struck in iron shackles?" Shame prevented Andromeda from speaking: her tears alone expressed her sorrow. But as Perseus strongly pressed her for an answer, she finally told him the cause of her misfortunes. She was still speaking when a monster was seen darting toward her from the midst of the waves. At that sight, Andromeda gave a great cry. Her father and mother, who were present at this sad spectacle, shed their tears, and embracing her, gave her their last farewell. Perseus, struck with pity, offered to release her and requested her as his bride. The King and Queen accepted this proposal with joy and promised, to encourage him further, to give their kingdom as a dowry for their daughter. Then the hero, having pushed off the ground with his feet, rose into the air: he showed to the

<meta>The text continues the narrative of Perseus and Andromeda, specifically focusing on the battle with the sea monster and the subsequent conflict with Andromeda’s former suitor, Phineus, before transitioning into historical and rationalist interpretations of the myth.</meta>

<page-num>112</page-num>
<header>A N D R O M E D A.</header>
<sig>XLIII. AT-</sig>

Perseus showed the monster the terrifying shield <note>This refers to the Aegis, the shield bearing the face of the Gorgon Medusa, which could turn onlookers to stone</note> and, throwing himself upon the beast, pierced it several times with his sword. As soon as the monster gave up the ghost, the entire shore echoed with joy. Cepheus and Cassiopeia ran to meet the victor, acknowledging him as their protector and son-in-law; and the Princess, who was the moving cause of the undertaking, became the prize and reward of the victor.

They were already busy celebrating the wedding feast when Phineus <note>3</note>, to whom Andromeda had been betrothed, suddenly fell upon them with a great number of armed men. Perseus had only a small number of friends with him; but full of a noble confidence, he went to meet these reckless guests, not deigning to use any but common weapons against them. However, seeing that bravery was about to yield to numbers, he spoke thus: "Since you force me, I shall take refuge in my female enemy <note>referring to Medusa, whose head he carried</note>. You, who fight for me, turn away your eyes." Having finished these words, he showed the deadly head, and at that very moment, Phineus's soldiers were turned into stone. Phineus, amazed and trembling at this terrible sight, begged the victor for mercy: but his prayer was in vain; and he had to undergo the same fate as the others.

### EXPLANATION OF THE FABLE.

The coasts <note>4</note> of Cepheus's kingdom were being devastated by a prince or a pirate. To make him cease his robberies, Andromeda was promised to him in marriage. However, Perseus gave him battle, killed him, and received the Princess as a reward for his victory. This is the historical basis for this Hero’s struggle against the monster. Phineus, who had not succeeded in exterminating the pirate, then attempted to oppose the marriage of his rival; but because the fear inspired by Perseus's victories forced him to abandon his claims, the story was spread that this Hero had turned him into stone.

Some maintain that the prow of the pirate's ship bore the image of a whale or another sea monster, and that this gave rise to the idea of the beast; but others say that Phineus himself was that monster. Finally, there are writers who guess that it was a certain wild animal that caused great destruction, from which Perseus delivered the land.

As for the rest, if one may believe Josephus <note>5</note>, Pliny <note>6</note>, Strabo <note>7</note>, and Pomponius Mela <note>8</note>, it was not in Ethiopia that this event occurred, but on the coasts of Syria, near Joppa, or Jaffa.

He adds that it was 40 feet long; and that from the ribs,

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Bellerophon's exploits in Lycia, following his departure from Argos. It maintains the 18th-century mythographic style common in classical reference works.</meta>

# B E L L E R O P H O N.

He entertained him for nine days, sacrificing a bull to the Gods each day to thank them for his happy arrival. On the tenth day, he asked him for the letter written to him by the King, his son-in-law; no sooner was this read than he gave Bellerophon the command to go and kill a terrifying monster called the Chimera, which was not of human but of divine descent. It had the head of a Lion, the tail of a Dragon, and the body of a Goat; and it spewed a cloud of fire and flames from its gaping maw. The Gods, who protect innocence and wisdom, did not abandon Bellerophon in an undertaking of such great danger, and sent him the horse Pegasus <note>The famous winged horse of Greek mythology.</note>. The young Hero, mounted upon this winged Steed, fought the Chimera and killed it. Subsequently, he overcame the warlike Solymi <note>A fierce people inhabiting the mountains of Lycia.</note>, and, moving from there against the bellicose Amazons, he likewise defeated them.

The King of Lycia, seeing that Bellerophon’s bravery triumphed over all these dangers, and remaining fixed in his intent to bring about his death, chose some of the bravest and most undaunted Lycians and laid an ambush against him. But they never returned home; the brave Bellerophon slew them all. Iobates, perceiving from these great heroic deeds that he was of the lineage of the Gods, kept him with him and gave him his daughter Philonoë in marriage, with half of his Kingdom as a dowry. The Lycians, following the example of their Prince, gave him a large estate in ownership, which contained a very beautiful vineyard and fertile farmlands. However, Anteia <note>Also known as Stheneboea, the woman who falsely accused him.</note>, out of spite that she had been unable to take her revenge, ended her days by poison. Bellerophon had three children by Philonoë: Isander, Hippolochus, and Laodamia. Jupiter, having fallen in love with Laodamia, made her the mother of the brave Sarpedon.

After so many excellent deeds, Bellerophon undertook to fly to heaven. But Pegasus, feeling himself stung by a wasp which Jupiter had sent to punish that presumption, threw him down into a plain of Cilicia, where, deprived of his sight and all human help, he found death only after long wandering and leading a miserable life.

---

### EXPLANATION OF THE FABLE.

It is said that the Solymi are compared to Lions because of their bravery; the Amazons to Goats, because they inhabited high and inaccessible places; and the Lycians to Serpents, on account of their military ruses and the ambushes they laid for travelers. From the qualities of these three nations, conquered by Bellerophon, the Poets forged the Chimera.

Others have believed that it was a famous Pirate, whose ship, called the Chimera, had the form of a Goat, and on its stern a Serpent, and

<meta>Catchword: and</meta>

---

### O B S E R V A T I O N S.

8. <term>It had the head of a Lion.</term>] Hesiod (<italic>a</italic>) <note>original: "Theog. vf. 321, 322"</note> adds this description: that it had three heads, one of a Lion, one of a Goat, and one of a Dragon; that it was the daughter of Typhon and Echidna; and the Mother of the Sphinx, the Nemean Lion, and of Ceto.

9. <term>They sent him the Horse Pegasus.</term>] Pausanias (<italic>b</italic>) <note>original: "Lib. II"</note> speaking of Minerva *Chalinitis* <note>The Bridler; original: χαλινὸς (chalinos), meaning bridle or bit.</note>, says that this surname is given to her because she had helped Bellerophon more than all the other deities; and that she, among other

<column-break/>

things, presented him with the Horse Pegasus, after she herself had taken the trouble to tame it and to ride it with a bridle. Others say that it was the Muses who, at Neptune's request, gave Pegasus to Bellerophon as a loan.

10. <term>The warlike Solymi.</term>] The Solymi (<italic>c</italic>) <note>original: "Mad. Dacier, sur le 6. Livre l'Iliade"</note> were peoples who lived near Termessus on the Meander: they were formerly called Cabali. In Strabo's time, one could still see near that place the valley of Bellerophon and the grave of his Son *Isander*, who fell in the battle.

# AMPHION
### BUILT THE CITY BY THE SOUND OF HIS LYRE:
## T H E B E S.

> *Amphion also, the founder of the Theban citadel, was said to move stones by the sound of his lyre, and by soft prayer to lead them wherever he wished.* <note>original: "Dictus & Amphion Thebanæ conditor arcis / Saxa movere sono testudinis, & prece blandâ / Ducere quò vellet."</note>
> — HORACE, *The Art of Poetry*

---

Antiope, daughter of Nycteus, Governor of the Boeotians, was famous throughout Greece for her rare beauty <gloss>1</gloss>. Jupiter fell in love with her, and having seduced her while in the form of a Satyr, the Princess—fearing her father's wrath—fled to Sicyon. Nycteus, having died shortly thereafter, had Laomedon, King of the Sicyonians, lead her back to Boeotia; but he was forced to stop on Mount Cithaeron along the way, where she brought Amphion and Zethus into the world. When Lycus—to whom Nycteus had left the governance of his kingdom upon his death—saw himself in possession of Antiope's fate, he cast her into a dark prison under the custody of his wife Dirce, and left the twins to be exposed <note>A common mythological trope where infants are left in the wild to die, only to be miraculously saved.</note>. They had the good fortune to be found by shepherds, who kept them alive. The Muses <term>Muses (Zanggodinnen)</term>, at Jupiter's request, took charge of their education; and Mercury, wishing to awaken the gifts Amphion had for the musical arts, presented him with a lyre of his own invention, and did not consider himself too noble to teach him how to play it himself. These young Princes, having grown up and finding in themselves no less bravery than skill, undertook to avenge the wrongs done to their mother by Lycus and Dirce. Followed by several bands of men recruited in Boeotia, they rescued Antiope; put Dirce to death <gloss>2</gloss> by tying her to the tail of a wild bull; defeated Lycus, and made themselves masters of the kingdom. Thebes felt the effects of the wildness of its inhabitants: at that time it was nothing more than a heap of huts, scattered here and there, and the city (if one may give it that name) had no walls to protect it against the attacks of its enemies. Amphion, trusting in the help of the Muses, flattered himself [with the hope]...

---

-> N O T E S <-

<columns>2</columns>

<margin>(a) Pausanias, Book 2.</margin>
<gloss>1. BECAUSE OF HER RARE BEAUTY.]</gloss> Her outstanding beauty was the reason people attributed a divine origin to her, considering her <gloss>(a)</gloss> to be a daughter of the River Asopus, which ran through the territory of Plataea and Thebes.

<margin>(b) Hyginus, Fable 7.</margin>
<gloss>2. PUT DIRCE TO DEATH etc.]</gloss> Bacchus later changed her into a spring <gloss>(b)</gloss> as a reward for her having honored him as one of his Bacchantes <note>Followers of the wine-god Bacchus, known for their ecstatic rituals.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the rationalized interpretation of the myth of Bellerophon, moving from the literal monster-slaying to historical and geographical explanations of the Chimera, as well as the hero's eventual tragic downfall.</meta>

# BELLEROPHON.

...and had a Lion on the prow. They add that Bellerophon, when he overcame this Pirate, boarded a ship which either bore the name Pegasus or had the form of a winged Horse.

It would be easy to bring forward many other explanations of this Fable; but we prefer to dwell upon that which seems most probable to us.

There was in Phaselis <gloss>11</gloss>, a region of Lycia, a Mountain called Chimera, which was full of Lions, Snakes, and wild Goats, and from time to time cast out flames <note>The "Eternal Fires" of Chimaera (Yanartaş) in modern Turkey, where natural gas vents through the rocks and burns constantly.</note>. The Poets have made a Monster of it, which had something of each of these animals; and spread the word that it was slain by Bellerophon because he destroyed these wild beasts and made the Mountain inhabitable. Others <gloss>12</gloss> understand by the three heads of the Chimera the three peaks of the Mountain, which roughly had the shape of the heads of these animals: they also believed that Bellerophon was only held to be the Victor over this supposed Monster because he had driven out the peoples who inhabited this Mountain; not alone, as the Poets claim, but at the head of an army provided by Iobates.

The fall of Bellerophon is a matter which teaches us that he, puffed up by his good fortune, involved himself in a reckless undertaking which was the cause of his ruin. Homer, from whom we have taken the greatest part of Bellerophon's History, does not mention the crime of which this Prince had made himself guilty. See here all that he says of it:

> "After Bellerophon had brought the hatred of the Gods upon himself, he gave himself over to such a heavy melancholy that he wandered alone through wild and lonely places, gnawing at his heart and avoiding the meeting of men. For the God Mars, insatiable for defeats and murders, slew his Son Isander in a battle against the Solymi; and Diana killed the Princess Laodamia with her arrows. Thus of Bellerophon’s three children, no one remained but Hippolochus <gloss>13</gloss>."

With this story, Homer wished to let us know that Bellerophon, out of grief and despair over the loss of his children, withdrew from the company of men and finally died of sadness.

One has seen in the History of Perseus and the Gorgons what one must understand by the wings given by the Poets to this Hero. The Horse Pegasus represents here the speed of the ship, or rather, of the Horse of Bellerophon; and it was said to be winged for no other reason than that one must have, so to speak, wings to climb the top of Mount Chimera.

---

<column-break/>

who is of the opinion that the three heads of the Chimera signify the three leaders of the Solymi, whose names mean the same as those of these animals.

# NIOBE
## TURNED INTO A ROCK.

---

> Even within her marrow there is stone. 
> Yet she weeps, and caught up in a powerful whirlwind
> She was carried back to her homeland, where, fixed upon a mountain peak,
> She dissolves; and even now the marble drips with tears.
<note>original Latin: "Intra quoque viscera saxum est. / Flet tamen, & validi circumdata turbine venti / In patriam rapta est, ubi fixa cacumine montis / Liquitur; & lacrymis etiam nunc marmora manant." — Ovid, Metamorphoses, Book 6.</note>
-> OVID. Metamorphoses Book 6. <-

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut drop cap 'N' featuring ornamental foliage and scrollwork.</image-desc>
Niobe ¹ was the most fortunate of all women, had she only known how to use her happiness with moderation. Born of illustrious blood, the wife of a Prince whose head was crowned with a Diadem <term>diadem: a jeweled headband worn as a symbol of sovereignty</term>, all of this served only to feed her pride. However, she did not flatter herself with these advantages as much as she did with her fertility. Seeing herself the proud Mother of fourteen children ², she dared to exalt herself above Latona ³, even despising her, who had no more than two. But it was not long before she felt the punishment her arrogance deserved. At a certain time, while her sons were practicing their horsemanship in a plain, Apollo shot them all through with his arrows; and Diana caused the young Princesses to perish ⁴ in the same manner, while they were dressed in mourning robes and, with loosened hair, were bewailing the death of their brothers. These miserable victims lay wallowing in the dust and their own blood for nine days, without anyone committing their corpses to the earth. For their unhappy Father had pierced his own breast with a sword, and Latona’s son <note>Apollo</note> had turned all the inhabitants of Thebes into stone; but on the tenth day, the Gods themselves buried them. Niobe was carried by a whirlwind to the summit of Mount Sipylus; and there, although turned into a rock as an eternal monument to the vengeance of the Gods, she still continually sheds her tears.

### R E M A R K S.

1. NIOBE.] She was the wife of Amphion, daughter of Tantalus, and sister of Pelops. One must not confuse her with another Princess of the same name, the daughter of Phoroneus, King of Argos, who was beloved by Jupiter.
2. MOTHER OF FOURTEEN CHILDREN.] Seven sons: *Sipylus, Minytus, Ismene, Damasichthon, Agenor, Phaedimus*, and *Tantalus*; and as many daughters: namely, *Ethodea* or *Thera, Cleodoxa, Astyochea, Phthia, Pelopia, Astycratia* and *Ogygia.* These names are not the same in all writers. The number of Niobe’s children is also uncertain. Homer gives her only twelve; and Hesiod increases the number to twenty.
3. EXALTED ABOVE LATONA.] See the 24th Book of Homer’s Iliad, and the 6th Book of Ovid’s Metamorphoses, which recounts this Fable at great length.

<column-break/>

4. PERISH IN THE SAME MANNER.] Homer says explicitly (*a*), that all the children of Niobe were killed. Pausanias, who is of the same opinion as Homer, nevertheless speaks (*b*) of two daughters of Niobe, named *Meliboea* and *Amycla*, who, according to some, escaped Latona's vengeance because they had prayed to her for mercy. They add that Meliboea was so terrified by the wrath of Apollo and Diana that she had a pale color for the rest of her life; for which reason her name was changed to Chloris (*c*), which signifies paleness.

---

-> (*a*) *Iliad.* Book 24. verse 609. (*b*) Book 2. (*c*) From the Greek word *chloros* <note>original: χλωρὸς</note>, pale. <-

> *Inside her vitals, too, there is stone. Niobe^1 was the most fortunate of all women, if only she had known how to use her happiness with moderation. Born of illustrious blood, the wife of a Prince whose head was adorned with a Diadem <note>A royal headband or crown.</note>, all of this served as fuel for her pride. Yet she did not flatter herself with these advantages as much as she did with her fertility. She dared, being proud because she saw herself as the Mother of fourteen children^2, to exalt herself above Latona^3, even despising her, who had no more than two. But it was not long before she felt the punishment that her arrogance deserved. At a certain time, while her sons were practicing horsemanship in a plain, Apollo pierced them all with his arrows; and Diana caused the young Princesses—while they were dressed in mourning robes and, with disheveled hair, lamented the death of their brothers—to perish in the same manner^4. These wretched victims lay wallowing in the dust and their blood for nine days, without anyone committing their corpses to the earth. For their unhappy Father had pierced his own breast with a sword, and Latona’s son [Apollo] had turned all the inhabitants of Thebes into stone; but on the tenth day, the Gods themselves buried them. Niobe was carried by a whirlwind to the top of Mount Sipylus; and there, although turned into a rock as an eternal monument to the vengeance of the Gods, she still continually sheds her tears.

1. NIOBE.] She was the wife of Amphion, daughter of Tantalus, and Sister of Pelops. One must not confuse her with another Princess of the same name, the daughter of Phoroneus, King of Argos, who was beloved by Jupiter.
2. MOTHER OF FOURTEEN CHILDREN.] Seven sons: *Sipylus, Minytus, Ismene, Damasichthon, Agenor, Phaedimus*, and *Tantalus*, and as many daughters: such as, *Ethodea or Thera, Cleodoxa, Astyochea, Phthia, Pelopia, Astykratia* and *Ogygia*. These names are not the same among all writers. The number of Niobe’s children is also uncertain. Homer gives her only twelve; and Hesiod increases the number to twenty.
3. EXALTED ABOVE LATONA.] See the 24th Book

They add that Meliboea was so terrified by the wrath of Apollo and Diana that she had a pale color for her entire life; for which reason her name was changed to *Chloris* (c), which signifies paleness.

<meta>This page continues the mythological sequence of Niobe, moving from the poetic narrative to a rationalist explanation of the fable. The text seeks to ground the supernatural elements—divine arrows and transformations—in historical and natural phenomena like plagues and geological formations.</meta>

# N I O B E.
XLVII. FI- <note>This is a signature mark, used by early printers to help binders arrange the pages of a book in the correct order.</note>

### EXPLANATION OF THE MYTH.

One has seen in the Explanation of the Myth of Amphion how this Prince, with the help of his brother Zethus, made himself master of the Kingdom of Thebes. A few years later, a violent plague ^5, which devastated Boeotia <note>A region in central Greece where Thebes was the primary city.</note>, caused all of Amphion and Niobe’s children to perish. Since infectious diseases were attributed to the influence of the stars and to the vapors drawn out of the earth by the excessive heat of the sun, it was imagined that Apollo and Diana had shot Niobe’s children with arrows. And because this Princess possibly boasted of her own fertility, the circumstance of the disdain with which she was said to have treated Latona <note>Mother of the twin gods Apollo and Diana; Niobe’s hubris lay in claiming her fourteen children were superior to Latona’s two.</note>—which accords very well with the rest of the Myth—was published. Furthermore, clever critics have everywhere noted that poets commonly attribute the sudden death of men to Apollo, especially when it is caused by infection; and the death of women to Diana. 

The transformation of Niobe into a rock teaches us, in a figurative way, that grief made her mute and motionless; which is the hallmark of great sorrow. After the loss of her children, gaining a dislike for her residence in Thebes, she went to spend her days at Sipylus ^6, her fatherland. There was a rock on the mountain of the same name which from a distance strongly resembled ^7 a woman burdened with grief. This was enough to make the poets say that Niobe, changed into a rock, had been carried there by a whirlwind, and that she still wept for her children there. Following Homer, we have said that they lay unburied for nine days because Apollo had turned all the inhabitants of Thebes into stones, and that at last the Gods themselves took upon themselves the care of their burial. That is to say, since these Princes had died of the plague, the priests were forced to commit them to the earth because no one dared to pay them the final respects; and that the Thebans, concerned only for their own safety, seemed very little touched by the misfortunes of their Queen.

---

### N O T E S.

5. A PLAGUE etc.] Pausanias (a) <note>original: "Lib. IX." (Book 9)</note> assures us that the plague swept away Amphion’s entire household. He adds that Zethus died of grief due to the death of his son, who was killed by his own mother. 
6. SIPYLUS.] A city bordering Phrygia <note>An ancient kingdom in the west-central part of Anatolia, modern-day Turkey.</note>.
7. WHICH FROM A DISTANCE STRONGLY RESEMBLED etc.] Pausanias (b) <note>original: "Lib. I." (Book 1)</note> has provided us with this explanation: "On a <column-break/> certain day," he says, "I climbed Mount Sipylus, solely to see that Niobe of whom so much is spoken. The Rock called by that name is close by. Everything that is true of it shows that, viewed from nearby, it does not resemble a woman in the slightest; but if one sees it from afar, it indeed seems to be a woman sitting, weeping, and burdened with grief."

->(a) Book IX. (b) Book I.<-

<summary>This page provides a rationalist interpretation of the Niobe myth, explaining the divine arrows as a metaphor for a plague and her transformation as a symbol of paralyzing grief. It identifies the "rock" of Niobe as a real geological formation on Mount Sipylus described by the ancient traveler Pausanias.</summary>
<keywords>Niobe, Apollo, Diana, plague, Mount Sipylus, Pausanias, rationalization, Greek mythology, Thebes, Boeotia</keywords>

# PHINEUS

# H A R P I E S

## D E L I V E R E D.

> *No monster is more dismal than these, nor does any fiercer plague or wrath of the Gods raise itself from the Stygian waves. They have the faces of birds like virgins, a most foul discharge from their bellies, hooked hands, and faces always pale with hunger.* <note>original: "Tristius haud illis monstrum est, nec sævior ulla / Pestis & ira Deûm Stygiis sese extulit undis. / Virginei volucrum vultus, fædissima ventris / Proluvies, uncæque manus, & pallida semper / Ora fame."</note>
->VIRGIL, Aeneid, Book 3.<-

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'F' containing a detailed woodcut illustration of a landscape with classical figures, possibly representing the story of Phineus and the Harpies.</image-desc>

Phineus<gloss>1</gloss>, Son of Agenor, King of the Phoenicians, and brother of Cadmus and the beautiful Europa, was a Prince renowned for the gift of prophecy granted to him by Apollo. After he had left his father's house to search for his sister Europa, who had been abducted by Jupiter, he finally arrived at the Thracian Bosphorus <note>The text uses "Fosforus," an archaic spelling for the Bosphorus strait</note>. As the sea prevented him from traveling further, he stayed there and took Cleopatra<gloss>2</gloss>, daughter of Boreas <note>The North Wind</note>, as his wife; with whom he fathered two children, Plexippus and Pandion. However, he later cast her aside to marry Idaea, daughter of Dardanus. This Princess, who bore a deadly hatred for the sons of the first wife, accused them of attempting to dishonor her; whereupon Phineus, all too easily believing this slander, had their eyes put out. But Jupiter did not leave his crime unpunished, robbing Phineus of his sight and abandoning him to the Harpies<gloss>3</gloss>. Never had there been...
<meta>catchword: a more...</meta>

---

### R E M A R K S.

<columns>2</columns>

1. **PHINEUS.**] He was King of Bithynia, or Paphlagonia.
2. **CLEOPATRA.**] Or Cleobula; *Calaïs, Zethes, Echion*, and *Cleopatra* were the offspring of the marriage of Boreas with Orithyia.
3. **HARPIES.**] This word comes from the Greek ἁρπάζω <note>transliterated: harpazo</note>, which means *to seize* or *to snatch*. Usually three are named: *Celaeno, Aëllo*, and *Ocypete*. However, Hesiod ($b$) speaks only of the last two, whom he says were sisters of Iris, and daughters of Thaumas and Electra, daughter of Oceanus. Some add a fourth, whom they call *Thyalle*. Others consider them daughters of Cocytus <note>A river in the underworld</note> and place them in the same rank as the Furies

<column-break/>

or Goddesses of Vengeance. According to their opinion, when the Gods sent the Harpies to punish Phineus, they made them rise from hell; and gave them freedom to remain on earth and torment the wicked: by which the Poets intended to signify the remorses of a guilty conscience. For the rest, Celaeno was indeed the foremost, or the Queen of the Harpies: as evidenced by these verses from Virgil ($c$):

> *What the almighty Father foretold to Phoebus, and Phoebus Apollo to me, I, the greatest of the Furies, reveal to you.* <note>original: "Quæ Phæbo pater omnipotens, mihi Phæbus Apollo / Prædixit, vobis Furiarum ego maxima pando."</note>

<note>(a) Servius, on Aeneid Book III. (b) Theogony, line 267. (c) Aeneid Book III, lines 251, 252.</note>

<page-num>XLVIII.</page-num>

### THE
# GOLDEN FLEECE
### CAPTURED BY
## J A S O N.

---

->By Gold<-
->*The Aesonian hero gains possession.*<- <note>original: "Auro Heros Æsonius potitur." Jason is referred to here as the "Aesonian hero" because he was the son of King Aeson of Iolcus. Quote from Ovid’s Metamorphoses, Book 7.</note>
->OVID. Met. 7.<-

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial letter 'D' showing a small landscape scene with classical architectural ruins and trees.</image-desc>

THE Argonauts, after a long journey full of terrors and dangers, finally arrived with their leader Jason at the banks of the Phasis <margin>1.</margin>. Having disembarked, they presented themselves before Aeetes, King of Colchis, and requested from him the Golden Fleece, which was the purpose of their journey. While this Prince set before them the great dangers involved in capturing this rich treasure, his daughter Medea felt her heart ignited with a violent love for Jason. She tried with all her might to smother this passion in its birth, and she might perhaps have succeeded, had Jason not met her in a dark forest where she was making an offering to Hecate <note>The goddess of magic, witchcraft, and the night.</note>. It was here that this Prince, throwing himself at her feet, entreated her to lend him a helping hand in his undertaking, with the promise that, if she became his wife, he would remain eternally faithful to her. Medea could not resist the sight of her lover. She promised to lend him a helping hand and gave him some enchanted herbs, through whose power he would overcome the bulls and dragons that guarded the precious prize he had come for. When the King and the people were gathered together on the appointed day to witness the struggle, the bulls with bronze feet <note>The text says "koperen," meaning copper or bronze; these are the Khalkotauroi, mythical automatons created by Hephaestus.</note>, breathing fire and flame—which caused the grass and herbs in the surrounding field to wither—were seen to come forth. Jason, instructed by Medea and relying on the power of his magic herbs, approached them with a bold step, despite their terrifying lowing, without the exhaled fire causing him any harm. He stroked them for some time with his hand to tame them; and when he had succeeded in placing the yoke upon them, he forced them to plow a field where the plowshare had never been. As soon as the fields were prepared, Jason sowed dragon's teeth into them, with

### *R E M A R K S.*

sprinkled with a magic potion: these, softened by the moisture of the earth, brought forth armed soldiers in an instant. Jason, seeing these bands coming at him with javelins in hand, threw a stone among that raging crowd; and immediately thereafter they were seen turning their weapons against one another; and they all perished in a mutual battle. Now there was nothing left for Medea’s lover but to subdue the Dragon, which ceaselessly guarded the Golden Fleece. This monster had a terrifying crest on its head, and in its maw, armed with sharp teeth, were seen three serpent tongues. When the Hero had sprinkled it with the juice of some sleep-inducing herbs, the eyes of the monster were closed for the first time. Jason seized that moment and stole the Golden Fleece; and emboldened by his conquest, he took to sea with Medea, a much more glorious victory than that of the Golden Fleece.

## EXPLANATION OF THE FABLE.

We do not intend to elaborate further on the Voyage of the Argonauts. It will suffice, to fulfill our purpose, to say in a few words what may shed some light on the subject at hand, in order to provide a natural explanation for it.

Phrixus <note>original: "Fryxus"</note>, son of Athamas, King of Thebes, and Nephele, having been warned that his stepmother Ino intended to have him killed, stole his father's treasures and took to sea with his sister Helle to find a sanctuary with Aeëtes <note>original: "Eëtes"</note>, King of Colchis. However, Helle drowned during this journey. Phrixus, having arrived in Colchis, dedicated the prow of his ship to the Gods, upon which was seen the image of a Ram. But Aeëtes, a short while later, had him imprisoned in order to become master of his treasures.

Jason, Son of Aeson, the King of Iolcos <note>2</note>, was raised by the Centaur <note>original: "Paerdenmensch," literally "horse-man"</note> Chiron. Upon his return to Thessaly, he found his uncle Pelias on the throne, whether because he had deposed Aeson or because Aeson had willingly vacated it for him. Pelias—whom the Oracle had prophesied would one day be killed by a Prince of the lineage of the Aeolids <note>3</note>, and that he must watch out for a man wearing only one shoe—saw Jason arriving, who had lost one of his shoes while crossing a river. He now feared to see the prophecy fulfilled. To send Jason away, he advised him to go and demand from Aeëtes the treasures of Athamas, brought to Colchis by Phrixus. The bravest youth of Greece accompanied him on this expedition. He was then declared leader of the undertaking, and having had a long ship built, he set out upon it with his companions. After having endured many adventures, they finally arrived in Colchis. Aeëtes, warned by an oracle that a stranger would deprive him of his life, sacrificed everyone who entered his kingdom to the Gods. The Argonauts would, in all likelihood, have suffered the same fate,

“...it is endured; and my house goes all too shamefully to ruin. Show yourselves, therefore, moved by a just indignation; have regard for the neighboring peoples; escape their accusations; and above all fear the wrath of the Gods, lest they, angered by so many evils, bring the deserved punishments upon your heads. I adjure you by the name of the Olympian Jupiter <note>The king of the gods, known for upholding justice.</note>, and that of Themis <note>The goddess of divine law and order who presides over assemblies.</note>, who presides over the assemblies, and either thwarts or brings to success all the counsels of men; my friends, oppose these unjust treatments, so that I may give myself entirely over to the sorrow caused by my father’s death. If the divine Ulysses <note>Odysseus, the king of Ithaca.</note> ever brought any calamities upon you with a hostile mind, avenge yourselves on me; I surrender myself to all your hatred; incite these insolent men even more, and follow their example. It would be much more advantageous to me if it were you who destroyed my goods, cattle, and everything dear to me; then at least I would have hope that my loss would at some time be compensated: for then I would only have to go through the whole city to show the wrong done to me, and demand my goods back until justice was done to me; instead, you now plunge me into irreparable disasters.”

So he speaks, carried away by anger, with tears trickling down his cheeks, and he smites his scepter upon the ground. The people are struck with pity. All the Princes remain silent, without daring to answer him. Antinous <note>The most arrogant of the suitors seeking Penelope’s hand.</note> alone had the boldness to do so.

“Telemachus,” he said, “who shows such pride and boldness in your speech, what are you saying to insult us? Would you then expose us to eternal reproaches? It is not the Suitors of the Queen, your mother, who have caused your misfortunes: it is the Queen herself, who has continually helped herself with clever excuses. It is now three whole years past, and the fourth is coming to an end, that she has disappointed the fiery requests of the Greeks. She soothes us all with a beautiful hope: she makes promises to each of us, and is continually busy acting against her promises. Behold here the latest trickery which she has put into work. She began to spin in her chamber a very fine and large web <gloss>3</gloss>, and said to us all: *Young Princes, who seek me in marriage; since the divine Ulysses is no more, wait, if it pleases you, and do not let me think of marrying until after I have finished spinning this Web I have begun. None of my work shall go to waste. I prepare it for the funeral rites of Laërtes <note>The father of Ulysses.</note>, when the cruel Fate <note>original: "Schikgodin"</note> shall have delivered him to death; so that no Greek woman could reproach me for leaving a man, so worthy and rich in means, stripped of a burial shroud wrought by my hand.* Thus she spoke, and we allowed ourselves to be delayed by these words; but she unraveled <gloss>4</gloss> at night, when the torches were lit, all that she had wrought by day. This deceit remained hidden from us for three whole years; but as the fourth year was now firmly drawing to a close, one of her women, who knew of the secret, revealed the scheme to us: we surprised her ourselves while she was busy destroying that magnificent work, and her...”

<gloss>3. WEB.] The word Phares <note>original: "Faros"</note>, used here by Homer, does not mean a Web <gloss>(a)</gloss>, but a garment—one of those precious fabrics with which Princesses and noblewomen provided themselves, and which they used for the funeral rites of those who were dear and worthy to them. > “...necessitated that she unpick it against her own will. Behold then the answer, which all her suitors give you through my mouth, so that neither you nor any of the Greeks can pretend ignorance. Send Penelope back to her father Icarius <note>Penelope’s father, a Spartan prince.</note>, and compel her to declare herself for him whom she shall choose, and who shall please her best. If she wishes to detain us here even longer, and make us pine away, until she has put to work all the instruction which Minerva <note>The goddess of wisdom and crafts, known as Athena in Greek.</note> has given her—teaching her so many fine works, bestowing so much wisdom and virtue upon her soul, and instilling in her such clever tricks as would never have occurred even to the most famous men—she will choose a path that will not be advantageous to you: for we shall continue to consume your goods for as long as she remains steadfast in her purpose. It is true, she will gain much honor by this behavior; but she will also completely ruin you through it: we shall not depart from here to attend to our own affairs until Penelope has given her hand to the one who shall please her best.”

These threats were not in vain: for Antinoüs and his fellow suitors did not cease to plague the unfortunate Penelope with their troublesome solicitations. But she saw, after twenty years of suffering, her faithfulness rewarded by the return of her Husband, and the wrongs she had endured avenged by the death of her unworthy Suitors.

### E X P L A N A T I O N  O F  T H E  F A B L E.

One finds in Penelope’s work, whether one regards it as a true event or as a fiction, nothing but that which is very natural and requires no explanation. Some, however, finding this artifice too crude to believe that this Princess could have led her Suitors astray for three or four years, suggest that Homer intended to signify by this Web <note>The "Web" refers to the shroud Penelope wove for her father-in-law, Laertes; she promised to choose a suitor once it was finished, but she unwove it every night.</note> the various inventions and pretenses used by Penelope to frustrate their suits.

<page-num>LI.</page-num>

# CASSANDRA.

> *Then also Cassandra opened her lips to the coming fates, she who by the god's command was never believed by the Trojans.* <note>original: "Tunc etiam fatis aperit Cassandra futuris Ora, Dei jussu non unquam credita Teucris." — Virgil, Aeneid, Book 2.</note>
->VIRGIL Aeneid 2.<-

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'K' featuring foliate scrolls and a border.</image-desc>
Cassandra, daughter of Priam, King of Troy, having kindled love in Apollo, was granted by this God—in order to win her to his will—that she might request of him whatever she desired. She then requested the gift of prophecy; and immediately everything that was to come was revealed before her eyes. Yet, far from returning his love, she showed him nothing but aversion. Apollo, enraged that he saw his helpfulness and love so poorly rewarded, resolved to punish her for her unfaithfulness: "Since," he spoke, "it does not lie in my power to rob you of a gift which my blind love has granted you all too lightly, I shall at least ensure that it turns out fatal for you; that no one shall ever place any belief in your prophecies, and that they shall serve no other purpose than to stir up hatred and contempt against you." From that time forward, she incessantly prophesied to her family and fatherland the calamities destined for them by Fate. Although her prophecies were confirmed by the outcome, the Trojans nevertheless held her for a madwoman. When the Greeks finally overran the city by night and filled everything with fire and murder, Cassandra took her refuge in the Temple of Minerva <note>The Roman name for Athena, the goddess of wisdom and war.</note>, where she remained with her arms tightly clasped around the statue of that Goddess. But the violent Ajax, son of Oileus, did not shrink from violating her, even in the presence of the Goddess.

When the Greeks, after the destruction of Troy, had divided the slaves among themselves, the unfortunate Cassandra fell as a portion to Agamemnon, who fell in love with her. When he then carried her away with him, she warned him that he would perish by the hand of his wife Clytemnestra and the treacherous Aegisthus; but this prophecy was no more believed than all the others. Yet when Agamemnon had arrived at Mycenae, he was killed at a banquet by Aegisthus and Clytemnestra. They also, in order to make him die a more cruel death, caused Cassandra to be slain before his eyes while he still lay gasping his last breath.

---

## EXPLANATION OF THE FABLE.

One finds among the Ancients nothing upon which one can build a Historical Explanation of this Fable. The most likely guess is that a Priest of Apollo, having fallen in love with this Princess, taught her the art of being able to predict the future, whether through magic or divination <term>wichelary: the practice of seeking knowledge of the future or the unknown by supernatural means</term>; and that, being unable to move her to return his love, he took revenge on her by bringing her prophecies into contempt.

Ajax received, in his return from the siege of Troy, the punishment which his godlessness deserved. Behold here what Virgil tells us of it, in

LII.

# THE PALLADIUM.

->*Preserve the heavenly Goddess; you will preserve the city:*<-
->*She will transfer the empire of the place with her.* <note>original Latin: "Ætheream servate Deam; servabitis urbem: / Imperium secum transferet illa loci."</note><-
->OVID, *Fasti*, Book 6.<-

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'W' showing a seated figure or mythological scene within a square frame with floral ornamentation.</image-desc>

WHILE Ilus <note>The mythical founder of Ilium (Troy).</note> was busy building the Citadel of Ilium, an Image was seen falling from Heaven, which took its place in a Temple being built for Minerva ¹. It was the likeness of this Goddess, three cubits <note>original: "ellen." An 'el' was a traditional unit of measurement; here likely referring to the classical cubit.</note> high, holding a pike in her hand which she seemed to brandish. The Oracle of Apollo, being consulted on the matter, gave the answer that as long as this gift from Heaven was preserved there, the city would never fall into the enemy's hands; but that as soon as it was carried outside the walls, the city would be destroyed. Ilus and Laomedon <note>The son of Ilus and father of King Priam.</note> guarded it carefully; and to ensure it would not be stolen, they had another made that corresponded in every detail with the first, which was placed behind it so that it could not be distinguished. However, when Troy was besieged by the Greeks during the reign of Priam, Ulysses and Diomedes entered the Citadel by night through subterranean passages—or rather through secret collusion with those inside—and stole the Image. After the city was conquered, Diomedes, obeying a divine prophecy, returned the Palladium to Aeneas, who brought it to Italy and placed it in Lavinium. From there it was taken to Rome, to the Temple of Vesta, where it was regarded as the protective deity of the Empire.

## EXPLANATION OF THE FABLE.

We have described the origin of the Palladium in the manner found among the Poets ². However, other more credible writers tell us that the Pagans themselves revealed the secret of this famous Statue. Arnobius and Clement of Alexandria <note>Early Christian authors who often critiqued pagan myths.</note> assure us that it was made from the bones of Pelops, that ancient King of the Peloponnese. It was a kind of <term>Talisman</term> <note>An object held to possess magical or protective powers, often linked to astrological configurations.</note>, or astral image, to which superstition had attributed the preservation of the City. A modern writer ³ makes this conjecture regarding the name given to it: that the Goddess Pallas herself received her name and origin from this Image, instead of it

<margin>1. MINERVA] or Pallas. These are one and the same Goddess: the only distinction is that Minerva was specifically the Goddess of arts and sciences, while Pallas was the goddess of war. However, the Poets do not always observe this distinction.</margin>

Ilus still possessed the rich wealth of Asia.
It is believed that the heavenly sign of weapon-bearing Minerva
descended upon the ridges of the city of Ilium."

--- Page 153 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Palladium's origins, referencing classical historians to provide a rationalist explanation for the myth of Pallas Athena.</meta>

# THE PALLADIUM.

The image was said to be made in the likeness of that Goddess, who at that time was not yet known. Here is the basis for this conjecture. Dionysius of Halicarnassus <note>Dionysius was a Greek historian living in Rome during the reign of Augustus; he sought to reconcile Greek and Roman history.</note> relates the origin of the **Palladium**, on the credibility of the most ancient writers, in this manner: *Pallas, he says, was one of the Kings of Arcadia, and father of Chrysa; who, being married to Dardanus, brought this Image, called the Gift of Pallas, to her husband as a dowry.* It appears, therefore, that the <term>Palladium|A sacred statue or object, especially the wooden image of Pallas Athena, believed to protect the city that possessed it.</term> received its name from King Pallas, who in all likelihood had fashioned that carved image. But, in the course of time, Mythology, in order to destroy this origin which lacked sufficient renown, made a Goddess out of this image, and gave her the name of the Arcadian Pallas, which the image already bore. 

What we have said is confirmed by another passage from Dionysius of Halicarnassus. He relates that those two images—namely, the Original and the Imitation—*were two young men in a seated posture, each with a spear in his hand.* Since the image of a young man can very easily be mistaken for a female image (especially if it is made somewhat crudely, as works of that time were), the common people made of that image a courageous and warlike Goddess. From this, the Goddess Pallas then took her origin. 

We read in that same writer how this image came from Arcadia to Troy: *When a violent flood, he says, had forced the Arcadians to leave the Peloponnese, Dardanus departed with his wife and his Image to Samothrace. Afterwards, he left for Asia, and founded there a city in his name <note>Dardania, which preceded the building of Troy.</note>, where he placed the Palladium.* When Ilium or Troy began to be inhabited in later times, his children transferred the image to that city. Finally, he relates by what event it was brought to Italy. *Ulysses and Diomedes, he says, had one of these images; but Aeneas kept the other Palladium while the lower city was being plundered, and brought it to Italy.*

***

### R E M A R K S.

4. **Dionysius of Halicarnassus.**] The passages we have cited here from Dionysius of Halicarnassus are taken from the First Book of his *Roman Antiquities* <note>original: "Roomsche Oudheden"</note>. Father Catrou <note>Pierre Catrou (1659–1737), a French Jesuit historian known for his multi-volume history of Rome.</note> has made some changes to them where he cites them, 

<column-break/>

without it being suspected that he did so intentionally: for the words of the original favor his conjecture no less than those he has substituted in their place.

# ANAXARETE.

> *While he tied the rope's knot to the top of the doorframe,* <note>original Latin: "Cum foribus laquei religaret vincula summis..."</note>
> *He said, "Are these the garlands that please you, cruel and impious girl?"*

> *And the unhappy weight hung from his strangled throat.*
> ————— *And gradually that stone,*
> *Which had long been in her hard heart, took possession of her limbs.*

->OVID'S METAMORPHOSES, BOOK 14.<-

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial I, decorated with elaborate floral scrolls and leaf patterns.</image-desc>

Iphis, born of humble parents, fell in love with the beautiful Anaxarete, who was of the noblest descent. Their social standings differed too much for him to hope that she would deign to hear his pleas; for a long time, he resisted this passion in its infancy. But love, stronger than all reason, finally drove him toward his beloved. He immediately approached her servants and spared no effort to win them over to his side; he even managed to persuade them to deliver letters from him to her, in which he made his entire love known to her. Often he hung small flower garlands on her door, which were wet with his tears. Sometimes he lay for entire nights before that same door, reproaching her for thwarting his happiness. 

But the cruel Anaxarete, more unfeeling than the rocks, answered his flame of love with nothing but a scornful contempt, which robbed him of all hope of ever touching her heart. When he could no longer withstand the pain and grief, he went to her door for the last time, where he poured out his complaints to her in this manner: "You triumph then, O cruel one! But you shall soon be freed from a miserable man who bothers you. Rejoice in your victory; enjoy the cruel pleasure of seeing me die. If my conduct has always displeased you, you will nevertheless be forced to admit that I, in killing myself, have nonetheless been able to please you in one thing. Do not believe, however, that before my death I shall fail to testify my love to you: no, I can no sooner lose the light of your eyes than the light of the sun. I will not burden <term>Fame|In Roman mythology, Fama was the personification of rumor and report.</term> with the news of my death, but I shall bring it to you myself, so that you cannot doubt it. You shall see me deprived of life, and your eyes will be able to satisfy themselves with that pleasant spectacle." 

Having said that, he turned his tear-filled eyes toward that door which he had so often draped with flower garlands and festoons, and tied a rope to it. "Cruel and inhuman woman," he cried out, "see here a crown, which—"

-># IPHIS AND ANAXARETE.<-

"...which will please you more." Immediately he placed his head in the noose and hanged himself. The servants, rushing toward the sound he made with his feet against the door, tried to release him; but seeing that he had already drawn his last breath, they brought him to his house. When his parents had mourned him with every sign of the most painful grief, they began making preparations for his funeral procession <term>funeral procession<note>original: "lykstaetsi"</note></term>. Anaxarete, whose soul was already being tormented by an Avenging God <term>Avenging God<note>A personification of divine retribution, such as a Fury or Nemesis</note></term>, hearing the sound of the funeral procession passing by her house, went to look out of the window. But she had hardly cast her eyes upon the bier <term>bier<note>original: "bedde" (bed), referring to the platform used to carry the deceased in a procession</note></term> on which her lover was carried, when she felt herself beginning to stiffen; her blood froze, and a deathly paleness spread over her face. In vain did she try to step back, or at least to turn her gaze away: she had lost all movement, and as the hardness of her heart spread throughout her entire body, she was transformed into a rock.

### EXPLANATION OF THE FABLE.

The case of Iphis <note>original: "Ifis"</note> and Anaxarete is known only from the account of Ovid <term>Ovid<note>The Roman poet Publius Ovidius Naso (43 BC – 17 AD), author of the *Metamorphoses*</note></term>. He had too great a love for the female sex to have missed the opportunity to show them that their cruelty exposed them to the vengeance of the Gods. This is undoubtedly why Ovid invented the Metamorphosis <term>Metamorphosis<note>original: "Gedaentewisſeling," a change of form or shape</note></term> of Anaxarete.

# O R P H E U S

->A N D<-

# E U R Y D I C E.

> He stopped, and on the very verge of light, unmindful, alas! and conquered in spirit, he looked back at his Eurydice: there all his labor was wasted, and the pact of the cruel tyrant was broken. <note>original: "Reſtitit, Eurydicenque ſuam jam luce ſub ipſâ Immemor, heu! victuſque animi reſpexit: ibi omnis Effuſus labor, atque immitis rupta tyranni Fœdera."</note>

->V I R G I L, Georgics 4.<-

<image-desc>Decorative initial letter 'H' showing a seated figure playing a lyre in a landscape with a tree.</image-desc>

It was not long ago that Orpheus and Eurydice were united by the bonds of Hymen <term>Hymen<note>The Greek god of marriage ceremonies and weddings</note></term>, when this Princess, enjoying herself in a meadow with a company of Nymphs, was targeted by Aristaeus <gloss>1</gloss>, who loved her and plotted to kidnap her. She took flight; but a snake, which lay hidden beneath the grass, bit her in the heel, from which she died. Orpheus, after mourning such a beloved companion for some time, soothed the pain of his loss with his lyre-playing. Wandering in the most solitary places, he sang of nothing else from the break of dawn until sunset but his dear Eurydice. When he could not move the Gods of heaven with his laments, he took the bold risk of descending into the subterranean realms of Taenarus <term>Taenarus<note>original: "Tenarus"; an ancient site in Greece believed to be an entrance to the Underworld</note></term>, and even penetrating to the sorrowful dwelling place of Pluto <term>Pluto<note>The god of the Underworld, also known as Hades</note></term>. Approaching these grim regions, he paired his voice with the sound of his lyre. Cerberus <term>Cerberus<note>The three-headed dog guarding the entrance to the Underworld</note></term> stood stunned with wonder, gaping with open maws; Ixion’s wheel stood still; Sisyphus went to sit upon his stone, which he otherwise had to roll forward incessantly; and even the shades <term>shades<note>original: "ſchimmen"; the spirits or ghosts of the deceased</note></term>, in the deepest part of the abyss, were enraptured by his pleasant, rhythmic song. Finally, he arrived at the Palace where Pluto and Proserpina <term>Proserpina<note>The Queen of the Underworld and wife of Pluto</note></term> keep their residence, and having approached them, he made himself heard thus:

> “Dread Deities, it is no bold undertaking which has driven me to your realms:
> “love alone has led me hither. The power of that God is not unknown to you,
> “because he alone made the bonds which unite you. May then that tender love,
> “with which you are ignited toward one another, allow you to feel my grief!
> “I have lost my dear Eurydice: a fatal accident robbed me of her at that time
> “when we had hardly begun to taste our happiness. Give her back to me, O mighty
> “Gods! The toll which one owes to you shall only be paid a little later:
> “everything that lives must, sooner or later, undergo the same law; and you could

### R E M A R K S.

1. ARISTAEUS.] We have spoken extensively about Aristaeus in the Myth of PROTEUS.

<summary>This page introduces the myth of Orpheus and Eurydice, detailing Eurydice's death and Orpheus's journey to the Underworld to plead for her life before Pluto and Proserpina.</summary>
<keywords>Orpheus, Eurydice, Aristaeus, Pluto, Proserpina, Underworld, Hymen, Cerberus, Sisyphus, Ixion, Greek mythology, Virgil</keywords>

--- Page 160 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of legendary figures from Greek mythology, specifically focusing on the parentage and historical skepticism surrounding Orpheus before delving into the accounts provided by the ancient geographer Pausanias.</meta>

-># ORPHEUS AND EURYDICE.<-

...and the Muse <note>The original Dutch uses "Zanggodinne" (Goddess of Song).</note> Calliope was his mother. Some writers imagine that there have been many persons of that name; others, such as Aristotle and Cicero, claim that no Orpheus ever lived. But this latter opinion is refuted by a multitude of testimonies to be found among the Ancients. We shall only cite that of Pausanias ⁴, which will simultaneously provide us with the explanation of this Fable:

> "Among the fictions which the Greeks hawk as truth, one can also place this one: that Orpheus was the son of the Muse Calliope; who by his pleasant song lured the wild beasts to him; that he even descended alive into Hell, and that by having enchanted Pluto and the subterranean deities thereby, he carried his wife away from there. These are so many fictions, in which I seem to see that Orpheus was a great poet, who surpassed all who had come before him; who earned the respect of men because he taught them the ceremonies of religion, and made them believe <note>The original "diets maekte" suggests persuading someone of something, sometimes with a hint of deception or simplification.</note> that he had discovered the mystery of atoning for crimes, of purifying those who had committed them, of healing diseases, and of appeasing the wrath of the Gods. It is said that the Thracian women lay in wait to kill him ⁵, resentful that their husbands had left them to follow him. Fear restrained these women for some time; but being drunk, they carried out the wicked deed: from this, it is said, comes the custom of the Thracians never to go to battle unless heated by wine. According to another tradition, Orpheus died by a stroke of lightning; which was a punishment from the Gods because he had revealed the deepest mysteries to the uninitiated. It is said that he, having lost his wife, went to a certain place in Thesprotia called Aornos, where in ancient times there was an Oracle which gave answers by calling back the souls of the dead. There Orpheus saw his dear Eurydice, and flattering himself that she followed him, he looked back; and not seeing her, he was so overwhelmed by grief that out of despair he ended his own life. The Thracians say that the Nightingales, which have their nests near Orpheus's grave, sing more shrilly and pleasantly than others. However, the inhabitants of Dion, a city of Macedonia near Mount Pieria, claim that Orpheus was murdered by the women in their region, and that he has his burial place there. Indeed, one finds, a few stadia <note>A stadium (plural: stadia) was an ancient Greek unit of distance, roughly 185 meters or 600 feet.</note> from the city toward the mountainside, on the right hand, a pillar with a marble urn <note>original: "busch"</note>, in which the country folk say the ashes of Orpheus are enclosed. The river Helicon, which ran nearby, continued its course for a stretch of seventy-five stadia, where, ending abruptly, it resumes its course twenty-two stadia further... The inhabitants of Dion say that the Helicon formerly kept its channel from where it takes its source to where it empties out; but that the women who killed Orpheus, wishing to wash themselves in that river, it took its course underground so as not to permit its waters for that use ⁶."
