### REMARKS.

4. PAUSANIAS.] <note>original: "Lib. IX"</note> Book IX.
5. LAY IN WAIT TO KILL HIM.] There are writers who say that the Maenads killed Orpheus because he, having sung of the birth of all the Gods, had not spoken of Bacchus; and that this God, to avenge himself upon him, had incited his priestesses to tear him apart. Others relate that Venus, enraged at Calliope, Orpheus's mother, because she had allowed Adonis to stay with her

<column-break/>

for only six months of the year, and had given the lover to Proserpina <note>The Roman name for Persephone, Queen of the Underworld.</note> during the other six months, made the Thracian women so enamored with him that, pulling and tugging at him in competition, they tore him entirely to pieces.
6. TO THAT USE etc.] One can find some other traditions regarding Orpheus in the previously cited passage of Pausanias.

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of mythological figures punished in the underworld, following the previous section's likely introduction to Ixion's crimes. It focuses on a "rationalist" interpretation of the myth, common in Enlightenment-era scholarship, which attempts to explain supernatural elements as historical misunderstandings.</meta>

->I X I O N.<-

### EXPLANATION OF THE FABLE.

After Ixion had unsuccessfully sought housing from several princes, he apparently had the good fortune to find someone who took him into his court and promised to reconcile him. This Prince, noticing that Ixion had fallen in love with the Queen and wanting to test his ingratitude, came to an agreement with his wife that she would permit him a secret visit, but would place a slave girl in her place. This slave was named *Nephele* <note>Original: "Nefelé"</note>, which means "cloud." It is possible that Ixion died from a lightning strike; which gave the Poets the opportunity to say that Jupiter cast him into Hell, and led them to invent that punishment <note>This refers to Ixion being bound to a forever-turning winged wheel of fire.</note> of which we have spoken.

To this Fable they add another: namely, that from the union of Ixion and the Cloud, the Centaurs were born—a kind of monster, half-man, half-horse. Here, in all likelihood, is the origin of this fiction. Pliny <gloss>4</gloss> relates that the Thessalians, who lived around Mount Pelion, were the first <gloss>5</gloss> who practiced fighting on horseback. Diodorus Siculus <gloss>6</gloss> and Virgil <gloss>7</gloss> say the same. In another place <gloss>8</gloss> Pliny also attributes to them the invention of fighting bulls on horseback and throwing them to the ground by their horns. From this comes the fact that the Greeks gave the Thessalians the nickname *Hippocentaurs*, which means *Heroes who drive back the bulls while on horseback* <note>The term comes from the Greek "hippos" (horse), "kenteo" (to goad/poke), and "tauros" (bull).</note>. Because it was during Ixion's reign that the Thessalians devoted themselves to this exercise, the story spread that the Centaurs, or Hippocentaurs, were his children; and his adventure with the slave girl Nephele gave the Poets the opportunity to say that he had fathered them by a Cloud—unless one would rather say with Palaephatus that the heroes on horseback lived for the most part in a village that bore the name of Nephele.

<meta>This page continues the account of Sisyphus’s life and crimes, specifically detailing the prophecy regarding his children and his eventual punishment in the underworld.</meta>

# SISYPHUS.

...should father children ⁴. Such horrific villainies doubled the hatred he had already incurred through the oppression under which he made his subjects groan, and through the devastations he wrought in the Athenian territory. Yet the principal cause of his punishment in Hell <note>The author uses "Helle" to refer to Tartarus, the deep abyss in Greek mythology used as a dungeon of torment.</note> was his indiscretion: that, at the River Asopus ⁵, he revealed the place where Jupiter <note>original: "Jupyn"</note> had hidden his daughter Aegina, whom he had abducted.

The authors do not agree on the death of this Prince. The aforementioned prophecy suggests that Salmoneus plotted his downfall. Others say that Theseus took his life to avenge his raids in the Athenian territory. Even the most ridiculous fables have been recorded about him: among others, that Jupiter, to punish him, had sent Death to him; and that Sisyphus, having chained her, was only defeated when Hercules released her by making him die.

To Sisyphus is attributed the institution of the Isthmian Games. This Prince, as Pausanias ⁶ tells us, having found the body of Melicertus on the shore, which had been brought there by a dolphin, had him buried on the Isthmus, and instituted those games in his honor, which were so famous among the Greeks.

### REMARKS.

<columns>2</columns>

4. SHOULD FATHER CHILDREN BY HIS NIECE.] He had two sons by her. But it is said that Tyro put them to death to prevent the fulfillment of the oracle and to keep her father alive.

5. THAT HE AT THE RIVER ASOPUS REVEALED THE PLACE.] Pausanias (a) relates that behind the Temple of Venus, which was in the Citadel of Corinth, a spring was found, which the Corinthians said Asopus had granted to Sisyphus

<column-break/>

in order to learn from him what had happened to his daughter Aegina, who had been abducted by Jupiter. Sisyphus, he adds, who had knowledge of it, promised to tell Asopus on the condition that he would provide water to the Citadel. This Asopus did, and Sisyphus revealed the secret to him. But he is still punished in Hell for this, if one may believe it.
